For days the sea had behaved like a beast that would not be broken. Night after night it flung itself against our ship, and morning after morning arrived only to show us fresh ruin—rigging snapped like old twine, timbers groaning, and the deck forever slick with salt water and fear. By the seventh day the storm no longer felt like weather at all, but like a will: a dark, thundering voice that spoke over every human plan.

We did not even know where we were. The compass might as well have been a toy. The crew, who had sworn and shouted through the first blasts, had been worn down to hoarse prayers and wild promises—bargains offered into the wind as though God might be persuaded by vows made between one wave and the next. The ship herself was opening like a split fruit. Leaks had multiplied; the water rose with a steady, cold patience that mocked our pumping.

In the cabin at the stern—our small island within the wreck—my wife gathered our four boys close as the whole world shook. Their faces, usually alive with appetite and argument, had gone pale and stiff. The youngest clung to her as if her arms could be a harbor.

“Dear children,” I said, forcing my voice to stay warm and ordinary, as though I were speaking at home beside a hearth instead of above a drowning sea, “if the Lord wills, He can save us even from this peril. And if He does not—then we will yield ourselves into His hand, and think of the joy of being for ever together in that home above. Even death is not too bitter when it does not separate those who love one another.”

My wife’s tears did not vanish, but her back straightened. She lifted her eyes and began to speak to the boys with a calm steadiness I loved her for—words soft as bread laid on a table: simple nourishment against panic. One by one we knelt on the tilting floor, our prayers rising into the roar. Fritz, my eldest, prayed as if his own life were nothing beside ours; his voice shook, but it did not falter.

The storm did not quiet. But inside me something did—the stubborn peace that comes when fear is named aloud and handed, like a heavy pot, into stronger hands.

Then, through the thunder of the surf, a cry cut sharp as a gull’s scream:

“Land! Land!”

In the same instant the ship struck. The shock slammed us to the boards; the sound that followed—wood splitting, iron tearing—was the sound of a thing being unmade. Water burst in from somewhere unseen and immediate, and the vessel began to shudder in a different rhythm, the death-throes of structure.

Above the tumult the captain’s voice bellowed, harsh as a saw: “Lower away the boats! We are lost!”

Lost. The word went into me like a blade. But my children’s eyes found mine, and terror is contagious unless a parent refuses to carry it.

“Take courage, my boys,” I called, making my tone almost cheerful, as though a storm were merely an inconvenience. “We are above water yet. The land is not far off. Let us do our best to reach it. God helps those that help themselves. Stay with your mother while I go on deck and see what is to be done.”

I fought my way upward. A wave flattened me as if I were no more than laundry on a line; another followed; salt stung my eyes and filled my mouth with a bitter, metallic taste. The ship was broken in all directions, and on one side a gaping hole yawned where the sea climbed greedily into the hull.

On deck the last of the crew were not thinking of passengers or promises. Men shoved and scrambled into the boats like rats abandoning a granary. The final sailor cut the ropes, and the boat dropped away into the heaving gray as decisively as a door slammed in my face.

“Wait! For God’s sake—my family!” I shouted, but the wind swallowed my words. Even if they had wanted to return, the sea would have forbidden it. The lifeboats rose and vanished between the mountain backs of waves, and the ship fell lonelier than any wilderness.

I stood gripping a splintered rail, the salt spray cold on my lips, and forced my eyes to look—not at betrayal, but at possibility. And gradually, as the mist tore and re-formed, I understood something: the stern, where our cabin lay, had been jammed between two high rocks and lifted partly above the worst of the breakers. The fore-part was being hammered to pieces, but our end held, wedged like a tool braced in a crack. Beyond the veil of rain there was indeed a coastline—rocky, rugged, but real. My heart leapt toward it with the hungry instinct of the living.

Still, the weight of our abandonment pressed on me as I went back down. In the cabin my wife read my face at once, but I smiled for the boys.

“Courage, dear ones,” I said. “Our good ship will never sail again, but she is so placed that our cabin will remain above water. Tomorrow, if the wind and waves abate, I see no reason why we should not be able to get ashore.”

My sons breathed as though I had opened a window. Children who trust their father will turn a “perhaps” into a certainty with the swiftness of hope. The ship’s violent motion had lessened now that she was fixed, and even that small change felt like mercy.

My wife drew me aside with her eyes. I did not hide the truth from her. I told her of the boats, of the crew’s flight, of how thin our thread of safety was. I watched, afraid that the knowledge would snap her. Instead she took it as she took everything—quietly, firmly, with a faith that did not need theatrics.

“We must find some food,” she said. “And take a good supper. We cannot afford to grow faint. Tomorrow will ask all our strength.”

Domestic words in a disaster have their own power. The stomach is not an enemy of the soul; it is the hearth where courage is baked. So while the night returned—still violent, still punctuated by crashes as the wreck surrendered piece by piece—we searched our stores. The sounds around us were a kind of brutal music: the drum of waves, the shriek of wind, the crack of timber. It was noise without meaning, the opposite of the gentle acoustics of a home where language lives and recipes are handed down in familiar tones.

We ate what we could. The boys, starving with exertion and fright, devoured biscuits as if chewing might keep the sea outside. A little wine warmed the throat and steadied the hands. My wife found goat’s milk later, and even that mild sweetness felt miraculous against so much salt.

In a quieter interval my youngest asked, small voice trembling, “God will help us soon now, won’t He, father?”

Fritz answered too sharply, the strain turning his words hard. “Don’t you know we mustn’t settle what God is to do for us? We must be patient.”

“Very well said,” I told him, “but it would be better said kindly. You too often speak harshly to your brothers, though you may not mean to.”

The boys slept quickly afterward, their bodies surrendering the moment food and stillness allowed it. Fritz stayed awake with us, the awareness of danger keeping him upright.

“Father,” he said at last, low so the others would not hear, “could we make swimming-belts for mother and the boys? You and I can swim. With belts, they might float.”

“It is an excellent idea,” I said, and at once the cabin became a workshop. We rummaged through the wreck’s reluctant offerings and found empty flasks and tin canisters. Two and two we bound them, knotting cord with cold fingers until each pair became a buoyant collar. My wife and the younger boys agreed to wear them without complaint—obedience born of trust and the plain knowledge that fear is less useful than preparation.

I pocketed what I could carry if the ship broke apart in the dark: matches and dry tinder, knives, a length of cord, small tools—portable hope. Then my wife and I kept watch. Each groan of timber made us brace. Each change in the sea’s voice—higher, lower, nearer—made us listen like animals.

At last the long night thinned. Dawn came as a pale washing of light, and with it a change in the world’s sound. The wind was still strong, but its rage had softened; the waves began to speak in longer breaths instead of shrieks. Blue opened above us, and the sunrise spread lovely colors over the eastern rim, as though beauty were not ashamed to appear even here.

I woke the boys. We climbed onto what remained of the deck, and there—suddenly, unmistakably—was silence of a new kind.

No footsteps. No shouted orders. No men.

“Hallo, papa!” Jack cried. “What has become of everybody? Are the sailors gone? Have they taken the boats? Why did they leave us? What can we do by ourselves?”

“My good children,” I said, “we must not despair though we seem deserted. See how those on whose skill and good faith we depended have left us cruelly to our fate. God will never do so. He has not forsaken us. Only let us bestir ourselves, and each cheerily do his best. Who has anything to propose?”

“The sea will soon be calm enough for swimming,” Fritz said at once.

“That would be very fine for you,” Ernest objected, his thoughtful face pinched with worry, “since you can swim. But mother—and the rest of us? Why not build a raft, and all go together?”

“A raft that can carry us safely will not be easy,” I admitted. “But we must contrive something. First, let each of us procure what will be of most use.”

They scattered through the damaged ship like ants in a fallen pantry. I went first to what mattered most: provisions and fresh water. My wife took little Franz with her to see to the animals, who had been neglected in the storm’s chaos. Fritz ran to the arms-chest; Ernest hunted for tools. Jack, bold as always, flung open the captain’s cabin door—and immediately two huge dogs sprang out like cannonballs of muscle and joy. They bowled him over headlong. His squeal of fright turned into indignant sputtering, and then into laughter as the dogs covered his face with apologetic licks.

A moment later I heard a commotion and looked up the hatchway to see Jack riding the larger dog, gripping its ears as if it were a pony. The sight broke a laugh out of me that I had not known still lived in my chest.

“Courage indeed,” I told him, still smiling, “but remember—hunger can make even good animals dangerous. Be cautious.”

When we gathered again in the cabin, each displayed his findings like offerings on a table.

Fritz laid down two guns, a shot-belt, powder-flasks, and plenty of bullets, his eyes bright with the fierce competence of youth.

Ernest produced a cap full of nails, large scissors, an axe, a hammer, and so many pincers, chisels, and augers in his pockets that he clinked when he moved.

Even little Franz dragged in a box nearly too large for him, prying it open proudly to show “nice sharp little hooks.”

His brothers smiled, but I did not.

“Well done, Franz,” I said. “These fish-hooks may contribute more than anything else to save our lives by procuring food. Fritz and Ernest, you have chosen well.”

My wife came in empty-handed, yet her face carried news.

“Will you praise me too?” she asked, a trace of humor steadying her voice. “I have nothing to show—but I can tell you some useful animals are still alive. A donkey, two goats, six sheep, a ram, and a cow and a fine sow, both big with young. I was just in time to feed them. I milked the goats—though I do not know how I shall preserve the milk in this dreadful heat.”

Milk. The word itself was comfort: a domestic sound, the kind a child learns early in his mother’s dialect and never forgets without loss. My mind leapt ahead—cheese, butter, nourishment not merely swallowed but made, transformed by patient hands into stored life.

“All these things are excellent indeed,” I said. Then, nodding toward Jack’s grinning companions, I added, “but Jack here has presented me with two huge hungry useless dogs, who will eat more than any of us.”

“Oh, papa! They will be of use!” Jack protested. “They will help us hunt when we get on shore!”

“If ever we do get on shore,” I said, “for I must confess I do not yet know how it is to be done.”

Jack’s eyes darted. “Couldn’t we each get into a big tub, and float there? I have sailed splendidly like that around the pond at home.”

The idea was so simple it felt like wisdom.

“My child,” I cried, “you have hit upon a capital plan. Ernest—bring your tools. And all of you—find tubs, casks, anything of sound wood!”

We found four large casks bound with iron hoops, bobbing among wreckage in the flooded hold. We fished them out with effort and dragged them to the lower deck, which barely kept above the water. With saw and axe I cut each cask in half. The work was heavy and wet; the ship’s boards sucked at our feet, and every breath tasted of tar, brine, and splintered pine. When our arms trembled with fatigue, my wife handed around goat’s milk—still warm from the animal, with that grassy sweetness that spoke of living fields somewhere beyond this ruin. We washed it down with a little wine and ate more biscuits, the simple meal anchoring us again in our bodies.

Eight tubs now stood in a row. I looked at them with satisfaction; my wife looked at them with pale doubt.

“I shall never,” she said, “muster courage to get into one of these.”

“Do not be too sure,” I answered gently. “When you see my contrivance completed, you may prefer it to this immovable wreck.”

I found a long thin plank and fixed the tubs upon it, bending both ends upward to form a keel. Along the sides I nailed other planks, flexible enough to bring to points at each end. When all was fastened firmly, the whole became a narrow boat divided into eight compartments, crude but promising. I could already imagine it—us seated as if in a strange dining bench, each tub a place setting, each body a needed weight.

But when we tried to move it toward the water we discovered the truth: the craft was so heavy and clumsy that even our combined strength could not shift it.

“I must have a lever!” I shouted over the surf. “Fetch the capstan bar!”

Fritz ran. With rollers cut from a spar and the capstan bar as leverage, we raised the forepart. My sons slid a roller beneath.

“How is it, father,” Ernest asked, panting, “that with that thing you alone can do more than all of us together?”

I explained as best I could, quickly, the principle of the lever—how a small force, applied rightly, becomes a great one. Ernest’s mind caught fire despite exhaustion. He muttered something about moving the world if only one had a place to stand, and promised a long discussion when we were safe on land.

We tied a long rope to the stern and made its other end fast to a beam, so the craft would not outrun us. With more rollers and steady pushing, at last the boat slid forward and launched into the water with such swiftness that the rope snapped taut and saved it from drifting beyond reach. The boys cried out in delight and tried to leap in at once, but the tubs tipped dangerously, the whole contraption leaning like a drunkard.

I threw in heavy objects as ballast until it righted somewhat, but I could see it still needed stability. I nailed long poles across at stem and stern as outriggers, and at the ends of each pole I fixed an empty brandy cask to act as floats. Only then did the craft sit more honestly on the water.

When it seemed steady, I climbed aboard, turned it toward the most open side of the wreck, and cleared away obstructions to give us a passage. The boys brought oars. But the day had slipped away in labor; evening was too near and the sea too uncertain to attempt the crossing.

It was bitter to spend another night on the wreck. Yet necessity is a stern instructor. We sat down to supper with a kind of grim gratitude. My wife, who could turn any handful of stores into something that felt like a meal rather than mere swallowing, portioned out the portable soup-cakes and spoke of how we would cook properly once we had a shore—how we would find herbs, how we might smoke meat if we could hunt, how milk could become cheese if we had salt and patience. Even in catastrophe she thought in techniques and seasons, as if the future could be kneaded into existence.

Before sleep I made everyone put on the float-belts again. I urged my wife—after much persuading—to wear a sailor’s dress for the next day’s work, telling her it would be safer and freer than skirts. She vanished briefly and returned blushing, embarrassed, in a neat midshipman’s suit she had found. It became her, though she tried to hide behind her own modesty. The boys stared, then grinned, and the awkwardness softened into family laughter—small, but precious.

We slept in hammocks. Even then, my ears remained half-awake to the ship’s noises, listening for any change, because a man’s mind cannot rest fully when the world’s acoustics still threaten him.

We rose early. Hope makes sleep light. After prayer, I said, “Now, my beloved ones, with God’s help we will escape. Feed the poor animals we must leave—give them fodder within reach. In a few days we may return and save them. Then collect everything you can think of that may be useful.”

They worked with joy and urgency. From the stores we chose canvas for a tent, a chest of carpenter’s tools, guns and pistols with powder and shot, fishing tackle, an iron pot, portable soup, and biscuits. We had to leave much behind for a later journey; it pained me, like leaving half a pantry in a burning house, but we could not risk overloading.

With a heartfelt prayer we took our places, each in a tub. At that moment the sound of cocks crowing and hens cackling rose from somewhere on the wreck like a reproach.

“Why should not the fowls go with us?” I exclaimed. “If we find no food for them, they can be food for us.”

So we gathered ten hens and two cocks and placed them in one tub, securing them under wire-netting. We set the ducks and geese free; they took to the water at once, as if they had been waiting for permission all their lives. The pigeons burst upward and made for the shore, happy to trade boards and ropes for air.

My wife delayed us a little, and when she returned she carried a bag as large as a pillow. “This is my contribution,” she said, and tossed it to Franz.

I assumed it was something soft for him to sit on, a mother’s instinct for comfort. She only smiled, keeping her secret.

At last we cast off and moved away from the wreck.

My brave wife sat in the first compartment; beside her Franz, nearly six, sweet-tempered and affectionate, packed snugly like a preserved delicacy in his tub until he could scarcely move. Fritz sat next, fourteen, handsome and spirited, hands eager for the oar. The two middle tubs held our cargo. Jack, ten, bold and thoughtless, took his place with the restless energy of a boy who believes the world is an adventure made for him. Ernest, twelve, intelligent and well-read, followed—inclined to idleness but quick of mind when stirred. I stood in the stern, guiding our strange craft with the careful, aching attentiveness of a father steering the entire meaning of his life toward a strip of land.

The tide flowed in our favor, pushing us as if the sea, having tormented us, now chose to assist. The boys rowed. At first we made no progress; the boat turned in circles, stubborn as an untrained animal. Only when I found the right way to steer—adjusting our angle, balancing force and current—did we begin to move steadily.

We had left the dogs, Turk and Juno, on the wreck to avoid their weight. But when they saw us departing, their howls rose raw and desperate, cutting through the sea’s voice with unmistakable grief. Then, without hesitation, they sprang into the water and swam after us. I watched, torn between pity and worry. The distance looked too great for them.

They persevered, sometimes resting their forepaws on the outriggers, panting, eyes fixed on us. Jack, in a moment of childish possessiveness and fear, moved as if to drive them off.

“Stop,” I said sharply. “That would be unkind as well as foolish. Remember—the merciful man regardeth the life of his beast. God has given the dog to man to be a faithful companion and friend.”

So they came with us, two dark heads in the glittering water, their paddling steady as a heartbeat.

The nearer we approached the shore, the less welcoming it seemed. The rocks ahead were barren and jagged, and my mind, always turning practical, tasted in imagination the dryness of such a place—no fruit, no grain, no sweetness, only hunger and hard teeth of stone. Around us floated casks, bales, boxes—fragments of our former plenty. Fritz and I managed to secure two hogsheads and tow them alongside. In a world that might starve us, every sealed container was a potential miracle.

As the light changed and we drew closer, green began to show beyond the cliffs—grass like a promise, and trees lifting their crowns. Fritz pointed at tall palms. Ernest, already dreaming of flavors, said he hoped they were cocoanut trees and spoke of drinking the milk.

“I am sorry I did not bring the captain’s telescope,” I muttered.

“Oh, look, father!” Jack cried, and pulled a little spy-glass from his pocket with triumph.

With it I saw that farther to the left the coast looked gentler. But a current carried us toward the harsh rocks. Scanning again, I noticed an opening—an inlet where a stream flowed out to sea—and I saw our geese and ducks swimming toward it with the confidence of creatures who read water as we read maps. I steered after them, and soon we entered a small bay where the water lay smooth and quiet, as if the storm’s noise had been left outside its mouth. The bottom sloped gently; the banks rose into a small plain where we could land without being smashed against stone.

We sprang out eagerly—everyone but Franz, who lay wedged in his tub so snugly that his mother had to lift him as carefully as if he were a jar of precious preserves.

The dogs scrambled ashore before us and greeted us with barking and frantic joy. The chickens began their anxious chatter; the geese and ducks added their own complaints. Above and beyond them came other cries—screaming and croaking from birds that had owned this place before we arrived: flamingoes and penguins, startled into noisy protest at our invasion. The air was full of life’s clamor, and though it was deafening, I found it oddly reassuring. This was not the empty, murderous soundscape of the storm—this was an ecology speaking, each voice a sign of water, food, and shelter somewhere nearby. It was a kind of wild choir, untrained and yet truthful.

As soon as we had our children gathered safely on dry land, we knelt. Our thanks rose with the stream’s murmur in our ears. We commended ourselves to God’s keeping for whatever days might follow. Then we worked—unloading our treasures with hands that suddenly felt rich. Each chest placed on earth was a victory; each coil of rope, each tool, each biscuit box was not merely an object but a retained word from our former life, a fragment of memory rescued from the sea’s attempt to erase us.

We left the poultry to forage and searched for a place to set our tent. We drove a long spar into a hole in the rock and supported the other end with a pole planted firmly in the ground. Over this frame we stretched sailcloth and pegged it down. We weighted the edges with heavy chests and boxes, and fashioned hooks so the entrance could be closed at night.

The boys collected moss and grass for bedding, their arms full, laughing as if making nests were a game. Near the brook I arranged a fireplace with large flat stones, and soon dry twigs and seaweed crackled into flame. The first smell of smoke on land—sharp, resinous, real—felt like the beginning of settlement.

I filled the iron pot with water. My wife took the portable soup-cakes, and with little Franz as her assistant she established herself as cook at once, as naturally as breathing. She leaned over the pot, breaking the cakes and stirring, watching the water cloud and darken, coaxing flavor out of compressed stores. In the steam rose a scent halfway between broth and the memory of broth—salty, meaty, comforting in its suggestion, and for a moment our new world felt less like exile and more like a household being rebuilt.

Franz peered at the dissolving cakes with suspicion.

“Mamma,” he said earnestly, “is that glue? Is papa going to make something next?”

“This is soup for your dinner, my child,” she replied, amusement warming her voice. “Do you think these cakes look like glue?”

“Yes, indeed I do!” Franz declared, wrinkling his nose. “And I should not much like to taste glue soup! Don’t you want some beef or mutton, Mamma?”

Night came down on us like a lid set carefully over a pot: all the day’s glare and salt had cooked us to weariness, and without the shelter of our tent we should have shivered miserably. As it was, the canvas held what little warmth our bodies could spare. The sand cooled, the sea quieted into a long, breathing hush, and we slept—each of us drained to the marrow by the labor of getting ashore and safe.

The last sound I remember before sleep took me was our vigilant cock, full of self-importance, saluting the rising moon as if he were still in a proper farmyard and not on the edge of a nameless coast. That crow, sharp and clean as a struck bell, woke me at daybreak too. In the pale interval when the world is mostly listening—when the surf speaks softly and even birds hold their tongues—I roused my wife. We stole out while the boys still slept, and took counsel in whispers, our voices small against the wide acoustics of water and cliff.

It was plain to us both what must be done first: learn, if we could, the fate of our late companions; and then, as quickly as prudence allowed, examine the nature and resources of the country that had received us.

So we resolved that after breakfast Fritz and I would set out on an expedition for those two purposes, while my wife remained near our landing-place with the three younger boys.

When the sun had just begun to warm the sand again, I clapped my hands softly and called, “Rouse up, rouse up, my boys. Come and help your mother to get breakfast ready.”

“As to that,” said my wife, smiling with that brave thriftiness which is its own kind of feast, “we can but set on the pot and boil some more soup.”

“Why! You forget Jack’s fine lobster,” I answered. “What has become of it, Jack?”

Jack’s eyes shone with triumph. “It has been safe in this hole in the rock all night, father. You see, I thought as the dogs seem to like good things, they might take a fancy to that as well as to the agouti.”

“A very sensible precaution,” said I. “I believe even my heedless Jack will learn wisdom in time. And it is well the lobster is so large, for we shall want to take part of it with us on our excursion to-day.”

At the word excursion the four of them burst into bright commotion—hands clapping, feet stamping in the sand, a little storm of boyish noise. The sound was pleasant, like the bustle of a kitchen when the fire is up and everyone has a task, yet I checked them with a lifted hand.

“Steady there. You cannot expect all to go. Such an expedition as this would be too dangerous and fatiguing for you younger ones, and this place seems perfectly safe. Fritz and I will go alone this time, with one of the dogs, leaving the other to defend you. Fritz, prepare the guns, and tie up Flora so that she will not follow us.”

At the word guns, Fritz colored to the roots of his hair. The shame of yesterday’s rashness rose in him like a bitter taste. He struggled to straighten his weapon, his hands careful now where once they had been reckless. I let him wrestle with that lesson a moment longer, then allowed him, seeing contrition had done its work, to take another gun.

When he went to lay hold of Flora to tie her, she showed teeth and a low growl rolled in her throat—a sound that is not loud but has weight, like a warning drum. Turk, too, held back, stiff and offended. They remembered the blows. I had to call them with my own voice before they would come close enough to be touched.

Fritz’s eyes filled. He turned to his mother. “May I have some biscuit?” he begged. “I would rather go without the rest of my breakfast to make my peace with the dogs.”

My wife gave it, and Fritz carried the biscuit as if it were an offering. He stroked and spoke to them, every movement a humble apology. Flora, whose heart was quick to soften, relented at once and licked the hand that fed her. Turk, fiercer and more independent, kept his distance, his distrust like a taut rope between them.

“Give him a claw of my lobster,” cried Jack, “for I meant to give it to you anyway, for your journey.”

That rich morsel—salt-sweet, with the sea still living in it—did what biscuit could not. Turk’s nostrils flared; his pride yielded to appetite; he accepted Fritz again with a grudging wag and a slow step forward. It was a small domestic reconciliation, but on a stranded shore such things are as important as any map.

We armed ourselves: each of us a gun and a game-bag; Fritz tucked pistols into his belt with the seriousness of a young soldier trying on a man’s duties; I fastened a small hatchet at mine. Breakfast was simple but sustaining: the familiar thin soup warmed in the pot, then the lobster shared out—firm flesh that demanded teeth and attention, the kind of food that makes you feel your own jaw and therefore your own life. We stowed the remainder of it, along with biscuits and a flask of water, for the road.

“Stop!” I cried, just as we were about to set off.

“Surely not,” said Fritz, startled.

“Yes,” I answered. “We have still left something very important undone. We have not yet joined in morning prayer. We are only too ready, amid the cares and pleasures of this life, to forget the God to whom we owe all things.”

So we commended ourselves to His protecting care, and then I took leave of my wife and children. I charged them not to wander far from the boat and tent. We parted with smiles put on like brave clothing, but underneath was that quiet anxiety one hears in the voice of the wind before a change.

We soon found a small advantage in the harsh shape of the land. The banks of the stream near our camp were rocky on both sides, and we could only reach the water by one narrow passage; there was no corresponding path on the opposite side. This pleased me, for it meant my wife and boys were in a comparatively inaccessible spot, guarded on the other side of the tent by steep, precipitous cliffs.

Fritz and I followed the stream upward until we reached a fall where the water spilled down from a considerable height into a white, cold chatter. Several large rocks lay half covered by the current; stepping from stone to stone, feeling for purchase with our boots, we crossed safely. Now the sea lay on our left, and to the right a long line of rocky heights stretched inland, here and there softened by clumps of trees.

We had pushed barely fifty yards into long rank grass—partly withered by the sun, snarled and tangled like poorly combed hair—when we heard a rustling behind us. The grass waved as if something heavy threaded through it. Fritz turned instantly, lifted his gun, and waited, not firing, not wasting a motion. I felt a surge of pride at his coolness; it told me I could rely on him when true danger came.

But no savage beast burst out. It was our faithful Turk—forgotten in the flurry of departure—come after us, no doubt sent by my wife’s thoughtful care. I praised Fritz for not yielding to alarm and for waiting until he was sure before resolving to shoot.

Still, the incident was a warning written in sound: on such ground, with grass tall enough to hide a body, danger could arrive silently until it was already too close. We hastened toward the open seashore where, at least, the ear has room to work.

The scene that opened there was indeed delightful: hills behind us, the grass moving in broad green swells, trees grouped pleasantly, some stretching almost to the water’s edge. The surf drew its long breath on the smooth sand—steady, cleansing, the sort of sound that steadies the mind.

We searched the shore with care for any trace of our companions. Nothing—not a footprint, not the drag of a heel, not even a broken twig to suggest a passing hand.

“Shall I fire a shot or two?” asked Fritz. “That would bring our companions, if they are within hearing.”

“It would indeed,” I said, “or any savages that may be here. No, no; let us search diligently, but as quietly as possible.”

“But why, father, should we trouble ourselves about them at all?” Fritz burst out. “They left us to shift for ourselves, and I for one don’t care to set eyes on them again.”

“You are wrong, my boy,” I replied. “In the first place, we should not return evil for evil. Then again, they might be of great assistance to us in building a house of some sort. And lastly, you must remember they took nothing with them from the vessel, and may be perishing of hunger.”

“But father,” he insisted, “while we are wandering here and losing our time almost without a hope of benefit to them, why should we not instead return to the vessel and save the animals on board?”

“When a variety of duties present themselves,” I answered, “we should always give the preference to that which can confer the most solid advantage. The saving of the life of a man is a more exalted action than contributing to the comfort of a few quadrupeds, whom we have already supplied with food for a few days. Also, the sea is so calm at present that we need not fear the ship will sink or break up entirely before we can return.”

Thus talking, we went on until we reached a pleasant grove that stretched to the water’s edge. There we rested under a large tree beside a rivulet that murmured and splashed along its pebbly bed into the ocean—fresh water making its bright, laughing argument against the salt’s endless hush.

Above us birds—gaily plumaged—flitted and twittered, a light net of sound. Fritz tilted his head, listening as much as looking, and suddenly started up.

“A monkey!” he cried. “I am nearly sure I saw a monkey.”

He sprang around the tree and, in his haste, stumbled over a small round object. He picked it up and handed it to me, saying it was a round bird’s nest of which he had often heard.

“You may have done so,” I said, laughing, “but you need not conclude that every round hairy thing is a bird’s nest. This, for instance, is not one, but a cocoanut.”

I turned it in my hand and showed him the fibrous covering like coarse rope. “Do you remember reading that a cocoanut is enclosed within a round, fibrous covering over a hard shell, which again is surrounded by a bulky green hull? Here the outer hull has been worn away by time; that is why the twisted fibres show so plainly. Let us break it and you will see the nut within.”

We split it open with effort—stone and knife and stubborn hands—but, to our disgust, the kernel was dry and uneatable, like old bread forgotten at the back of a cupboard.

“Hullo,” said Fritz, “I always thought a cocoanut was full of delicious sweet liquid, like almond milk.”

“So it is when young and fresh,” I told him, “but as it ripens the milk congeals, and in course of time solidifies into a kernel. This kernel then dries as you see. But when the nut falls on favourable soil, the germ within swells until it bursts the shell, takes root, and springs up a new tree.”

“I do not understand,” said Fritz, frowning at the thickness of the shell, “how the little germ gets through such a great thick thing.”

“Nature provides for all things,” I answered, fitting the broken halves together. “Look here—these three round holes near the stalk. It is through them that the germ obtains egress. Now let us find a good nut if we can.”

Because cocoanuts must be over-ripe before they fall naturally, it was not easy to find one still fit to eat. At last we did. The flesh was a little oily, even faintly rancid, but we were in no mood for fastidiousness. It refreshed us enough that we could postpone what we called our dinner until later, sparing our provisions.

We continued through a thicket so densely overgrown with lianas we had to hew our way—my hatchet biting and ringing, vines snapping with wet, green pops. We emerged again onto the shore beyond and found an open view. The forest swept inland; on the space before us stood single trees at intervals, their trunks oddly bumped and studded.

Fritz pointed. “Oh, what absurd-looking trees, father! See what strange bumps there are on the trunks.”

We approached, and I recognized calabash trees—gourds growing right from the stems, as if the tree itself had begun to hang its kitchenware where any passing hand might reach.

“The fruit of these,” I told him, “is a species of gourd. From the hard rind bowls, spoons, and bottles can be made. The savages are said to form these things most ingeniously, and they use them to contain liquids: indeed, they actually cook food in them.”

“Oh, that is impossible,” said Fritz. “I am quite sure this rind would burn through directly if it was set on the fire.”

“I did not say it was set on the fire,” I replied. “When the gourd has been divided and emptied, it is filled with water, and fish or whatever is to be cooked is put in. Red-hot stones are dropped in until the water boils; the food is cooked, and the gourd remains uninjured.”

“That is a clever plan—simple too. I daresay I should have hit on it, if I had tried,” said Fritz, half proud already of an invention not yet his.

“The friends of Columbus thought it very easy to make an egg stand upon its end,” I said, “when he had shown them how. But now, suppose we prepare some of these calabashes, that they may be ready for use when we take them home.”

Fritz seized a gourd at once and tried to split it neatly with his knife. The blade slipped; the cut went jagged.

“What a nuisance!” he cried, and flung it down. “The thing is spoiled; and yet it seemed so simple.”

“Stay,” I said. “You are too impatient. Those pieces are not useless. Try to fashion a spoon or two from them while I provide a dish.”

From my pocket I drew a piece of string. I tied it tightly around a gourd near one end. Then I tapped the string with the back of my knife, working it in as if it were a saw. When it had bitten into the rind, I tightened the string again and again. Then, drawing the ends with all my might, I split the gourd cleanly in two.

“That is clever!” cried Fritz. “What in the world put that plan into your head?”

“It is a plan the savages adopt, as I have learned from reading books of travel,” I answered.

He held the halves up. “It makes a capital soup-tureen, and a soup-plate too. But supposing you had wanted to make a bottle—how would you have set to work?”

“Easier, if possible,” I said. “Cut a round hole at one end, scoop out the interior, then drop in shot or stones. Shake it; the remaining pulp detaches; the gourd is cleaned, and the bottle completed.”

“That would not make a very convenient bottle,” he objected. “It would be more like a barrel.”

“True. If you want a more shapely vessel, you must begin when it is younger. To give it a neck, tie a bandage round the young gourd while it is still on the tree; all will swell but that part which you have checked.”

As I spoke, I filled the gourds with sand and left them to dry, marking the place in my mind so we might find them again on our way back.

“Are the bottle-shaped gourds I have seen in Europe trained similarly?” Fritz asked.

“No,” I said. “Those are another species; what you have seen is their natural shape.”

For three hours or more we pushed on, alert for any trace of men—listening for any sound not made by wind, water, or bird. At length we reached a bold promontory thrusting into the sea. From its rocky summit I knew we could survey the surrounding country. We climbed without much difficulty, but from the top, for all our careful searching, we saw no sign of human beings—no smoke, no movement, no mark of habitation.

Before us lay a wide bay fringed with yellow sand. The arms of it stretched away and dwindled into shadowy promontories. The enclosed water rippled and flashed, holding the sun like molten metal. Inland was no less fair—yet the beauty had a loneliness that crept into our chests. The world seemed to be holding its breath, and that great silence made even our own speech sound strange, as if we were borrowing a language not meant for this place.

“Cheer up, Fritz,” I said at last. “Remember we chose a settler’s life long ago, before we left our dear country. We did not expect to be so entirely alone—but what matters a few people, more or less? With God’s help, let us live here contentedly, thankful we were not cast upon some bare and inhospitable island. But come—the heat is getting unbearable. Let us find shade before we are completely broiled away.”

We descended and made for a clump of palms in the distance. To reach it we had to pass through a dense thicket of reeds. The air there was close and full of dry whispering; each step made a rasp as stalks slid against one another. It was no pleasant task, and I feared at every footfall that we might tread on some venomous snake hidden beneath.

I sent Turk ahead and cut myself a stout reed, thinking it might serve better against a reptile than a gun that requires aim and distance. After a few steps the cut end began to weep a thick juice. I tasted it, and to my delight it was sweet and clean on the tongue.

Sugar-cane.

Wanting Fritz to discover it for himself, I told him to cut a cane for his defence. He did, beating the ground before him. The reed split, juice smeared his hand. He touched it cautiously with the tip of his tongue, then again, then sprang back to me with a shout that rang through the cane-brake like a boy in a fair.

“Oh, father—sugar-cane, sugar-cane! Taste it. Oh, how delicious! Do let us take a lot home to mother,” he cried, sucking eagerly at the cane as he spoke.

“Gently there,” I said. “Take breath. Moderation in all things, remember. Cut some to take home if you like; only do not take more than you can conveniently carry.”

In spite of my warning he cut a dozen of the largest, stripped off their leaves, and hugged them under his arm like treasure.

We forced our way through and reached the palms. At our entrance, a troop of monkeys that had been disporting themselves on the ground sprang up, chattering and grimacing, and before we could clearly distinguish them they were already at the very top of the trees, a storm of small bodies and sharp voices among the fronds.

Fritz, provoked by their impudent faces, lifted his gun.

“Stay,” I cried. “Never take the life of any animal needlessly. A live monkey up in that tree is of more use to us than a dozen dead ones at our feet, as I will show you.”

I gathered a handful of stones and threw them upward. The stones fell far short, but the monkeys, seized by their mania for imitation, immediately began snatching cocoanuts within reach and sending them down in a lively hail. The nuts thudded into sand, rattled against roots, and rolled at our feet.

Fritz laughed in delight and ran forward to gather the best. I pierced holes in the shells, and we drank the milk. It was not pleasant in flavour—thin and strange, neither quite sweet nor quite fresh—but it cooled our throats wonderfully. What pleased us most was the thick, pale cream clinging to the inside of the shell, which we scraped off with our makeshift spoons—rich and satisfying, like a reward for ingenuity.

After this meal we thoroughly despised the lobster we had been carrying and threw it to Turk, who devoured it gratefully. Still hungry, he gnawed the ends of the sugar-canes and begged for cocoanut with his eyes fixed on our hands.

I slung a couple of nuts over my shoulder, fastening them together by their stalks. Fritz resumed his burden of canes. Then we began our march home.

Before long I saw Fritz begin to feel the weight he had boasted so lightly. He shifted the canes from shoulder to shoulder, carried them under his arm, then stopped with a sigh.

“I had no idea a few reeds would be so heavy,” he admitted. “How sincerely I pity the poor negroes who are made to carry heavy loads of them. Yet how glad I shall be when my mother and brothers are tasting them.”

“Never mind, my boy,” I said. “Patience and courage. Do you not remember Aesop’s story of the breadbasket—how heavy it seemed at first, and how light at the end because it had been eaten away? Let us each take a fresh staff, and fasten the bundle crosswise with your gun.”

We did so and went on. Now and then I sucked at the end of my cane as we walked, letting the sweetness temper the heat and the dust.

Fritz noticed. “Oh, come—that’s a capital plan of yours, father. I’ll do that too.”

He set to work with great vigour, but could draw not a drop. He frowned, tried again, and finally asked in a vexed tone, “How is this? How do you get the juice out?”

“Think a little,” I said. “You are quite as capable as I am of finding the way, even if you do not know the real reason of your failure.”

“Oh, of course,” he cried, and his face cleared. “It is like trying to suck marrow from a bone without making a hole at the other end.”

“Quite right,” I said. “You form a vacuum and expect the air to force down the liquid from the other end, which it cannot possibly enter.”

He laughed and corrected his mistake, and soon was as expert as any schoolboy who has turned a lesson into a game, discovering also by practice that he must make a fresh cut at each joint. He chattered on about teaching his brothers, and how Ernest would enjoy the cocoanut milk with which he had filled his flask.

“My dear boy,” I said, “you need not have added that to your load; the chances are it will be vinegar by the time we get home. In this heat it will ferment soon after being drawn.”

“Vinegar!” Fritz cried, horrified. “That would be a horrid bore! I must look directly and see how it is getting on.”

He swung the flask down and tugged out the cork. With a loud pop the contents burst forth, foaming like champagne.

“There now!” I laughed as he tasted it. “You will have to exercise moderation again, friend Fritz. I daresay it is delicious, but it will go to your head if you venture too deep into your flask.”

“My dear father, you cannot think how good it is,” he said, eyes bright. “Do take some. Vinegar indeed! This is like excellent wine.”

The unexpected draught warmed us and made our steps lighter. We went on so merrily that the distance to the place where we had left our gourd dishes seemed shorter than we expected. We found them quite dry, and so light that carrying them was like carrying empty bowls to a table.

Just as we passed through the grove where we had eaten our first cocoanut, Turk suddenly darted away and sprang furiously among a troop of monkeys that were gambolling on the turf. The whole scene erupted in a confusion of squeals and thumps. Taken by surprise, the dog—now truly ravenous—seized one and tore it before we could reach him.

The victim was a mother with a tiny one clinging to her back. That helpless burden had hindered her flight. When she fell, the little creature tumbled off and tried to hide in the grass, trembling, watching with round eyes as Turk worried the body.

Fritz, horrified, rushed forward to rescue them, flinging down everything he carried and losing his hat in his haste. He was too late for the mother. And then the moment turned, as moments will, from tragedy to something absurd: the baby monkey, seeing Fritz, sprang in one bound to his shoulders and, clutching his thick curly hair, planted itself there as if it had chosen its new refuge. Fritz screamed, plunged about, tried to shake it off, tried to pry its fingers loose, but the little creature only clung tighter, making ridiculous grimaces as if it mocked the whole human struggle.

I laughed so heartily at the sight that for a moment I could scarcely help him. Between my mirth and his distress, the grove rang with strange noises—Fritz’s cries, the monkey’s chattering, Turk’s snarling subsiding into satisfied grunts, and above all the steady, patient speech of the sea.

At last, still laughing, I told Fritz the creature, having lost its mother, seemed determined to adopt him. “Perhaps he has discovered in you something of the air of a father of a family.”

“Or rather,” Fritz panted, “the little rogue has found out he has to do with a chicken-heart who shrinks from the idea of ill-treating an animal that has thrown itself on his protection. But father, he is yanking my hair terribly, and I shall be obliged to you to try once more to get him off.”

“I will spare you a description,” my wife began, smoothing a crease in the sun-warmed canvas as if she could iron the whole day flat by will alone, “of our first day’s occupations. Truth to tell, I spent most of it with my thoughts in a knot—watching your progress and straining my eyes for your signals.”

She had risen before the boys, she said, while the night’s salt still clung to everything like a second skin. When she saw my answering sign that all was well, she felt her lungs loosen for the first time since the wreck; yet relief, in that hard bright place, never stayed relief for long.

“For it is perfectly impossible,” she told herself as she sat with the sleeping children and listened to the bay’s steady breathing—water folding on stone, gulls calling, our own little world held inside a ring of cliffs—“to live much longer where we are now. The sun beats upon this bare rock the livelong day; our only shelter is a poor tent, and even beneath its canvas the heat thickens, as though the air were being cooked. Why should I and the little boys do nothing but endure? Why should not we, too, accomplish something useful?”

She had tasted the thought as one tastes a sharp berry—sour at first, then bracing.

“If we could exchange this melancholy, unwholesome spot for a shady dwelling-place,” she went on, “we should all improve in health and spirits. Among those woods you and Fritz described—cool, green, full of living sound—there must be some retreat where we could establish ourselves. There must be, and I will find it.”

By the time the boys woke, her resolve had already become routine—like kneading dough before breakfast because bread must be had. Jack, meanwhile, had found occupation of his own. She noticed him crouched with his knife beside the place where Fritz’s jackal lay, his tongue between his teeth in concentration. He had cut two long narrow strips of skin, scraping and cleaning them with a patience that surprised even her. Then he produced, with the proud stealth of a boy revealing treasure, a handful of large nails from his pocket and drove them through the skin so their points stood outward. After that he cut sailcloth strips twice as broad as the thongs, doubled them, and laid them over the raw side to cover the nail heads.

At this stage Master Jack presented his work with the air of a chef offering a dish that must be finished properly: would she kindly stitch canvas and damp hide together?

She gave him needle and thread and refused to steal the satisfaction from him. Yet his fingers, willing but clumsy, fought the stubbornness of skin that wanted to curl and shrink. When she saw how good-humouredly he persevered—working by feel, grimacing at the smell, blinking against the heat—she took pity, conquered her own disgust, and finished the linings for the dogs’ spike-collars. Afterward she was called upon to complete, in the same manner, a fine belt he had fashioned for himself.

“Think how you’ll keep it from shrinking,” she advised, because she knew—whether it was a hem or a hide—materials always tried to return to their old language, their old shape.

Ernest, who ridiculed Jack’s manufacture with his usual airy superiority, offered a sensible plan nonetheless. Jack nailed the skin stretched flat upon a board and set it in the sun to dry, like fruit laid out on a roof in a Swiss village.

Then she announced her journey.

The scheme was embraced with instant joy. Weapons were checked, provisions portioned with a care that made the biscuits feel precious—food no longer an abundance but a history being rationed. The two elder boys carried guns; she took the water flask and a small hatchet. The dogs came too. Turk, who had accompanied me on my first expedition, understood at once. His ears pricked; his paws chose the path as confidently as if the route were a remembered song, and he led with the pride of one who knows his usefulness.

Looking at her two young sons, each with a gun, and weighing in her mind how much their safety depended on those small hands, she felt gratitude toward me for teaching them early—gratitude sharpened by fear, as salt sharpens broth.

They filled the water-jar, crossed the stream, and climbed to the height from which, as I had told her, the prospect opens. The view struck her like cool water on a fevered brow. Something buoyant—hope, a sensation she had not felt in days—rose in her chest. Even the air sounded different there: less of surf’s unchanging hammer, more of wind combing leaves, birds threading notes through branches.

A pretty little wood in the distance caught her eye. Toward it they set their course. But the tall grass grew in dense luxuriance, higher than the children’s heads, and it clutched at them with a rasping whisper, as if the island itself objected to being crossed. Unable to force their way, they turned toward the open beach on their left, followed it until they were nearer the little wood, then left the strand and made toward it again.

They had not entirely escaped the tall grass. They were pushing through reeds—fatiguing, sweat-slick work—when suddenly a great rushing noise burst up at their feet. A very large powerful bird sprang upward on the wing. Both boys tried to take aim; both were too slow. The bird was already a dark stroke against the bright sky.

“Oh dear, what a pity!” Ernest exclaimed. “If I had only had my light gun, and if the bird had not flown quite so fast, I should have brought him down directly!”

“Oh yes,” she answered, “no doubt you would be a capital sportsman if only your game would always give you time to make ready comfortably.”

“But I had no notion that anything was going to fly up just at our feet,” he protested.

“A good shot must be prepared for surprises,” she said. “Neither wild birds nor wild beasts will send you notice.”

“What sort of bird can it have been?” Jack asked.

“Oh, it certainly must have been an eagle,” little Franz declared, round-eyed. “It was so very big!”

“Just as if every big bird must be an eagle,” Ernest replied with derision.

“Let’s see where he was sitting,” said their mother.

Jack sprang forward—and instantly a second bird, larger than the first, exploded into the air with a startle of wings and a harsh tearing sound, like a sail ripping free. The boys stood staring upward, stupefied. She laughed—an honest laugh that relieved the tightness in her throat.

“Well, you are first-rate sportsmen, to be sure! You will keep my larder famously supplied!”

At that Ernest flushed and looked as though tears threatened; Jack pulled off his cap and made a low bow toward the fleeing bird.

“Adieu for the present, sir! I live in hopes of another meeting!”

On searching the ground they found a rude nest, untidy with dry grass. It was empty. Broken egg-shells lay nearby. They concluded the young had escaped into the grass, and indeed, at a little distance, the reeds waved oddly as the chicks ran through them.

Now Ernest’s eyes, though his gun-hand had been slow, had been sharp.

“Just consider, Franz,” he said presently, warmed by his own certainty, “how this could possibly have been an eagle. Eagles never build on the ground; neither can their young leave the nest and run as soon as they are out of the egg. That is a peculiarity of the gallinaceous tribe alone, to which these must belong. The species is indicated by the white belly and dull red colour of the wing-coverts which I observed; I believe them to be bustards—especially as in the largest I noticed the moustache-like feathers over the beak, peculiar to the Great Bustard.”

“My dear boy,” she said, amused and a little proud, “your eyes were actively employed, I must confess, though your fingers were unready. And after all, it is just as well perhaps that we have not thrown the bustard’s family into mourning.”

So chatting, they approached her “pretty wood”—and found it was hardly a wood at all: only a dozen trees, but each one so enormous it made the mind falter. Their roots rose up and outward, propping the trunks on arching buttresses, like a cathedral held on living stilts. The space beneath was open, shaded, and astonishingly clean—soft green herbage underfoot, no thorns, no briar, no tangled undergrowth to snag cloth or skin. The air there was cooler, thick with leaf-scent and the faint sweetness of sap. Birds sang above—clear, layered calls that seemed to measure the place, as if sound itself were a kind of shelter.

She gave Jack some twine. Scrambling up one of the open-air roots, he managed to measure the trunk: eighteen yards round. She saw no fruit, but the foliage was dense and generous, pouring shade like a blessing onto the ground. They ate their midday meal there with an enjoyment that made even hard biscuit seem softened, especially when followed by water that tasted, by contrast with seawater and sweat, like something almost civilized.

The dogs joined them after a while. They had lingered along the shore, and she was surprised to see them lie down and sleep without begging, as they usually did when we ate.

The longer she sat in that green, hushed palace, the more it charmed her. She imagined a dwelling among those grand branches—an airy nest made human. Up there, above jackals and creeping night-things, she felt she could breathe without bracing herself against fear.

It seemed absurd to her to suppose they would find another place half so lovely; she decided to search no further. Instead, they would return to the beach and see whether anything from the wreck had been cast up—anything they could carry away with them.

Before starting, Jack persuaded her to sit a little longer and finish the belt and the spike-collars. He had, in fact, been carrying the board on which the skins were stretched all the while, so they would take the full benefit of the sun, like strips of meat being dried for a winter that might yet come.

Now the leather was dry. She completed the stitching easily. Jack buckled on his belt with immense pride, slid his pistols into it, and marched about with a self-important swagger while Ernest fitted the collars onto Turk and Juno.

When they reached the shore, it was strewn with objects—casks, chests, heavy pieces of wreckage—valuable but too weighty for them to lift. They rolled a few casks beyond the reach of high water and dragged a chest or two higher. While doing so, they noticed the dogs among the rocks, intent as foragers. They nosed crevices and pools, pounced suddenly, and swallowed what they found with obvious relish.

“They are eating crabs,” Jack said. “No wonder they have not seemed hungry.”

Indeed, the tide-pools teemed with little green crabs. The dogs snapped them up like crisp morsels. Yet it did not seem to satisfy them entirely.

A little later, as they were preparing to turn inland toward the ford, Juno began scraping furiously in the sand, uncovering round pale objects which she devoured with greedy haste.

Ernest inspected the spot and reported, with his calm air of announcing a fact from a book, that she had found turtles’ eggs.

“Oh,” cried my wife, “then let us by all means share in the booty!”

Mrs. Juno did not approve of sharing. It took effort to drive her aside while they gathered two dozen eggs and stowed them carefully into provision bags, as if each smooth shell contained not only food but a future breakfast—warmth, comfort, continuation.

While they worked, they caught sight of a sail approaching beyond the cliffs. Ernest declared it must be our raft. Little Franz, forever haunted by tales of savages, turned pale. For a moment even my wife’s certainty wavered. But they hurried to the stream, crossed by the stepping-stones, and came in sight of the landing-place—where, to their joy, they met us.

She finished her account with the brisk satisfaction of someone presenting not adventure for its own sake, but useful gain.

“Now I hope you approve of the proceedings of your exploring party,” she said, “and that tomorrow you will do me the favour of packing everything and taking us away to live amongst my splendid trees.”

“Aye, little wife,” I answered, half laughing, half honest in my doubt, “so that is your idea of comfort and security? A tree—on which we are to perch and roost like birds? If we had wings or a balloon, it would be a capital plan.”

“Laugh as much as you like,” she returned, “my idea is not so absurd as you pretend. We should be safe up there from jackals’ visits. And I know I have seen, at home in Switzerland, a pretty arbour with a strong floor up among the branches of a lime-tree, reached by a staircase. Why could we not contrive such a place to sleep safely at night?”

“I will consider it seriously,” I said. “Perhaps something may come of it after all. Meantime, as we have finished supper and night is coming on, let us commend ourselves to Almighty protection and retire to rest.”

Under our tent we slept soundly as marmots until break of day. Then my wife and I woke and took counsel again, our voices kept low so as not to disturb the boys—though the morning itself was already loud with the island’s living sounds: surf, wind, birds, the occasional crack of a shell under a dog’s tooth.

Concerning the tree-dwelling she had proposed, I admitted that any removal involved difficulties that must be examined closely.

“In the first place,” I said, “I am unwilling hastily to quit a spot to which, I am convinced, we were providentially led as a landing-place. See how secure it is—guarded by high cliffs, accessible only by the narrow passage to the ford. From here the ship is within reach; its valuable cargo is at our disposal. Suppose we stay patiently here for the present—until at least we have brought ashore everything we possibly can?”

“I agree to a certain extent,” she replied, “but you do not know how dreadfully the heat among these rocks tries me. It is almost intolerable for those who remain here all day while you and Fritz are away—out on the sea, or wandering shaded woods where cool fruits refresh and fair scenes delight you.”

She hesitated, then confessed what sat beneath her impatience like a stone in a pot.

“As for the ship’s contents, much has already been cast ashore. I would rather give up all the remainder than endure the anxiety that gnaws at me whenever you speak of venturing again on the faithless deep.”

“There is much right on your side,” I said. “Suppose we remove to your chosen abode, and make this rocky fastness our magazine—a place of retreat in case of danger. I could render it still more secure by blasting portions of rock with gunpowder. But first a bridge must be constructed to enable us to cross with baggage.”

“Oh, I shall be parched to death before we can leave if a bridge must be made,” she cried, impatience breaking through her careful self-command. “Why not take our things on our backs and wade across as we have done already? The cow and the donkey could carry a great deal.”

“They will have to do so in whatever fashion we move,” I said. “But bags and baskets we must have, to put things in. If you will give your attention to providing those, I will set about the bridge at once. It will be needed not once, but continually—the stream may swell and become impassable, and even as it is, an accident might happen.”

“Well, well,” she yielded, “I submit to your opinion—only pray set about it without delay. I long to be off. And it is an excellent idea to store the gunpowder here when we go. It is frightfully dangerous to keep so much close to our habitation.”

“Gunpowder is indeed the most dangerous and at the same time the most useful thing we have,” I said, “and for both reasons we must be careful of it. In time I will hollow a place in the rock where it can be kept safe from fire and damp.”

Thus, by morning counsel, we settled the great matter: we would move—yet not abandon our landing-place entirely. It would become our storehouse, our refuge, our remembered beginning.

When the children heard of the change, their joy was boundless. They began at once to call it our “journey to the Promised Land,” and regretted only that time must be “wasted,” as they put it, on bridge-building before the journey could truly begin.

Breakfast was hurried, not from lack of appetite but from eagerness. The cow and goats were milked, and we ate biscuit boiled in milk—plain fare, yet the warm sweetness of it steadied us like a hymn in the mouth.

Then I prepared to go toward the wreck, intending to bring planks for the bridge. Ernest and Fritz accompanied me. We soon fell under the influence of the current and were carried swiftly out. Fritz steered.

No sooner had we passed beyond the islet at the mouth of the bay and come in sight of its seaward beach than we were astonished to see a countless multitude of sea-birds—gulls and others—rising in a cloud, disturbed by our approach. Their cries battered the air, a wild screaming so thick it felt like noise you could chew. It was the opposite of the grove’s layered music—a harsh, competitive din, like a market where everyone shouts and no one listens.

Fritz caught up his gun and would have fired, but I forbade it. I was curious what drew so many.

A fresh breeze rose. I set the sail and directed our course toward the island. The swelling canvas and flying pennant delighted Ernest; Fritz narrowed his keen eyes toward the sand where the flocks settled again.

“Aha!” he shouted at last. “Now I see what they are after! They’ve got a huge monster of a fish there—and a proper feast they are making! Let’s have a nearer look, father!”

We could not bring the boat close, but landed at a short distance, secured our raft by looping a rope around a large stone, and drew near.

A monstrous fish lay stranded, and the birds were feeding on it with ravenous fury. It was extraordinary—almost instructive—to watch envy and gluttony flare among them as they fought for bites, shrieking, striking, tearing, as if the island had set out a banquet and the guests had forgotten all order.

“There was nothing on this beach when we passed yesterday,” Fritz said. “It seems strange to see this creature here.”

“Why, Fritz!” Ernest cried. “It must be the shark! Your shark! I believe I can see where you hit him in the head.”

“You are right, I do believe, Ernest,” I said, though I suspected imagination did much of his seeing, given the birds’ destruction. “Look, boys, at those terrific jaws—beneath that projecting snout. See the rows upon rows of teeth, and thank God we were delivered from them. Let us try if we can persuade these greedy birds to spare us a little of the shark’s skin. It is extremely rough; when dry it may be used like a file.”

Ernest drew the ramrod from his gun and charged into the crowd, striking right and left with such hearty zeal that he soon killed several birds and scattered most of the rest. Fritz detached broad strips of skin with his knife, and we returned toward the boat.

To my satisfaction the shore was strewn with boards and planks—just what I needed. I lost no time collecting them. We formed a small raft to tow behind us, and within a short time we were able to head homeward without visiting the wreck at all.

As we sailed, pleased with our good fortune, Fritz—at my direction—nailed part of the shark-skin flat on boards to dry, and stretched the rest over the rounded mast.

“Will that be a good idea, father?” he asked. “It will be bent and crooked when it hardens.”

“That is just what I want,” I said. “We may find it useful both flat and curved. It would make beautiful shagreen to smooth and polish wood.”

“I thought shagreen was made from asses’ hides,” Ernest remarked.

“And you thought rightly,” I answered. “The best is prepared in Turkey, Persia, and Tartary from the skins of horses and asses. The roughness is produced artificially: while the skin is newly flayed and still soft, hard grains of corn are spread on the under surface and pressed into it as it dries. The grains are removed afterward, and the roughness remains indelibly. Shagreen is useful in polishing joiners’ work. It is made in France also from the rough skin of a hideous creature called the angel-fish.”

“Angel-fish!” Fritz exclaimed. “What a name to give to anything hideous!”

“There are bad angels as well as good ones,” Ernest observed dryly. “It is better to leave people to see for themselves which is meant.”

By then we were close in shore. We lowered the sail and moored our craft—raft in tow—safely to the bank.

No one was in sight. Not a sound answered but the water and the birds. We hallooed loudly together. After a moment a shrill reply came, and my wife appeared with Jack and little Franz running from behind the high rocks near the stream, each carrying a small bundle knotted in a handkerchief, while Franz held aloft a landing-net like a flag.

Our return so soon surprised them. They asked anxiously the reason, which we explained. Then their bundles were opened, and a great heap of crawfish tumbled out—fine, glossy creatures snapping their claws and scuttling away in every direction the instant they touched ground. The boys shrieked with laughter as they chased them, only to find others escaping in a dozen new ways.

“Now, father, have we not done well today!” Jack cried, holding up one as though presenting a prize lobster from a grand kitchen. “Did you ever see such a splendid crawfish? Oh, there were thousands—two hundred at least. Just look at their claws!”

“No doubt you were the discoverer,” I said.

“No! Fancy young Franz being the lucky man,” Jack answered. “He and I went toward the stream while mother was busy, to look for a good place for the bridge. Franz was picking up pebbles and alabasters—some because they’re pretty, some to strike sparks with in the dark, and some he insisted were ‘gold.’ Then he cried, ‘Jack! Jack! Come and see the crabs on Fritz’s jackal!’ You know we threw it away there—and to be sure it was swarming with these creatures.”

He looked up, eager for approval, and asked the question that mattered most in our new life: would it feed us?

“Are you glad we found them, father? Will they be good to eat?”

“Very excellent,” I told him, “and we may be thankful that food for our wants is thus provided day by day.”

When each party had told its adventures, my wife set about cooking the crawfish. Even in our rough circumstances she treated the task with care—rinsing them in fresh water, letting them purge, then boiling them until their shells blazed red and the air filled with a rich briny sweetness that made the camp feel, for a moment, like a home with a pot on the hearth. The scent cut through the tent’s stale heat, and even the gulls’ distant racket seemed less insulting with such honest nourishment at hand.

While she worked, we went to bring our planks ashore. Even that simple labour required contrivance. I improvised rope-harness for the cow and donkey, so they could drag each board separately from the water’s edge to the margin of the stream, the ropes creaking, hooves grinding pebbles, the animals’ breath steady and patient.

Jack showed me where he thought the bridge should be. The banks were there tolerably close, steep, and of equal height.

“How shall we find out if our planks are long enough to reach across?” I asked. “A surveyor’s table would be useful now.”

“What do you say to a ball of string, father?” Ernest suggested. “Tie one end to a stone, throw it across, then draw it back and measure the line.”

We adopted his idea. The distance proved eighteen feet. Allowing three feet more on each side, I calculated we needed boards of twenty-four feet.

How to lay the planks across—that was the difficulty. We decided to discuss it over dinner, which now called us. My wife brought her needlework to show me: with hard labour she had made two large canvas bags for the ass to carry. Having no suitable needle, she had bored each stitch-hole with a nail. The work was rough, but strong—like everything we were learning to make.

Dinner was quickly dispatched; no one lingered when there was building to be done and daylight to spend. A scheme had occurred to me for conveying one end of a plank across the water, and I rose at once to set about it.

The next morning found us awake with the first clean rinsing of daylight. The tree that had become our home held the night’s coolness in its bark, and the leaves overhead still trembled with the small, watery sounds of dawn—dew loosening, a distant surf breathing in, and the bright piping of birds rehearsing the day.

The children were in motion at once, springing and clambering among the branches with the heedless energy of young creatures newly certain of life.

“What shall we begin to do, father?” they cried from above. “What do you want us to do today?”

“Rest, my boys,” I answered. “Rest.”

“Rest?” they echoed, as if the word itself were a dull stone. “Why should we rest?”

“‘Six days shalt thou labour and do all that thou hast to do, but on the seventh, thou shalt do no manner of work.’ This is the seventh day. On it, therefore, let us rest.”

Jack’s face lit as though Sunday were a toy. “Sunday? How jolly! Then I won’t work at all. I’ll take a bow and arrow and shoot, and we’ll climb and have fun all day.”

“That is not resting,” I told him gently. “That is not the way you are accustomed to spend the Lord’s day.”

“No—but there’s no church here,” he protested, “and nothing else to do.”

“We can worship here as well as at home,” I said.

“But there is no church, no clergyman, and no organ,” Franz put in.

I looked up into the green roof of our great tree, where light lay in wavering panes and the air was sweet with sap and leaf-milk. “The leafy shade of this tree is more beautiful than any church,” I said. “There we will worship our Creator. Come, boys—down with you. Turn our dining hall into a breakfast-room.”

One by one they slid down the ladder into our hollowed dwelling. To my dear Elizabeth I said, “This morning we will devote to the service of the Lord. I will speak to them by a parable. But without books, without the usual Sunday occupations, we cannot keep them quiet the whole day; afterward, therefore, let them pursue any innocent recreation. In the cool of evening we will walk.”

She agreed at once; and after breakfast—plain fare, yet eaten with the gratitude of people who have known hunger breathing near them—we gathered on the soft grass in the shade. The island had its own Sabbath music: not the measured peal of bells, but the layered calls of birds, the creek’s light chatter, the sea’s steady, solemn hush. I felt, as I often had since our wreck, how a place’s sounds settle into the heart like salt into food—too harsh, and the spirit tightens; balanced, and the mind grows spacious.

We sang hymns as best we could, voices a little rough from open air and new labours, yet warmed by one another. We prayed—heartfelt, plain words rising under leaves as naturally as breath. Then I told the children I would speak to them in a parable instead of a sermon.

“Oh, that would be delightful!” Franz cried. “I like the parables in the Bible better than anything. When can we hear you read out of the Bible again, father?”

His question struck me with quiet shame. In our eager hunt for what would feed our bodies—tools and powder, rope and flour—I had neglected the Bread of Life.

“Ah, my little boy,” I said, “your words reproach me. I blush to think I have neglected the word of God. On my next return to the wreck I will search for a Bible. Though our own books were nearly all destroyed, I am sure to find one.”

At that, my wife rose without a word and went to her bag—the boys called it her magic bag, for from it she drew what we most needed as if by miracle. She returned with a copy of the Holy Scriptures and placed it in my hands. I received it with such gratitude as I cannot fit into language; for it seemed to me that in an island where even familiar speech began to feel like a dwindling coin, the old words of home still held their full weight.

I read aloud, and then I spoke:

“A Great King, ruling in power and splendour over a vast realm of light and love, possessed within its boundaries a desolate and unfruitful island. This spot he made the object of his special care; and lavishing on it all the varied resources of his might and goodness, it bloomed in beauty, and became the happy residence of a band of colonists, who were charged not only with the cultivation and improvement of the soil, but each, individually, was bound to cherish in his soul the spirit of love and true allegiance to his Sovereign.

“While this faithful union was maintained, the colony flourished; and the noblest virtues exalted and rendered happy the existence of every member of the race.

“That a discontented and rebellious spirit should ever have infected these fortunate subjects of so loving a master seems incredible, yet it was so; disobedience and pride brought misery and punishment, the fair prospects of the colony were blighted, the labours of the colonists were unblessed, and total separation from the parent kingdom seemed inevitable.

“A message of pardon—of free forgiveness—was nevertheless accorded to these rebels; and to all who, humbly accepting it, moulded their future lives to the will of the Great King (now revealed in a character even more gracious than before), was held out the promise of removal at last from among the ruins caused by the great rebellion, to the glory and undimmed splendour of the realm of Light and Blessedness.”

The boys listened with faces that held, for once, more inward light than outward mischief. Leaving allegory, I pressed simply to their young hearts the truths I wished them to carry. A short prayer ended our little service.

Then, after a thoughtful pause, we separated, each to what suited him. Even our idleness had tasks hidden in it, as flour hides its strength until water draws it into dough.

I took reeds for arrows and tried to fit porcupine quills as points. The work had a pleasing fineness: the rasp of thread over reed, the tiny resistance as a spine bit into place. Jack helped, quick-fingered and eager, binding a sharp spine at one end of each reed with pack-thread. He began to wish aloud for glue to keep everything firm.

“Oh, Jack!” cried Franz, eyes bright with invention. “Mamma’s soup is as sticky as anything. Shall I run and ask for a cake of it?”

“No, no, little goose!” Jack laughed. “Better look for real glue in the tool-box.”

“He will find glue there, to be sure,” I said. “And the soup would scarcely have answered your purpose. But Jack, my boy, I do not like to hear you ridicule your little brother’s idea. Valuable discoveries have come from thoughts that first appeared no wiser than his.”

While we talked and worked, a shot cracked above us—so sudden it startled the leaves into a rain of little tremors. Two small birds dropped dead at our feet. Looking up, we saw Ernest among the branches, his face shining with triumph.

“Well hit! Well hit!” he cried. “A good shot, wasn’t it?”

He slipped down, picked up the birds, and brought them to me. One was a kind of thrush; the other an ortolan—a small dove, famed even in Europe as a delicacy for the richness of its flavour.
