I held it in my hand, feeling the warmth ebbing from the soft body, and already my mind—like my wife’s—moved toward the questions of keeping and cooking. The figs these birds came to feed on were only just beginning to ripen; if the flocks arrived as I expected, we could take many and preserve them for the rainy season by half-cooking them and laying them in casks, sealed beneath melted lard or butter, so that the fat cooled into a smooth, pale lid of safety against air and decay. Even on an island, the old arts of the larder—salt, smoke, fat—were a kind of language: each method saying, in its own untranslatable way, This will last.

By then Jack had made a good supply of arrows and was practising in earnest. I finished a small bow and arrows for Franz. I hoped to be left in peace; but the young hunter immediately demanded a quiver. So I invented that too, to complete his equipment: I stripped bark from a small tree, shaped it into a tube, fitted a flat side and a bottom, and added a string so it could hang at his shoulder. When he had filled it with arrows and gone off, his mother laughed that he looked like an innocent little Cupid bent on conquest.

Not long after, we were called to dinner, and none needed to be asked twice. Hunger makes even a rough meal musical: the clink of cups, the soft tear of bread, the satisfied silence between mouthfuls. As we ate, I proposed that we give names to the places we had visited.

“It will become more and more troublesome to explain what we mean unless we do so,” I said. “Besides, we shall feel more at home if we can talk as people do in inhabited countries, instead of saying ‘the little island at the mouth of our bay where we found the dead shark,’ or ‘the large stream near our tent across which we made the bridge.’ Let us begin by naming the bay in which we landed. What shall we call it?”

“Oyster Bay,” said Fritz at once.

“No, no—Lobster Bay,” cried Jack, rubbing his leg as if the memory still pinched, “in memory of the old fellow who took a fancy to it!”

My wife, whose gratitude was always practical, said, “In token of our escape we should call it Safety Bay.”

Safety Bay pleased us all, and so it was fixed. Our first abode became Tentholm; the islet in the bay, Shark’s Island; the reedy swamp, Flamingo Marsh. A name for our leafy castle took longer. At last we settled on Falconhurst. Then we quickly named the remaining landmarks: Prospect Hill for the first eminence we had climbed; Cape Disappointment for the rocky height where we had strained our eyes in vain for the ship’s company; and Jackal River for the great stream at our landing place.

In the afternoon the boys returned to their occupations. Fritz finished the cases he was contriving from the tiger-cat skin. Jack begged my help with a plan that delighted him: to make a cuirass for Turk from the porcupine skin. We cleansed the inside thoroughly—scraping, washing, and airing it until the smell of animal was diminished, though never entirely gone—then cut and fitted it round the patient dog’s body. When we had sewn on the strings and the hide had dried enough to hold its shape, Turk was armed in a coat of mail both defensive and offensive, the quills standing like an army of small spears.

He became, at once, a creature out of a fable. Juno wanted none of him; she slipped away from his friendly approaches as if the very air around him pricked. It was plain Turk could put to flight the fiercest animal, so equipped. I determined to make a helmet for Jack from the remainder of the skin, and did so quickly, to his boundless delight.

Evening came down softly. We took a walk among the sweet glades near our abode, where the air cooled and the insects began their minute, ceaseless fiddling. We closed the Sabbath with prayer and a glad hymn, and went to rest with peaceful hearts, the tree cradling us in its living timbers.

The next morning I proposed an expedition to Tentholm, for I wished to reach it by a different route. We left Falconhurst well armed: I and my three elder sons carried guns and game-bags; little Franz was equipped with bow, quiver, and arrows. Fritz, with his belt of margay skin, and Jack with his extraordinary headdress looked like young savages out of some traveller’s tale.

My wife walked beside me, the only one unarmed; she carried a jar to fetch butter from Tentholm. The dogs went before us—Turk in his porcupine cuirass, Juno keeping a respectful distance from so formidable a companion.

Master Knips had fully intended to ride his charger as usual. But when he saw Turk seemingly arrayed in a new skin, he approached with caution. Touching him with one paw, he discovered instantly that such a hide would make anything but an agreeable seat. The grimace he made was so comical we could not help laughing. Chattering in protest, he bounded toward Juno, hopped upon her back, seated himself, and soon looked perfectly satisfied with his change of steed.

The flamingo, much petted in the last day or two, decided he was entitled to accompany us. For some time he walked beside the children, following first one and then another as they darted into the wood. Their zig-zagging at last disgusted him; abandoning them, he settled into a dignified pace at my side, his long legs making a quiet, regular tapping on the ground, as if he approved of steadier company.

We followed the stream. The boys ranged ahead, full of exploration. Then I heard a joyful shout, and Ernest came running toward me with his brothers behind him, all breath and excitement. In Ernest’s hand was a plant.

“Potatoes! Potatoes, father!” he panted, eyes sparkling.

“Yes,” Jack cried, “acres and acres of potatoes!”

There was no mistaking leaf and flower, nor the light clear-green bulbous roots. “My dear Ernest,” I said, “you have indeed made a discovery. With the potato we shall never starve.”

“But come and look!” Jack urged. “Come and feast your eyes on thousands of potatoes.”

We hurried to the spot and found a great tract of ground covered with the precious plant.

“It would have been rather difficult,” Jack observed, “not to have discovered such a great field.”

“Very likely,” Ernest replied with a smile, “but I doubt if you would have discovered that it was a potato field.”

“Perhaps not,” Jack conceded. “You’re welcome to the honour. I’ll take the honour of being first to get a supply.” With hands and knife he dug up plants and filled his game-bag with roots. The monkey followed, scratching with nimble paws until he had a heap beside him.

So delighted were we, and so eager to secure a store, that we did not stop until every bag, pouch, and pocket was stuffed with potatoes. Some wanted to return at once to Falconhurst to cook and taste them. But I overruled this, and we continued our march, heavily laden and happy, the weight at our hips and shoulders as pleasing as coin.

“How,” I said, “can we thank the Giver of all these blessings sufficiently?”

“Oh,” said Franz readily, “we can say, ‘We thank thee, O Lord, for all thy goodness and mercy; and bless us for Jesus Christ’s sake. Amen.’”

“That would not be sufficient,” Fritz said. “Do you think it would be enough just to say to father and mother, ‘Thank you for all you do,’ and not show that we were really thankful by loving them and doing what we can to please them?”

“You are quite right, Fritz,” I answered. “Franz did not say all that was necessary. He should have added, ‘Give me grace to do Thy will, and to obey Thee in all things.’”

As we talked, we reached the head of our streamlet, where it spilled from the rocks in a bright cascade, sparkling and splashing with a voice like laughter. We crossed and pushed into tall grass on the other side. The reeds were thick and tangled; we forced our way through with difficulty, their dry blades hissing against our clothes.

Beyond them the landscape opened in a richness that seemed almost too lavish for our need. Tall palms rose like pillars; luxuriant ferns unrolled beside them. Brilliant flowers and graceful creepers poured colour over everything; prickly cactus thrust up amid softer leaves; aloe stood like a patient sentinel; jasmine and sweet-scented vanilla loaded the evening air with perfume. The Indian pea was there, and—most enticing to boys—a profusion of pineapples. Their scent carried a sharp sweetness, like warm honey cut with green acidity.

The boys fell upon the fruit with such eager mouths and sticky fingers that my wife had to caution them, “Remember, there are no doctors here. If you make yourselves ill, you must cure yourselves as best you may.”

Her warning did little. They showed Knips what they wanted, and sent him off to fetch the ripest fruit. He climbed with the smugness of a practised thief, returning with prizes as if he had always lived in this bright, noisy pantry of the wild.

While they were thus employed, I examined the shrubs. Among them I noticed a plant I knew from description: long pointed leaves and a beautiful red flower.

“Come here, boys,” I called. “Here is something far more valuable than your pineapples. Do you see that plant? It is the karatas. The filaments of the leaves make capital thread. The leaves themselves, bruised, form an invaluable salve. The pith may be used either for tinder or for bait for fish.

“Suppose, Ernest, you had been wrecked here alone—how would you have made a fire without matches, or flint and steel?”

“As the savages do,” he replied. “I would rub two pieces of wood together until they kindled.”

“Try it,” I said, “but do it when you have a whole day before you and no other work to be done; I am certain it would be night before you succeeded. But see here.”

I broke a dry karatas twig, peeled off the bark, and laid the pith on a stone. Striking two pebbles over it, I coaxed out a spark; the pith caught at once, and a small flame lifted like a living thing born from patience. The boys’ faces brightened as if the fire itself had spoken a new word into their minds.

Then I drew threads from the leaves—fine, strong fibres—and gave them to my wife. She took them with the look she wore whenever she saw a new means of keeping house and history alive: thread meant net, snare, stitching, binding—measured, repeatable acts that outlast mere luck.

“But what,” Fritz asked, “is the use of all these other prickly plants except to annoy one? Here, for instance, is a disagreeable little tree.”

“That is an Indian fig,” I told him. “It grows best on dry rocky ground, for much of its nourishment is derived from the air. Its juice is used medicinally, I believe, and its fruit is pleasant and wholesome.”

At the word fruit Jack was off. He tried to gather it, but the sharp thorns defied him, and he returned with bleeding hands and a face pulled long with disappointment.

I removed the thorns and made a sharp wooden skewer. Thrusting it into a fig, I twisted the fruit from its branch and split it open with my knife while still holding it on the skewer. The rest followed my example, and we ate. The fruit was excellent—cool, mildly sweet, with a faintly earthy undernote that spoke of sun on stone.

Ernest examined his fig closely. “What are these little red insects?” he exclaimed. “They cling all over it. Can they be cochineal?”

He handed it to me. I looked carefully. “You are right, my boy. It is cochineal—worth its weight in gold to traders, though of little use to us unless any of you care to appear in gay colours. It makes the loveliest scarlet dye.”

“No, thank you,” Jack said. “But we’ll take a lot when we go home again. Now let us find something more useful.” And from then on they pelted me with questions about every shrub and tree.

“Stop, stop,” I said at last, laughing. “The most learned naturalist would be puzzled by many of these, and I, who have never seen them before and know them only by description, cannot pretend to name or explain a quarter of them.”

Talking still of those plants I did know, we reached Tentholm. Everything was safe. We set to work for what we wanted. I opened the butter cask, and my wife filled her pot. Fritz looked after ammunition. Jack and Ernest ran to the beach to capture the geese and ducks we had left there.

This proved no easy task. Left alone so long, the birds had grown shy; they would not come on shore to be caught. Ernest at last devised an ingenious plan. He tied pieces of cheese to long strings and threw the bait into the water. The hungry ducks snapped at it at once; with careful, patient drawing he lured them shoreward. There Jack and he seized and tied the rebels together by the feet, the birds protesting with furious quacks.

Meanwhile we procured a fresh supply of salt. We packed it on Turk’s back, first relieving him of his porcupine armour. The birds were fastened to our game-bags. We closed the tent-door carefully and started home by the sea-shore.

The walk was cheerful; the sea laid down its endless sound beside us like a road of breath, and the sand, strewn with shells and weed, glittered in places as if it had swallowed bits of sky. At Falconhurst I released the birds, clipped their wings so they could not leave us, and established them on the stream.

Then came supper—one of those meals that become a landmark in memory. Potatoes, newly unearthed, were washed and cooked until their skins split and their insides turned to steam and softness. With milk and butter—fresh, rich, and fragrant—it was delicious beyond any feast of our former life, for it tasted of security as much as of food. We ate until our bodies felt settled and warm, and then climbed to our hammocks and slept.

Having noticed much driftwood on the sands the previous evening, it occurred to me that we should collect it and make a kind of sledge, to lessen the labour of fetching supplies from Tentholm. I woke early and roused Ernest, wishing to encourage him to overcome his natural indolence.

He stretched and yawned, then rose more cheerfully than I expected, pleased with the idea of an expedition while the others slept. We took the donkey with us; I brought a large broad bough I meant to use for hauling our load.

As we walked I said, “I suppose you are rather sorry for yourself, Ernest, and grudged leaving your cosy hammock and pleasant dreams at this hour.”

“Oh, father, do not laugh at my laziness,” he replied. “Indeed I mean to cure myself of it. I am glad to go with you. I intended to shoot more ortolans this morning, but there will be time afterwards. The boys will be shooting at them, I daresay, but I don’t expect they will have great luck.”

“Why not?” I asked.

“I don’t believe they will know what shot to use at first,” he said, “and they will most likely shoot upwards and miss, because of the height and the thickness of the branches and foliage.”

“You certainly possess prudence and reflection, as well as observation,” I told him. “These are valuable. But sudden action is so often necessary in life that you must cultivate the power of instantly perceiving and deciding what must be done in emergencies. Presence of mind is precious; though natural to some, it may be acquired by all who train themselves.”

On the shore our work went quickly. We selected the wood I thought suitable and laid it across the broad leafy branch. With our help the donkey dragged a fair load homeward, and I added to it a small chest that lay half-buried in sand.

As we drew near Falconhurst we heard the boys’ guns popping away at the birds. They ran to meet us, eager to know where we had been. Their eyes fixed on the chest, and I allowed them to open it while I asked my wife to excuse our absence without leave. She reprimanded me gently, and I explained my plan for the sledge, which pleased her greatly; she already pictured it loaded with her butter hogshead and gliding from Tentholm to Falconhurst like a patient beast.

The chest proved to be a common sailor’s, containing only clothes, sodden and salted by sea water.

At breakfast the boys displayed several dozen birds and recounted their failures and successes. Ernest had guessed their mistakes, but practice was improving them, and they were ready to continue their sport. Their mother, however, said she could not use more birds than those already killed and asked if I did not think a snare might be contrived; we would waste much powder and shot if they continued so.

I agreed. The lads laid aside their guns. The younger ones set about making snares from the long threads of karatas leaves in a simple method I showed them, while Fritz and Ernest helped me with the sledge.

We were busy when a tremendous commotion among our fowls made us suppose a fox or wild cat had broken in. The cocks crowed defiantly; the hens fluttered and cackled in wild excitement.

We hurried toward them, but Ernest noticed Master Knips slipping away as if guilty. He followed and caught the monkey in the act of eating a newly laid egg, which he had carried off and hidden among grass and roots. Ernest found several more.

They were very welcome to my wife, for as yet the hens had given us no eggs. From that day she determined to imprison the monkey each morning until the eggs were collected.

Soon after, as Jack was setting the newly made snares among the branches, he discovered a pair of our own pigeons building in the tree. It was much to be desired that we should increase our stock of these pretty birds. I cautioned the boys against shooting near our tree while nests were there, and warned them also about their snares, which were meant only for the wild fig-eaters, not for the birds that had already begun to make Falconhurst their home.

Next morning, while the first stir of breakfast went on in the leafy house—fire crackling low, a pot whispering at the edge of boil, the hens answering one another in brisk, domestic argument—I set myself to the kangaroo’s hide. I wanted it whole, not hacked and patchy, for a skin well taken is a tool in itself: shelter, vessel, clothing, cord. I scraped and cleaned it as carefully as if I were trimming sinew from a haunch for the stew, working by feel and by smell, until the sharp wild tang of the animal gave way to the clean, raw scent of rinsed leather.

Fritz, meanwhile, made all ready for our journey to the wreck. When the time came I called for Ernest and Jack, meaning to leave them strict directions—what to do, what not to do, and above all the old rule that keeps families alive in strange countries: no wandering off without a word.

But directly after breakfast they had vanished.

Their mother, looking out through the green doorway of boughs, could only guess, with that mixture of tenderness and exasperation known to all who keep a household, that the boys—remembering our need—had gone after potatoes. I asked her to reprove them when they returned. Yet hearing they had taken Turk, I forced my uneasiness down. The forest had its own jaws and tempers, but the dog was wise and watchful.

Still, it went against my heart to leave my wife alone with little Franz. I kissed her, promised we would come back laden with “treasures” from the ship, and tried to make my voice sound like certainty instead of hope.

We went on steadily, crossed the bridge at Jackal River, and then—at the very moment I had begun to count the day’s work ahead of us—two figures burst from cover with a whoop.

Jack and Ernest sprang out like sprites of mischief from a thicket where they had been lying in ambush, their faces lit with triumph at the shock they had given their “unsuspecting” father and brother. Their laughter rang against the banks; even the river seemed to chuckle over stones.

It was plain they believed their trick had bought them a place in our boat.

I stopped that notion at once. I could not bring myself to scold them hard—their delight was too honest, their prank too boyish—and yet I would not have our plans ruled by childish cunning. Besides, their disobedience offered me, quite accidentally, a service I had needed.

They must go home. Their mother must not wait with a tightening heart all day, watching the water and listening for oars that did not come. But since they had already come so far, I sent them to collect salt—useful, precious, the quiet guardian of meat and fish.

And I gave them a message I had not had courage to speak aloud at parting: the work aboard the wreck would take longer than I had admitted. If we were to build the raft I had in mind—and build it well, without forcing a second, wasteful journey—we should sleep on the ship and return the next day.

I had meant to say this, but I knew how my wife would object, and my resolve had been to return that very evening. Now necessity tightened the plan into firmness. The boys’ appearance, like a foolish miracle, gave me a messenger.

“Goodbye, boys—take care of yourselves! We’re off!” Fritz shouted, as I stepped into our tub-boat and we pushed away from land.

The current took us briskly out of the bay. The surface of the water carried its own sound—hiss, lap, and that hollow knock when a small wave strikes wood. It was a music far kinder than any city’s hammering. In a world without wheels and iron streets, the sea’s voice could steady a man’s thoughts.

We soon moored safely alongside the wreck. Up her shattered side we scrambled, and stood upon what remained of her deck. Wind moved through her broken timbers with a low, complaining note, as if the ship itself still tried to speak in the old language of ropes and canvas.

I wanted a raft—broad-backed, obedient, able to bear what our little boat could never carry: weight, bulk, awkward angles that would capsize us if we tried to cram them aboard. Empty water-casks offered themselves as if the sea had rolled them there for this very purpose. We closed them tight, shoved them overboard, and set twelve side by side in rows of three, binding them with spars. Over this buoyant foundation we laid planks for a firm floor and built a low bulwark as defense against any sudden slop of wave.

In a short time we had, indeed, a first-rate raft, fit to serve our exile.

By evening it was impossible to return. Fatigue had settled into our arms and backs, and we had lived all day on the light refreshment we carried—dry mouthfuls, swallowed almost without noticing, because we grudged even minutes away from work.

Now, glad that we were not expected, we made our supper from the ship’s provisions. Salt meat, biscuit, and what small comforts could be found in tins and barrels—plain fare, but after hard labor it tasted like a feast. The ship’s stores carried their own smells: brine, flour, old wood, and spices sealed up from another world. We laid ourselves afterward upon spring mattresses—softness so luxurious it felt indecent—remembering our narrow hammocks with something like scorn.

The next morning we attacked the work with new vigor. First we carried off the entire contents of our own cabins. Then we went on to the captain’s room and removed furniture and fittings—doors, window-frames, bolts, bars, locks—everything that could become security for our house. We took the officers’ chests, and those belonging to the carpenter and gunsmith. Their weight was so great we had to empty them in portions, and even then the lifting made our shoulders burn.

One large chest glittered like a shop-window: watches in gold and silver, snuff-boxes, buckles, chains, rings, trinkets of every sort. There was also a box of money. For a little while, the shine held our eyes—human nature is old and easily dazzled. But such finery was a cold comfort in a wild land. Far more precious to us was a case of common knives and forks. I welcomed them like honest friends, better suited to our rough hands than any silver ornament we had brought ashore.

Then my delight rose in earnest: carefully packed, we found a number of young fruit trees. The tickets attached bore names that sounded like home itself spoken aloud—apple, pear, chestnut, orange, almond, peach, apricot, plum, cherry, vine. I touched the damp packing moss around their roots as if it were sacred. These were not merely plants; they were memory made living, a future orchard that could teach our children the taste of the old world even while their tongues learned a new one.

The cargo, meant to supply a distant colony, proved an almost inexhaustible treasure: ironmongery, plumber’s tools, lead, paint, grindstones, cart wheels, all that a forge would need, spades and plough-shares, sacks of maize, peas, oats, wheat, a hand-mill, and even the parts of a saw-mill—each piece numbered so carefully that, had we the strength, we might set it up without confusion.

We stood amid this abundance, bewildered. How does one choose what to save when the sea is ready to swallow the rest? The first storm would finish the wreck; everything left behind would be lost to us forever.

At last, selecting the most useful—grain and fruit trees above all—we loaded raft and boat gradually, sweating and calculating, trying to think like farmers rather than like thieves in a treasure-house.

We added fishing lines, reels, cordage, two harpoons, and a mariner’s compass. Fritz, remembering our encounter with the shark, laid the harpoons in readiness. He flourished one with a boy’s swagger, imagining himself a whaler, and I could not help smiling at the bright, dangerous enthusiasm that made him both a comfort and a risk.

By early afternoon both craft were heavily laden. With the raft in tow, we began the voyage shoreward with real anxiety; one bad twist, one gust, one misjudged pull, and the sea could take our harvest of labor.

But the water remained calm and the wind favorable. We spread the sail, and the motion smoothed into steady progress: the creak of rope, the soft slap along the hull, the low, satisfied groan of wood doing what it was made to do.

Then Fritz asked for the telescope. He had noticed something floating at a distance.

He handed it back, and I looked. A dark shape lay upon the water, motionless—a turtle, asleep on the sea, unconscious of our approach.

“Father—steer towards it!” Fritz cried.

I turned the boat, meaning only to indulge his curiosity, never dreaming what the lad would attempt behind the broad curtain of sail. My eyes were on the water, the raft’s line, the set of the wind—when suddenly a shock ran through the vessel, and I felt the unmistakable thrill of line racing through a reel. Before I could shout, a second shock came, and the boat lurched forward, drawn fast as if seized by some invisible hand.

“Fritz! What are you about?” I cried. “You are sending us to the bottom!”

“I have him—hurrah! I have him safe!” he shouted, wild with triumph.

To my amazement he had struck the turtle with a harpoon. A rope was attached; the creature was towing us—our boat, and our raft, and all our precious salvage—as if it were a toy.

I dropped the sail and seized my hatchet, hurrying forward to cut the line.

“Father! Wait!” Fritz pleaded. “There is no danger—just yet! I promise to cut it myself the instant it is necessary! Let us catch this turtle if we possibly can.”

“My dear boy,” I said, trying to keep my voice from turning into anger, “the turtle will be a very dear bargain if he upsets all our goods into the sea—even if he does not drown us too. For heaven’s sake be careful. I will wait a few minutes; but the instant there is danger, cut the line.”

The turtle made for the open sea. I hoisted the sail again, and the pressure of wind and our resistance turned him. He swung landward, dragging us rapidly. The shore he aimed at lay considerably left of our usual landing-place. The beach there shelved gradually, and at some distance from land we grounded with a sharp shock—hard enough to make my teeth click—yet, by mercy, without a capsize.

The turtle was exhausted. No wonder: it had acted the part of a steam tug, towing two loaded vessels at speed. It struggled to reach land; I leaped into the water, waded up, and with my axe struck it. The creature’s tenacity was dreadful. It did not cease its convulsions until I had severed head from body.

We were not far from Falconhurst. Fritz announced our approach by firing his gun and shouting with glee. While we still secured the boats and dragged the heavy turtle ashore, the whole family appeared in the distance, hastening toward us. Their figures grew larger against the green; even before I could see their faces I heard them—voices carrying across the open air, each one distinct in the clean, unspoiled soundscape, where no mechanical roar dulled the ear.

Our prize—the turtle, the raft, the boat piled high—excited the liveliest interest. But my wife’s chief pleasure was simply seeing us safe. Our night’s absence had worried her; and when she heard, in fuller detail, how we had been dragged at the whim of a harpooned beast, her hand went to her breast as if to press her heart back into its place.

We were anxious to remove some goods before night. The boys ran for the sledge. Lacking an anchor, I contrived to moor our vessels with heavy blocks of iron we had brought. Then came the turtle: such a weight that all of us together could scarcely get it onto the sledge. With the sapling fruit trees added, it made a load fit to break a lesser cart.

We set off for home, talking as we went, the rhythm of our footsteps and the squeal of the sledge runners stitching our day into a story that would be told again—perhaps altered, perhaps embellished—until even danger took on the sheen of family legend.

At Falconhurst the first task was supper: to put the turtle’s flesh into the pot. My wife regarded it as a work of time and difficulty, but I turned the creature on its back, broke the lower shell with the hatchet, and detached a portion from the breast.

“Cook it with a little salt,” I said, “shell and all.”

“But let me first cut away this disgusting green fat,” she said, with a shudder. “See how it sticks all over the meat. No one could eat anything so nasty.”

“Leave the fat—whatever you do!” I cried. “That is the very best part, the delight of epicures. If there is too much, cut some off—it can serve as lard, and the dogs may have the refuse.”

Even here, in exile, taste had its hierarchy, its old arguments. The tongue carries culture the way a seed carries a tree; and what one calls “disgusting” another calls “delicacy,” and neither word truly translates the other’s experience.

“And the handsome shell!” Fritz exclaimed. “I should like to make a water-trough of that, to stand near the brook and be kept always full of clear water. How useful it would be!”

“A capital idea,” I said, “and we may manage it easily, if we can find clay to make a firm foundation.”

“Oh, as to clay,” Jack cried, “I have a grand lump of clay there under that root.”

“Well done, my lad! When did you find it?”

“He found a bed of clay near the river this morning,” said his mother, “and came home in such a mess I had regularly to scrape his clothes and wash him thoroughly!”

“Well, mother,” Jack insisted, “I can only tell you I should never in all my days have found the clay if I had not slipped and fallen amongst it.”

“That I can well believe,” she returned, “only, to hear your talk this morning, one would have thought your discovery of clay the result of very arduous search indeed.”

“When you have ended the question of the clay and the turtle-shell,” said Ernest, “I should like to show you some roots I found today. They are getting rather dry now. They look something like radishes, although the plant itself was almost a bush; but I have not ventured to taste them—though our old sow was devouring them at a great rate.”

“In that you did wisely,” I told him. “Swine eat many things injurious to men. Let me see your roots. How did you discover them?”

“I was rambling in the wood,” he said, “and came upon the sow very busy grubbing under a small bush and eating something ravenously. So I drove her away, and found a number of these roots, which I brought for you to see.”

I took them in my hand and examined them closely—shape, skin, smell, the way the cut surface dulled in the air.

“Indeed, Ernest,” I exclaimed at last, “I am inclined to believe you have made a brilliant discovery. If this proves to be the manioc root, we might lose every other eatable we possess and yet not starve. In the West Indies cakes called cassava bread are made from it. Already having potatoes, we shall be very independent if we can succeed in preparing flour from these roots. But great care must be taken: the juice must be expressed, otherwise the flour may be injurious—even poisonous.”

The word “poisonous” fell among the children like a stone in water; the ripples of caution spread through their faces. Here, ignorance could kill as swiftly as a storm.

“If we can collect a sufficient quantity,” I continued, “we will attempt bread-making. I think I know how to set about it.”

There was still time before dark for another sledge-load, so I went back with the elder boys, leaving Franz with his mother. We walked with hunger sharpening our thoughts, all of us looking forward to the “princely supper” promised in the shade, for the day’s work had been heavy enough to make even ship’s biscuit seem like a dream.

“I have been thinking about my turtle, father,” Fritz said as we went. “Is not the shell valuable? Surely combs and boxes and ornamental things are made of tortoise-shell. It seems a pity to use it for a water-trough.”

“Your turtle,” I told him, “is only fit for eating. Its shell is worthless for ornament. The species prized for tortoise-shell is unfit for food. The shell used for such work is treated with heat; the outer layer peels off, leaving a beautifully marked surface, semi-transparent and capable of high polish.”

The sledge quickly received its second load: chests, four cart-wheels, the hand-mill, and many smaller articles. We hurried home.

My wife met us with relief that bordered on reproach. She said we had been “regularly overworked” for two days. “However, now you are come home to rest,” she said, “and you little think what refreshment awaits you here in the shade. Come and see my cellar!”

She showed us a small cask, half sunk in the ground and sheltered with leaves and branches like a creature she had tucked into a nest.

“You wonder where this came from,” she continued. “I found it myself on the sands today while you were absent. I fancied it was wine of some sort, and I got it up here on purpose for you. The boys are most anxious to know what sort it will prove to be.”

To discover this simply, I inserted a straw at the vent-hole. A sip rose to my mouth—warm, fragrant, honeyed—and I felt at once how taste can call up a whole vanished world. “In all my life,” I declared, “I have never enjoyed a more delicious draught of canary sack.”

My wife’s pleasure was plain; her labor had not been wasted. The boys pressed in with straws, begging a taste. After praising it as I had, I could hardly deny them; I let them come in turns, but soon had to call a halt. Their eagerness turned noisy and reckless, and I reproved them for greediness, warning them of the risk of intoxication. Here, where every sound mattered—the dog’s alarm, the bird’s sudden silence, the night wind’s change—there was danger in dulling the senses for a sweet moment.

Supper was more to the purpose. The turtle proved delicious, rich with its green fat melted into the meat like seasoned lard, and the family ate heartily. The smell of it—sea-brine tamed by fire and salt—filled our leafy rooms and made the tree-house feel, for an hour, less like a refuge and more like a home with customs. Strength returned with the meal. We hauled the spring mattresses up into our sleeping-rooms, and very refreshing slumbers closed the day.

Early next morning I rose without rousing any of the others. I meant to visit the beach; I still doubted the safety of our vessels on an open shore. The dogs, delighted, bounded to meet me. The cocks crowed and flapped; two pretty kids gambolled, tapping their small hooves on the wood. All was life and clear sound—each creature voicing itself into the morning. Only the ass seemed disinclined to begin the day.

As I especially required his services, this was unfortunate.

I put his dreams to flight, harnessed him to the sledge, and left the cow dozing unmilked so as not to wake the household. The world outside our tree was cool, smelling of damp earth and crushed leaf, and the distance carried the hush of a land that had not yet learned to roar.

My fears about the boats were soon dispelled: they were safe. I collected only a light load and returned quickly. The donkey trotted willingly, and both of us were uncommonly ready for breakfast.

Approaching our tree, not a sound was to be heard, not a soul to be seen, though it was broad day. When I climbed up, my good wife started in astonishment at the clatter I made.

“What can have made us oversleep ourselves like this?” she cried. “It must be the fault of those mattresses; they are delightful, but really too lulling—see, the children are sound asleep still!”

With much stretching and many yawns, the boys tumbled down at last, rubbing their eyes and looking half awake. Ernest came last, as usual.

“Come, my boys,” I said, “this will never do. Your beds were too luxurious last night.”

Yet in my own mind I laid blame not only on softness. The captain’s canary had likely helped to hush our senses too thoroughly. I resolved it should be dealt with sparingly in future.

“Now for prayers and breakfast,” I continued, “and then to work. I must have our cargo landed in time to get the boats off with the next tide.”

By dint of hard labor we accomplished it. As soon as the vessels were afloat I got on board with Fritz. The rest turned homeward; but Jack lingered behind, his face pleading so earnestly that I could not resist. I took him with us.

My intention had been simply to take the vessels round to the harbor in Safety Bay. But the calm sea and fine weather tempted me to make another trip to the wreck. It took more time than expected, so that when we reached the ship we could only examine further, gather a few portable things, and then hurry away to catch the sea-breeze before it failed in the evening.

To Jack, rummaging in the hold was new enchantment. Soon a tremendous rattling and clattering announced him. He came pushing a wheelbarrow, eyes bright, cheeks flushed with victory, delighted at having found so “capital” a thing for bringing home potatoes.

Fritz followed with still more important news. He had found, packed carefully within partitions, what appeared to be the separate parts of a pinnace—rigging and fittings complete, even to a couple of small brass guns. It was indeed a great discovery. I hastened to see, and he was right. My pleasure, however, was tempered by the thought of the labor required to assemble such a craft fit for sea.

We had barely time to eat and hurry into the boat. Our new acquisitions included a copper boiler, iron plates, tobacco-graters, two grindstones, a small barrel of powder, another of flints, two wheelbarrows besides Jack’s—he guarded his as if it were a pet.

As we drew near shore we were surprised to see a line of little figures ranged along the water’s edge, gazing fixedly at us. They seemed dressed in dark coats and white waistcoats, and stood quite still with arms hanging, except that now and then one extended them gently, as though longing to embrace us.

“Ah! Here at last come the pigmy inhabitants of the country to welcome us!” I cried, laughing.

“Oh, father!” Jack exclaimed. “I hope they are Lilliputians! I once read in a book about them, so there must be such people—you know—only these look rather too large.”

“You must be content to give up the Lilliputians and accept penguins, my dear Jack,” I said. “We have not before seen them in such numbers. Ernest knocked one down, if you remember, soon after we landed. They swim excellently, but on land they are helpless—neither flying nor running.”

We were nearing shallows as I spoke. Jack sprang out, waded ashore, and with his stick felled half a dozen, right and left, either stunned or dead at his feet. The rest escaped into the water, dived, and vanished.

As these penguins are disagreeable food, with a strong oily taste, I was sorry he had attacked them. But when we examined the fallen, some rose from their swoon and began solemnly to waddle away. We caught them, tied their feet together with long grass, and laid them on the sand until we were ready to start.

The three wheelbarrows each received a load. The live penguins, seated gravely, were trundled along by Jack, and away we went at a great rate.

The unusual noise of our approach set the dogs barking furiously. When they recognized us, they rushed forward with such forceful demonstrations of delight that little Jack—already struggling with his barrow—was fairly upset, penguins and all. It was absurd to see him leap up and cuff the dogs soundly for their boisterous welcome.

This scene, and the examination of our burdens, caused great merriment. The tobacco-graters and iron plates puzzled everyone.

I sent the boys to catch some geese and ducks and told them to fasten a penguin to each by the leg; it seemed worth trying to tame them.

While we had been away my wife had not been idle. She had stored potatoes and also manioc root. I praised her industry, and little Franz—bursting with secrets—cried, “Ah, father! I wonder what you will say when mother and I give you some Indian corn, and melons, and pumpkins and cucumbers!”

“Now, you little chatterbox!” she cried. “You have let out my secret! I was to have the pleasure of surprising your father when my plants were growing up.”

“Ah, the poor disappointed little mother,” I said. “Never mind—I am charmed to hear it. Only tell me: where did those seeds come from?”

“Out of my magic bag, of course!” she replied. “Each time I have gone for potatoes, I have sown seeds in the ground dug up to get them, and I have planted potatoes also.”

“Well done, you wise little woman!” I cried. “You are a model of prudence and industry!”

“But,” she continued, eyeing our haul, “I do not half like the appearance of those tobacco-graters. Is it possible you are going to make snuff? Pray let us make sure of abundance of food for our mouths before we think of our noses!”

“Make your mind easy,” I said. “I have not the remotest intention of introducing the dirty, ridiculous habit of snuffing into your family. But treat my graters with respect: they are to be the means of providing you with the first fresh bread you have seen this many a long day.”

“What possible connection can there be between bread and tobacco-graters?” she said. “And to talk of bread where there are no ovens is only tantalizing.”

“Ah, you must not expect real loaves,” I answered. “But on these flat iron plates I can bake flat cakes—scones—which will serve us as excellent bread. I mean to try at once what I can do with Ernest’s roots. And first of all, I want you to make me a nice strong canvas bag.”

She willingly undertook it, though I could see she had little faith in my baking. While she set to work she also set on a potful of potatoes, as if to make sure a meal would exist whether my cassava scheme succeeded or not.

Now that the question of bread had been answered—answered not with idle wishing, but with hands blackened by work and a hearth that finally smelled like a true home—my mind kept circling back to the wreck, as a tongue circles a sore tooth. There were stores still sealed inside her ribs: flour that would spare us more cassava-grinding, rice that would soften in a pot instead of fighting the teeth, casks that might hold salt or sugar. And above all, the pinnace: that neat, beautiful little vessel like a promise folded up and stowed away.

To win her, I needed all the strength we could muster. So I pressed my wife—gently at first, then with the stubbornness of a man who hears the sea calling through every quiet hour—to let me go to the wreck with all the boys save Franz.

She resisted as any sensible woman would; there are bargains you strike with necessity and then regret the moment you speak them aloud. At last she consented, but only after I pledged—reluctantly, because I knew how much time the work would swallow—that I would never pass a night aboard the wreck. We separated with the uneasy tenderness of those who agree, yet do not feel entirely satisfied.

The boys, however, were light-hearted as sparrows. They slung provision-bags over their shoulders—cassava-bread wrapped carefully against damp, potatoes tucked in like round stones of comfort—and set out as if the walk itself were a holiday.

At Safety Bay we first visited our marsh tenants. The geese and ducks greeted us with their low, gossiping gabble, the soft percussion of bills in water and the wing-rustle that made the reed-beds sound alive. We fed them and saw they prospered. It pleased me—this small husbandry in a wild place—to hear their contented murmur, a domestic music that answered the harsh, indifferent thunder of the surf beyond.

Then each buckled on his cork-belt. We stepped into our ridiculous tub-boat, towed the raft behind, and pushed out toward the wreck, the water slapping in hollow notes against the staves of our craft.

Once aboard, I set the boys to gathering whatever came first to hand and loading it on the raft so our return would not be empty. Meanwhile I went straight to the pinnace and began a careful inspection.

What I found sobered my triumph. The little vessel lay at the far end of the hold, packed into a cramped, narrow compartment like a delicate tool wedged in stone. There was no room to assemble her there. Yet the parts were so heavy that hauling them out piece by piece into a wider space seemed equally hopeless. The wreck had swallowed her and intended to keep her.

I sent the boys off to rummage—partly to keep their spirits from souring, partly because I needed silence—and sat down amid the dim, dusty air to think.

As my eyes adjusted to the gloom, I noticed something that made my heart lift: every part of the pinnace had been carefully arranged and numbered. Whoever had stowed her here had expected her to be fitted together again. Time, space, and patience—those were what she demanded, like any good bread that refuses to be hurried.

“Room,” I called when my sons came back, bright-eyed and trusting as if the world never withheld anything from determined hands. “Room to work in, boys! That’s what we must win first.”

I bade them fetch axes. We attacked the wooden partition, chopping and hewing to clear space around the imprisoned craft. The sound of our blows reverberated strangely through the ship’s belly—a dull, suffocating thud that made the wreck seem to groan. But evening fell before we had made much impression. The timberwork had been built to endure storms, not boys with axes.

We had to admit the truth: an immense labour and steady perseverance would be required before we could claim the pinnace. She lay in that vast hulk like a fossil shell embedded in rock—beautiful, entire, and stubbornly out of reach.

We returned to shore with tired limbs and little relish for the long walk back to Falconhurst—only to find, to our astonishment and joy, my wife and little Franz waiting for us at Tentholm. She had come with quiet resolution and that practical courage which so often outlasts our noisier kind.

“In this way,” she said, smiling as if it were the simplest thing in the world to uproot herself for our sake, “you will live nearer your work, and I shall not quite lose sight of you.”

I could have embraced her on the spot. “You are a good, sensible, kind wife,” I cried, delighted by her plan. “And we shall work the more diligently, that you may return the sooner to your dear Falconhurst.”

“Come and see what we have brought you, mother!” Fritz exclaimed. He and his brothers rolled forward two small casks of butter and three of flour, with corn, rice, and other treasure besides—things that spoke to my wife more eloquently than any boast. Butter meant pastry, frying, richness; flour meant real loaves, not merely survival-cakes; rice meant bowls of steam and softness on days when teeth were weary of root and fibre.

Her eyes shone, not with greed, but with that deep satisfaction known only to those who keep a household alive: the food-historian’s pleasure in stores, the farmer’s gratitude in provisions, the cook’s visions kindled by ingredients.

So began a stretch of days that welded toil to hope. We rose early, took the tub-boat and raft to the wreck, and worked until dusk. First we cut away and cleared an open space around the pinnace; then we began assembling her, matching numbered pieces like a vast, patient puzzle. Every evening we brought back a freight: tools, cloth, casks, anything of use. Tentholm became a place of industry and supper-smells—cassava and potatoes at first, then richer meals as flour and butter transformed our table. Even in our exile, the ritual of eating together gathered the day into meaning.

At last, after labour that felt beyond belief, the pinnace stood complete—ready to be launched—and yet still imprisoned. Massive wooden walls hemmed her in, mocking our strength.

She looked awake, that graceful little vessel, as though she had dreamt for years in darkness and now longed to leap into the free blue sea and spread her wings to the wind. To have come so far only to fail would have been a cruelty.

I turned the problem over and over, as one turns over a stubborn dough, seeking the moment it yields. Despair began to sour my thoughts—until an idea came like a sudden change of wind.

I said nothing to the boys. Instead I fetched a large cast-iron mortar, filled it with gunpowder, secured a block of oak atop it, and pierced a hole for the match. I placed this great petard so that its force would blow out the side of the wreck next to the pinnace. Then I chained it fast so the recoil would do no harm.

When all was ready, I told the boys I meant to go ashore earlier than usual and calmly ordered them into the boat. My heart hammered as if it wished to betray me. I lit the prepared match—long-burning, slow as a fuse in a nightmare—and hurried after them.

We brought the raft close to shore and began unloading, talking as if nothing were unusual. I left the other boat afloat and ready, and while my family worked, I listened with every nerve. The sea’s ordinary sounds—lap, hiss, gull-cry—seemed sharpened, waiting.

Then it came.

A flash. A mighty roar. Smoke burst up like a storm-cloud torn from the ocean.

My wife and children froze, terror-stricken, eyes flying seaward. The blast rolled along the cliffs and returned in echoes, the landscape suddenly full of thunder. In that moment I could not help thinking how sound itself rules the heart: how a single violent noise can turn peace into panic, and how much worse it must be in lands where such noise is constant—where machines and clamor grind the ear until fear becomes the everyday air.

My wife looked to me, searching my face for explanation. “Perhaps,” she said, because I held my tongue, “perhaps you have left a light burning near some of the gunpowder, and an explosion has taken place.”

“Not at all unlikely,” I replied, forcing calm into my voice. “We had a fire below when we were caulking the seams of the pinnace. I shall go off at once and see what has happened. Will anyone come?”

The boys needed no second invitation; they sprang into the boat. I lingered only long enough to whisper a few words to my wife—enough to steady her—and then joined them. We pulled for the wreck faster than ever before, oars biting water with sharp, urgent splashes.

On the side where we usually boarded, nothing looked changed. We rounded to the other side—and there a marvellous, ruinous sight awaited us. A huge rent gaped in the ship. Decks and bulwarks were torn open. The sea was strewn with floating fragments. The wreck looked as though it had taken one last, fatal breath.

And the compartment where the pinnace had slept lay open to the sky.

There sat our little beauty, to all appearance uninjured.

The boys stared at the scene of destruction, too overwhelmed to speak. But I—who saw through the smoke to the one thing that mattered—shouted in a joy that sprang up like a hymn: “Hurrah! She is ours! The lovely pinnace is won! We shall be able to launch her easily after all. Come, boys, let us see if she has suffered from the explosion which has set her free.”

They looked at me, then comprehension dawned. “You planned it!” they cried. “You clever, cunning father! That machine we helped to make—it was to blow it up!”

They followed me into the shattered opening. I examined her carefully, hands running over planks and seams, listening to the quiet creak of wood that had been woken and not broken. To my intense satisfaction, the pinnace had suffered nothing.

The boys, fascinated, pestered me for an explanation. I told them the principle and management of a petard, warning them that such power is a servant that must be handled with fear and knowledge, never with vanity.

Now the launch itself was plain. I had already placed rollers beneath her keel. With levers and pulleys and the united strength of all hands, we worked her forward toward the water. A rope was fixed to regulate her descent. Shoulders braced, breath held, we set her in motion. She slid—slowly at first, then more willingly—until at last she slipped gently into the sea.

She floated as if she had always belonged there, as if she recognized the taste and cradle of her native element. We cheered until our throats burned.

We secured our prize at the most sheltered point and returned to Tentholm. There we spoke of the explosion in general terms, saying only that one side of the ship had been blown away and that we should now be able to reach her remaining contents with far fewer days of labour.

Those days we spent in completing the rigging, mounting the two little brass guns, and making all necessary arrangements. It was astonishing how quickly the possession of two real guns awakened martial ardour. The boys talked incessantly of savages, fleets of canoes, attacks and defenses, and final annihilations—turning danger into a kind of play-song.

I reminded them that, brilliant as their imagined victories might be, we should have cause to thank God if their fighting powers were never put to the proof.

At last the pinnace was fully equipped and ready. Yet my wife still suspected nothing of the surprise. The boys, who had kept the secret with admirable discipline, begged leave to fire a salute as we entered the bay. I consented, though I feared the harsh crack would assault her ears and heart.

We cast off from the wreck, spread the sail, and began our voyage. The pinnace glided swiftly, neat and obedient. I stood at the helm. Ernest and Jack took the guns. Fritz, puffed up with command, gave the word: “Fire!”

Bang! Bang!

The reports rang out, sharp and thrilling, and the cliffs flung them back in echo after echo until the whole bay seemed full of cannon. The boys shouted and hurrahed, drunk on their own noise.

On shore my wife and little Franz rushed forward from near the tent. Even at a distance I could see alarm in their posture: the sudden violent sound had turned the day strange. But when she recognized our voices, her fear melted into relief, and she waved and hurried down to the landing-place.

With careful handling we brought the pinnace close to a projection of the bank. Fritz helped his mother aboard. Breathless with haste and excitement, she burst out, “You dear, horrid, wonderful people—shall I scold you or praise you? You have frightened me out of my wits! To see a beautiful little ship come sailing in was startling enough, for I could not conceive who might be on board; but the report of your guns made me tremble with fear—and had I not recognized your voices directly after, I should have run away with Franz, Heaven knows where!”

Then, as her eyes took in the craft more calmly, wonder overcame reproach. “Have you really done all this work yourselves? What a charming little yacht! I should not be afraid to sail in this myself.”

We basked a little in her admiration—because praise is a kind of nourishment too, especially after long labour—then she said, “Now you must come with me and see how little Franz and I have improved our time every day of your absence.”

We landed and followed her up the river toward the cascade. There, to our astonishment, she had laid out a garden: beds and walks neatly planned, the soil turned and tamed. The place smelled of fresh earth and crushed green, the scent that rises when ground is opened to seed. Even the sound was different there: water murmuring over stones, leaves whispering above, a gentle acoustic shelter from the sea’s harsher voice.

“We don’t frighten people by firing salutes in honour of our performances,” she said, giving us a look that mixed humor with reproof, “though by and by I too shall want fire—in a peaceable form. Look at my beds of lettuce and cabbages, my rows of beans and peas! Think what delicious dinners I shall be able to cook for you, and give me credit for my diligence.”

My mind leapt at the images: lettuce crisp with cool river-water, cabbages sweetened by sun, peas that would pop green and tender, beans that would simmer with butter. In that moment the island felt less like exile and more like a new chapter of older human stories—seed and stew, work and worship.

“My dear wife,” I exclaimed, “this is beautiful! You have done wonders! Did you not find the work too hard?”

“The ground is light and easy to dig hereabouts,” she replied. “I have planted potatoes, and cassava-roots. There is space for sugar-canes and the young fruit trees. And I shall want you to contrive to irrigate them by leading water from the cascades in hollow bamboos. Up by the sheltering rocks I mean to have pineapples and melons; they will look splendid when they spread there. To shelter the beds of European vegetables from the heat of the sun, I have planted seeds of maize round them. The shadow of the tall plants will afford protection from the burning rays. Do you think that is a good plan?”

“It is capital,” I told her—because it was. She spoke like an elder of an old agrarian line, shaping the land with knowledge and patience, making a future out of soil and water. “Now, as sunset approaches, we must return to the tent for supper and rest, for both of which we are all quite ready.”

That evening passed in happy talk. Everyone had that deep, clean satisfaction which attends successful labour, and the added joy of giving unexpected pleasure to others. I pointed out to my sons how real happiness is found there—in the shared bread, the shared work, the shared surprise—rather than in mere self-gratification.

Next morning my wife said, “If you can exist on shore long enough to visit Falconhurst, dear husband, I should like you to attend to the little fruit trees. I fear they have been too much neglected. I have watered them occasionally and spread earth over the roots as they lay, but I could not manage to plant them.”

“You have done far more than I could have expected,” I replied, “and provided you do not ask me to give up the sea altogether, I agree most willingly. I will go to Falconhurst as soon as the raft is unloaded and everything safely arranged here.”

Life on shore became a pleasant change. The boys worked with energy; the stores were quickly carried up to the tent, piled in order, and covered with sailcloth, pegged down all round. The pinnace, newly anchored, lay properly moored. Her elegant shape transformed our harbour, which until then had been graced only by our grotesque tub-boat and flat, uninteresting raft.

We took an ample supply for Falconhurst and soon were reestablished in that charming home. Its shade felt cooler than ever after the heat and hard work at Tentholm; even the quiet there seemed richer, like a familiar dialect heard again after days of harsher tongues.

Several Sundays had passed at Tentholm, and now the Day of Rest returned. We observed it with heartfelt devotion and grateful praise. I did not attempt long sermons, knowing well the limits of young attention, but we read from the Bible, drew simple instruction, and sang favourite hymns my wife knew from memory—her voice carrying the tunes as if she were preserving them in a place where no church bell would ever ring. In such songs there is a kind of cultural memory: without them, the language of home thins and fades, and a family becomes merely bodies surviving together.

In the evening I had the boys show their skill at running, leaping, wrestling, and climbing, telling them they must keep their bodies strong and active—powerful to repel danger, agile enough to escape it.

“No man can be truly courageous and self-reliant,” I said, “without an inward consciousness of physical power and capability. I want to see my sons strong, both morally and physically. That means, little Franz”—for his great blue eyes were fixed on me—“brave to do what is good and right, and strong to work, hunt, and provide for themselves and others, and to fight if necessary.”

The following day, seeing them eager for exercise, I encouraged them with the promise of a curious new weapon—provided they did not neglect archery, for their guns I knew they would never forget.

I took a long cord and fastened a leaden bullet to each end. Instantly came the storm of questions.

“This is a miniature lasso,” I explained. “Mexicans, Patagonians, and various tribes of South America use such a weapon in hunting with marvellous dexterity. They have ropes far longer than this and stones instead of bullets. One end is swung round and round the hunter’s head and then cast with skill toward the animal. Drawing it back, he can strike again, killing or wounding his prey.

“Sometimes, however, they wish to take the animal alive—wild horse, buffalo, whatever it may be. Then the lasso is thrown in such a way that it twists many times round the neck, body, or legs of the fugitive, arresting him even at full speed.”

“Oh, father!” cried the boys. “Try it now! There is the donkey! Catch the donkey!”

Not trusting my own skill upon a living creature, I refused and chose a stump at no great distance. My success surprised even myself: the cord whipped out and wound round the stump so neatly that there could be no doubt of the weapon’s use. At once the boys clamoured for lassos of their own. Because the making was simple, I granted their petitions, and lasso-practice became the order of the day. Fritz, being strongest and most adroit, soon excelled.

That night the weather changed. Early next morning I saw a gale rising; from the height of our trees the sea looked violently agitated. I thought with satisfaction of our hard-won pinnace lying safe at anchor, and of the fact that nothing called us to the wreck for some days.

My attention was not held only by the boys’ amusements. My wife had much to show me, asking approval here, advice there, help where it was needed. She had snared a good supply of wild pigeons and ortolans and had partly cooked and preserved them in lard. She showed me the small cask well filled—meat protected against spoilage, ready to be reheated and served when labour left no time for hunting. She showed me too the nests of our tame pigeons, but her chief anxiety remained the fruit trees, dry and withered from neglect.

We set about planting them at once, proposing afterward an excursion to the Calabash Wood to make a great supply of vessels and utensils. Everyone was eager for the expedition. So the orchard planting was carried on with surprising vigour, though it was not finished until toward evening. Then we prepared for an early start next day. My wife and Franz would come too; their equipment took time, for we meant to make a grand family excursion—complete with our domestic pets and servants.

By sunrise we were all astir. The sledge, loaded with ammunition and baskets of provisions and drawn by the donkey, would carry home our gourd manufactures and any other prizes we might find.

Turk headed the procession, as usual, clanking in his coat of mail. Then came the boys with guns and game-bags. My wife and I followed. Behind trotted Juno, out of sorts, because Master Knips refused to be left behind and insisted on riding her back like a little tyrant.

I carried two guns: one loaded with shot for game, one with ball for defence against beasts.

We crossed Flamingo Marsh and came into the magnificent country beyond, spread out in beauty and fertility. It was new to my wife and to two of the boys. Their delight was plain; they breathed in the scents of crushed grass and warm resin, and even the air seemed to ring with a different chorus—birds calling, insects humming, wind moving through leaves in a sound like distant rain.

Here Fritz and Jack darted into the bush. Loud barking followed, then the quick report of a gun. A large bird sprang from the thicket and fell heavily to the ground before us.

But it was not resigned to death. Unable to fly, it leapt up and rushed away with extraordinary speed. Turk barked furiously and gave chase; Fritz ran after it, panting. Juno, eager, sprang aside so suddenly that poor Knips was flung into the sand as she darted to intercept the bird’s retreat. She succeeded in cutting it off, but the bird defended itself fiercely, striking with powerful legs and sharp claws so that neither Fritz nor the dogs could master it.

I hurried to assist and found Juno holding bravely to a wing while the bird—a magnificent bustard—struggled and fought. Watching my moment, I threw a large handkerchief over its head and, with difficulty, bound its legs and wings. We carried it back in triumph to the others, who had been resting on the sand.

“What have you got?” they cried, jumping up.

“A bustard!” Jack shouted in delight. “Splendid! It is the very one we missed that day, don’t you remember, mother? Ah-ha! Old fellow, you are done for this time!”

Ernest examined it gravely. “I think this is a hen bustard—the mother bird.”

At that my wife’s face softened with concern. “Ah, poor thing. It is most likely the same, and I know she had a brood of young birds. Now they will be left unprotected and miserable. Had we not better let her go?”

“My dear, kind-hearted wife,” I said, “that was weeks ago. Those young birds will be strong by now, and I daresay Mrs. Bustard has forgotten the nursery cares. Besides, she is badly wounded; we must try to cure her. If we succeed, she will be a valuable addition to our poultry-yard. If not—then you shall roast her for dinner.”

The words “roast her” drew a different light into the boys’ faces, for in their minds already she was browning over a fire, her fat hissing, the smell filling our tent. Even in adventure, the thought of supper is never far; and perhaps it should not be, for food is the oldest story we tell ourselves in any land.

We marched on and soon reached the Monkey Grove—the very place of Fritz’s tragicomic adventure that had made him guardian to the orphan ape. He entertained us with a lively account, acting out the scene so well that even my wife laughed in spite of herself.

Ernest, however, stood apart beneath a splendid cocoanut palm, gazing upward with fixed admiration at the immense stem and the graceful crown of leaves. The cluster of nuts below the fronds held his attention like a pantry shelf high out of reach. As I drew near I heard him sigh and murmur, half to himself, “It’s awfully high! I wish one would fall down!”

No sooner had he spoken than, as if the island understood his words and answered them in its own language, a huge nut plumped down at his feet.

He sprang aside, startled, looking up with timid awe. Then another nut dropped.

“Why, this is just like the fairy tale of the wishing-cap!” Ernest cried. “My wish is granted as soon as formed!”

“I suspect the fairy,” I said, “is less inclined to give gifts than to pelt us and drive us away. There may be a cross-grained old ape up there among those leaves.”

We examined the fallen nuts. They were not old; they were not even quite ripe. That made the matter stranger. We all surrounded the tree, craning our necks and staring into the shadowy crown.

“Hollo! I see him!” Fritz shouted suddenly. “Oh, a hideous creature! What can it be? Flat, round, as big as a plate—and with a pair of horrid claws! Here he comes! He is going to creep down the tree!”

At dawn the next day we turned our faces again toward Calabash Wood, for the sledge waited there with the dishes, bowls, and baskets we had shaped with such patient, homely pride. I took only Fritz with me. The others I left beside their mother; I meant to look beyond the rocky line that fenced our coast, and in such searching work a smaller tread seemed wiser.

The evergreen oaks received us with their clean, resinous breath and the soft, papery hiss of leaves rubbing one another—an honest, living hush that made our own footfalls sound like intruders. Our sow was there, snout deep among the acorns, crunching with a contented rhythm that seemed to mend yesterday’s alarm. If anything, she regarded us more kindly than usual, as though she had decided we were allies against the savage dogs.

Birdsong stitched the grove together—bright calls, dry clicks, a fluting that traveled from branch to branch like a hand passing bread. The birds scarcely stirred until Fritz, eager for sport and for the pot, fired and brought down a splendid blue jay, then two parakeets: one scarlet as embers, the other green and gold like sun through young leaves. He was ramming home his next charge when a new sound struck both our ears and froze us mid-step.

It came like a drum muffled under earth: dull, repeated thumps swelling into a low roll. In that instant my thoughts leapt, as they often did now, to the possibility of men—strangers, savages, a language we could not answer, a lexicon that might claim our shore by naming it. We moved with the caution of those who have only one home left. Keeping low among creepers and thick grass, we edged toward the noise until the undergrowth thinned into an open glade.

There, upon an old fallen log, stood a bird about the size of a cock. His chestnut coat was mottled with dark brown and grey like bark after rain, and on his shoulders rose curious tufts of velvety black glossed with green. He strutted and wheeled with grave ceremony, ruffling, erecting, showing himself off to a ring of plainer companions gathered about him like an audience around a village storyteller. He was not merely feeding or fleeing; he was performing, and the forest itself seemed to hold its breath to listen.

Then he spread his tail like a fan, stiffened his wings, and began striking them in short rapid beats. Faster—faster—until the air itself trembled. A rumble like very distant thunder rolled out, and for a moment his whirring wings wrapped him as a cloud. Now the mystery was plain: the “drum” was his courtship, amplified when the wing-strokes met the hollowed, decayed log beneath him.

I watched, captivated by this wild pageantry—by the way even a creature’s daily need could become art in a world not yet soured by engines and iron clamor. And then, behind me, a shot cracked the grove open.

The drummer toppled dead from his perch. The ring of birds exploded outward in alarm, vanishing into the leaves as if the forest had exhaled.

My indignation flared, sharp as the gun-smoke. Fritz’s impatience had turned rare comedy into needless tragedy. I blamed him openly; he had fired without leave, interrupting what few men ever see. I was all but certain it was a ruffed grouse, and a magnificent specimen—yet my regret was not only for the meat or the rarity, but for the sound itself, that delicate thunder now silenced forever.

We set the bird upon the donkey, who had waited with saintly stillness, and went on.

The sledge lay safe where we had left it, and the day was still young. I kept my resolve: we would follow the line of cliff and rocky hills that, at varying distance from the shore, bounded all the country we knew. I wanted to find a passage inland, or else learn, once for all, whether we were walled into this strip of coast like bees sealed in a glass hive.

Leaving Calabash Wood behind, we crossed ground lush with manioc, potatoes, and unknown plants with fat leaves and saps that stained the fingers. Clear streamlets ran through it, their music steady and medicinal, a reminder that this island’s quiet was not emptiness but order.

Soon a shrub drew my eye, heavy with small white berries that looked as though they had been dipped in pale wax. When Fritz plucked one, it clung to his fingers, sticky and strange. My heart lifted at once; here was Myrica cerifera. I explained what it promised us: candles—real light after sunset, not merely the brief glow of a fire and then sleep by necessity. His mother, who hated to be driven from her work the moment the sky dimmed, would rejoice.

We did not delay. With the donkey beside us we filled a great canvas bag with the wax berries. I told Fritz the wax would be greener, more brittle than beeswax, yet it would burn fairly and give off a pleasing scent, as though the plant remembered sunlight and could release it again at night.

Not far beyond we met another wonder: a whole colony of birds housed beneath one roof. Their nests—brown birds, the size of yellowhammers—were built in a clustered mass around a solitary tree, and a roof of grass and fibres covered the community as neatly as thatch over a village. Openings dotted the irregular sides, like doors into tiny apartments. Twigs protruded here and there and served as perches and lookouts. The entire establishment, from a distance, resembled a vast bath-sponge alive with motion.

Thousands of little bodies swarmed in and out, and among them flashed many small parrots, bright as fruit. Some seemed to be quarrelling with the rightful owners as impudently as tenants who have learned the landlord’s language better than the landlord.

Fritz, nimble as a cat and always hungry for the next marvel, climbed to inspect the holes, hoping to steal a young bird. He thrust his hand into several empty nests, growing bolder each time—until, reaching deep into one cavity, he yelped and snatched his hand back. A powerful beak had clamped down on his finger like a vice. He shook his hand hard, more from surprise than pain.

Stung in pride, he tried again. This time he seized the offender, dragged it out despite its shrieks, and stuffed it into his pocket, buttoning his coat with an air of triumph. Instantly the air filled with furious wings. Relations of the captive burst from the other entrances, screeching, swooping, pecking at the robber’s shoulders and hair.

His prize was not one of the brown builders—the nests belonged to the sociable grosbeak—but a pretty small green parrot, which delighted him. He was already speaking of taming it and teaching it words, as if language were a net we could throw over any creature. For the moment, however, the parrot remained a prisoner against Fritz’s warm side, beating its heart like a trapped drum.

As we walked on, that bird-city gave us much to ponder: their cheerful labour for the common good, their instinct to build together, to shelter together, to live by shared structure. I spoke of other communities in nature—beavers with their dams, bees and wasps and ants with their cities, even the coral insect that, by perseverance and union, raises whole kingdoms under the sea.

Fritz told me he had often watched ants at work at home; the bustle of their little commonwealth had amused him.

“Have you noticed,” I asked, “the care they take with what people call their eggs—how they carry them into the sun for warmth?”

He corrected me, proud and precise: it was not truly eggs they were carrying, but the chrysalis of the larva, produced from the egg. The common name was wrong.

He was right, and I told him so. Yet I could not help adding that in warmer lands the ant’s industry becomes a force beyond imagining: mounds four or six feet high, with streets and galleries and nurseries so firm that, if abandoned and altered, a man might use one as an oven. The ant had been praised since Solomon as a model of diligence, yet it was not a pleasant creature; it exuded sticky moisture, smelled unpleasant, and devoured whatever food it found. In those foreign countries, great ants were ruinous, hard to check—kept down only by enemies: birds, other insects, and beasts.

I described the tamanoir, the great ant-eater of South America: long as a man is tall, with coarse hair trailing like a heavy plume, head narrow and elongated, no teeth, and a tongue like a great red earthworm. Its claws could tear down the hard walls of an ant-hill; then its sticky tongue would coil among the swarming millions and draw them into its hungry mouth. There was also a smaller ant-eater with finer fur, living in trees. Fritz listened as though I were seasoning a dish: each detail a pinch of spice, making the world sharper on the tongue of the mind.

In time we reached a grove of tall trees with strong broad thick leaves. They bore a round fruit like a fig, full of little seeds and sour to the taste—harsh enough to pucker the mouth. Fritz noticed gum exuding from cracks in the bark, and he fell to scraping it off with the same delight boys have in hoarding cherry-tree gum at home. He returned to me kneading it, displeased that it would not soften properly. He was about to throw it away when it stretched between his fingers and sprang back.

“Father—look! It’s elastic! Could it be india-rubber?”

I took it, tested it myself, and felt my pulse quicken. “If it is,” I said, “we have found a treasure.”

He asked what use it could be besides rubbing out pencil marks, and I explained as well as I could: caoutchouc, a milky resin tapped from certain trees in South America; how the natives made bottles by coating earthen molds again and again, then breaking the mold and smoking the finished shape until it darkened and toughened. Europeans cut such bottles into pieces for drawing. Here, on our island, it meant something more urgent than novelty: it might be shaped into useful forms—above all, boots and shoes to keep our feet dry when the rains came and the ground turned treacherous.

Not long after, the cocoanut wood appeared ahead, and beyond it the bay and that great promontory we had named Cape Disappointment, the limit of our wandering so far. Passing beneath the palms, I noticed a smaller sort among its grand companions. One had been broken by wind, and its pith looked mealy. My mind leapt again: sago-palm.

In that pith crawled fat worms—maggots—feeding, as I suddenly remembered hearing, on the sago itself. In the West Indies they were said to be eaten as a delicacy. Hunger and curiosity make philosophers of castaways; also cooks, if they have any courage. I kindled a fire at once, skewered half a dozen on a little wooden spit, sprinkled them with salt, and set them to roast.

Soon rich fat dripped, hissing as it struck the coals. The smell rose warm and savoury, like browned butter and roasted marrow. All revulsion slipped away before that honest perfume. I spread one—soft as a pat of butter—onto a baked potato and swallowed it. It was good. Fritz, watching me closely, forced his own courage into being and tasted them too; and before we were done our dinner of potatoes had become a feast with an unexpected luxury, as though the island itself had offered us a secret ingredient.

When we set off again we found the direct route choked by a dense thicket, so we turned aside toward the sugar-brake near Cape Disappointment.
