We could not pass it without cutting a handsome bundle of sugar-canes, their joints cool and smooth under the knife, their sweet scent already promising relief to thirsty mouths. The donkey carried the canes along with the bag of wax berries.

At last we reached Calabash Wood and found the sledge. We unloaded the donkey and set everything onto the runners. The patient beast, relieved of the burden on his back, leaned into the traces and drew the load as though it were his natural occupation.

No further adventure befell us. We reached Falconhurst in the evening, and the welcome there warmed us more than any fire. Our stories were listened to with eager attention, our discoveries handled and admired. The pretty green parrot enchanted the boys above all, fluttering and scolding in Fritz’s hands as if already rehearsing the first syllables of a stolen new life.

Supper awaited us—real supper, not mere sustenance: a table of familiar labor turned into comfort. We ate with thankful hearts, and when we climbed into our tree-castle and drew up the ladder, the night sounds of the island—river, leaves, distant surf—seemed like a great protective breathing around our sleep.

If the island had its own music, the next morning our family had one word. Candle. One after another the boys woke with it on their lips. Through breakfast, candles. After breakfast, still candles, until there was no peace to be had except by yielding.

“So be it,” I said. “Let us become chandlers.”

I spoke with confidence, though privately I doubted. We lacked animal fat, which helps candles burn with steady brightness, and I was uncertain how faithfully my memory could reproduce the necessary steps. But I kept my misgivings to myself. We had learned that discouragement, once spoken, behaves like a weed.

Under my direction we began. We stripped the wax berries from their stems and threw them into a large shallow iron vessel over the fire. Heat loosened their secret. The green, sweet-scented wax melted and rose to the surface of the juice. We skimmed it off again and again into a separate pot and kept it warm.

My wife had prepared wicks. I dipped each wick into the liquid wax and passed it to Fritz, who hung it on a bush to cool. The first coating was thin, but we dipped and cooled and dipped again, patient as bees. Slowly the candles gained body and dignity, until they were sturdy enough to be called real.

When our wax ran out we hung the finished candles in a cool shady place to harden. That night, when the sun fell and the forest darkened into its deeper voice, we lit them.

Falconhurst glowed. Not a harsh glare like a city’s brazen lamps, but a civilized, honeyed light with a faint woodland perfume. We sat up three whole hours after sunset, as if we had recovered a lost portion of our former lives. In the quiet, even our speech sounded different—more deliberate, less pressed by darkness.

My wife praised our success; then, with a practical smile that always meant a new campaign, she said she wished I would show her how to make butter. Day after day, she had watched good cream spoil because we had no use for it. Could I invent a plan?

After a moment I answered that I might help—not by my own invention, but by borrowing from the Hottentots. I called for one of our gourd bottles, hollowed and cleaned, with a small hole at one end. I filled it partway with cream and corked it tight.

“Here, boys,” I said, “you can continue this while I turn carpenter and make a cart to replace our sledge.”

They fixed four posts in the ground, fastened a square of sailcloth between them with cords, making a sort of cradle. In that hammock they laid the gourd and, each taking a side, rolled it steadily back and forth for half an hour. The sailcloth creaked softly; the cream sloshed within like distant surf, and the boys’ laughter rose and fell with the rhythm—an island music made by human hands.

At last I called to them to open the gourd and carry the contents to their mother.

They did, and her eyes shone at the sight: a large lump of fresh butter. She touched it as reverently as if it were gold, and when she tasted it, the expression that crossed her face was not merely pleasure but a kind of homecoming. Butter is not only a food; it is a memory you can spread on bread.

Meanwhile, with Fritz’s help, I finished the cart. It was clumsy, but strong enough, and in time it proved of immense use when we began to gather our harvests.

Then we turned to our fruit trees, which had been planted in a plot ready for transplanting. We arranged walnut, cherry, and chestnut in parallel rows to form a shady avenue from Falconhurst to Family Bridge, laying down a tolerable road between them so that we might reach Tentholm even in foul weather. We planted vines around the arched roots of our great mangrove, and set other trees where they would thrive—some near Falconhurst, some beyond Jackal River to adorn Tentholm.

Yet my thoughts about Tentholm were not chiefly of beauty. I wanted it safe—a refuge in emergency. So we planted a thick prickly hedge that could hinder wild animals and even form an obstacle against men, should they appear. We fortified the bridge as well, and mounted two guns on hillocks—guns hauled from the wreck—so that their angry mouths might bark defiance at any enemy, beast or human.

Six weeks slipped away in hard yet pleasant labour. Sundays arrived like cool water. Each one we kept with gratitude, and on that day we thanked God especially for health and safety.

The work strengthened the boys. I encouraged it, making them practice running, leaping, climbing, swimming. Yet it ruined their clothes. They were patched and mended and still grew untidy, disreputable. Necessity drove me back to the wreck. I resolved to replenish our wardrobe and see how much longer the vessel would hold together.

Three of the boys and I went in the pinnace. The old ship looked much as when we left her, though a few more planks had surrendered to sea and wind.

“Come,” I cried, “not an article of the slightest value must be left on board. Rummage her to the very bottom.”

They took me at my word. Sailors’ chests, bales of cloth and linen, two small guns with ball and shot, tables, benches, window shutters, bolts and locks, barrels of pitch—everything we could pry loose or drag free was piled on deck. We loaded the pinnace and carried it ashore. We returned with our tub-boat in tow and made more trips until nothing remained worth taking.

“One more,” I said to my wife before we started again, “and it will be the end of the brave ship that carried us from Switzerland. I have left two barrels of gunpowder on board and mean to blow her up.”

Before lighting the fuse I found a large copper cauldron, too useful to abandon. I lashed it to two empty casks so that when the ship burst it might float clear. Then we placed the powder, laid the train, and returned to shore.

Supper was set outside the tent at a spot from which we could see the wreck. Darkness came. Then—suddenly—a vivid pillar of fire leapt up from the black water, and a sullen roar boomed across the sea. The sound rolled through the bay, answered by echoes off the rocks, and then the night fell strangely quiet again, as if the island were listening after a shout.

Our good old ship was no more.

We had planned it, and knew it was for the best. Yet when we lay down to sleep, sadness pressed us. It felt as though we had destroyed a friend—one that had carried not only bodies but language, tools, habits, and the very names of things we had once taken for granted.

Morning dispelled much of it. The shore was lined with planks and beams, the sea’s repayment. We found the copper cauldron too, successfully floated by its casks. I hauled it up among the rocks and stored under it the powder casks we had landed the day before.

Gathering these valuables took time, and while we worked my wife brought good news: two ducks and a goose had each reared large families among the reeds by the river. Soon they appeared, waddling past with an air of satisfaction, as if pleased with their own domestic triumph. We greeted them with real joy.

Ernest cheered that we would have duck and green peas one day and might imagine ourselves civilized again. Even that jest carried truth: what we longed for, beneath all our courage, was not luxury but the familiar language of meals—dishes that meant seasons, and seasons that meant home.

The sight of those birds reminded me of our life at Falconhurst. I declared we should visit. Everyone wanted to go. As we approached Falconhurst I noticed several young trees in our avenue bent by wind; it resolved me to cut bamboos next day to support them.

Since only Fritz besides myself had visited Cape Disappointment and the surrounding country, my wife and the younger boys begged to accompany me on the next expedition. I consented.

The following morning we set out with the cart, drawn by the cow and the donkey, laden for several days: tent, provisions, ammunition, and a host of implements and utensils. I meant to gather fruits and whatever produce the trees would yield.

The morning was lovely. We passed through our plantations of potatoes, manioc, and cassavas, and came again to the great nests of the sociable grosbeak. The children were charmed by that busy architecture, and even their delighted cries did not feel like noise; they belonged to the place, like birdcall and water.

We reached the wax shrubs and halted. I wished to gather one or two sacks of berries to renew our candle stock. The berries were quickly plucked, their sticky skins perfuming our hands. I stored them among bushes and marked the spot, to collect them on our return.

“Now for the caoutchouc tree,” I said. “Now for waterproof boots and leggings to keep your feet dry, Ernest.”

We sought it out and soon stood at the trunk. Then, with knives and a careful hand, we began to stab the bark and set vessels beneath to catch the milky sap as it bled slowly into our waiting cups.

The next morning the boys and I set out with the cart creaking under a new load of bundled bamboos, the stems knocking together like dry bones whenever the wheels dropped into a rut. I left the buffalo at Falconhurst. The ringed wound in his nostrils had closed cleanly at last, but I would not tempt it to open again; there is no good husbandry in haste, and no good cook ever bruises a tender thing before it has had time to set.

We reached our avenue of young fruit trees at the hour when the island’s day-sounds are still gentle: the thin whistle of waking birds, the busy clicking of insects in the grass, and, under all, the slow hush of wind moving through leaves. That soft soundscape had steadied us often. It was the opposite of the iron clang and shouted orders of a ship at work—noise that forces itself into your skull and leaves nothing of you unshaken. Here, every rustle had a place, and that order did a kind of healing.

We were not a moment too soon. The storm that had merely threatened our planting before had now done its worst. Many saplings lay flat, their roots half-wrenched; others bent like bows, and only a few stood upright, stubborn and lucky. We set to work at once. We lifted each fallen tree, dug deep and wide at the root so it might sit again in loosened earth, then drove stout bamboo props beside it. With broad fibres—tough, flat strips that bit well without cutting—we lashed trunk to stake, pulled the knot tight, and tamped the soil firm with heel and spade until the tree stood with its head properly toward the sun.

“Papa,” said Franz, grave with attention as he fed me fibres one after another, “are these wild or tame trees?”

Jack, whose hands were never idle but whose tongue liked to sprint ahead of them, threw back his head and laughed. “Wild—ferocious!” he cried. “We must tie them up so they don’t run away. And one day we’ll untie them, and they’ll trot after us, and wherever we go they’ll drop fruit into our pockets. We’ll tame them with rings in their noses like the buffalo!”

“That’s not true,” Franz returned, offended not so much by Jack’s teasing as by the injury done to words. “But there are wild and tame trees. Wild grow in the woods, like crab-apples; tame grow in gardens, like our pears and peaches at home. Which are these, papa?”

“They are tame, as you call it,” I told him, tightening a lash around a leaning sapling. “Cultivated—grafted. In Europe, no fruit tree gives its best fruit until it is grafted.”

Franz’s brow tightened in that particular way it did when he met a new term, as if he were trying to fit a strange seed into a known row.

So I explained it, as I would explain a recipe—because grafting is, in its own fashion, a recipe for sweetness. “You take a slip—a twig—from a tree with good fruit. You cut an opening, what gardeners call an eye, in another tree’s branch. You set the slip into that place so the living layers meet, and you bind it firm. Then the stock feeds it, but the slip decides what fruit will come. If we have a sour crab tree, and from another land we have an apple that bears fine ribston pippins, we put a slip of that apple into the crab stock. In a year or two, that branch is heavy with good apples, though its roots belong to the crab.”

“But,” Ernest asked at once, because his mind always followed the chain back to its first link, “where did the first good slips come from, if none grow without grafting?”

“From warmer places,” I said. “In our colder countries, fruit often needs the gardener’s art to coax it into sweetness. But in many southern lands the soil itself is generous. There the most luscious trees are native, and they bear without anyone pruning or protecting them—while in England and Germany, even France, the same kinds demand all a man’s skill just to give a respectable crop.

“When the Romans first went into England, they found little but crab-apples, nuts, and brambles. They improved them by grafting—fine apples where sour ones had been, filberts, raspberries. And in Switzerland, too: our orchards were not born with us; they were brought, and then made ours by care.”

“Were cherries brought too?” Fritz asked. “May we not call cherries Swiss? I always thought they grew nowhere else.”

“I am afraid we cannot claim even their name,” I said. “They are called cherries from Cerasus, a state of Pontus in Asia. A Roman general, Lucullus, brought them to Europe about seventy years before Christ. Hazelnuts also came from Pontus. Walnuts from Persia. Grapes are older still—Noah, you remember, cultivated vines. They have travelled from tongue to tongue and place to place until they belong, now, to almost every civilized land.”

As we spoke, we crossed Jackal River and entered our plantation at Tentholm, where the ground rose a little and the air held a different smell—drier earth, bruised leaf, the faint resin of young pines.

“Do you think all these will grow?” Fritz asked, looking along our line of plantings as though he could already see the shade they would cast years from now. “Here are lemons, pomegranates, pistachio nuts, mulberries.”

“I have little doubt,” I answered. “We are evidently within the tropics. Such trees should flourish here.”

And I named them as we passed—like reading a pantry list and remembering the taste of each. Pines from France, Spain, Italy; olives from Armenia and Palestine; figs from Chios; peaches and apricots from Persia; plums from Damascus; pears from Greece. Each name carried its own sound and history, and as I said them I felt, more sharply than usual, how a thing can be carried across the world and still keep its old meaning—while a word, carried into a new mouth, often changes its flavour. Even here, on this island where no one but ourselves spoke, the old names began to sound strange, like heirlooms taken out of their familiar room.

We finished propping and lashing every tree that needed it, and then, with appetites that would have made a gourmand proud, we returned to Falconhurst.

My wife had set our meal ready—corned beef and palm-cabbage. The scent met us at the ladder: salt and meat, and that green, milky smell of the palm heart, softened by heat. After hours of labour, the first mouthful seemed to wake every sense at once. We ate with the honest greed of those who have earned their food by the swing of arm and the ache of back; and if she looked half-alarmed at our speed, it was only until she saw the colour return to our cheeks.

When the plates were cleared and we lay back to let our bodies turn work into warmth, we spoke of what must come next.

“I wish,” my wife said, looking up at our nest in the great tree, “you would find some other way of climbing. The nest is perfect—I could ask no better home—but that dreadful ladder… Must we scale it every time? Could you not make steps?”

I turned the idea over as I would turn a problem in carpentry or cookery: what we had, what we lacked, what the material itself would permit.

“Outside stairs would be impossible,” I said at last. “But inside the trunk—if the tree is hollow—it might be done. I have wondered more than once whether this trunk is partly empty. Did you not say you saw bees going in and out of a hole?”

“Oh yes,” Franz put in quickly, as if to prove his part in our household history. “And I went close to look and one flew right into my face and stung me. I almost cried—but I didn’t.”

“Brave little fellow,” I told him, and kissed the crown of his head. “If bees have a home inside, the cavity may extend far. Some trees draw their nourishment through the bark and can look flourishing while the heart is hollow—like certain willows at home.”

Jack was already on his feet. Practical curiosity carried him like a gust. The others followed, and soon they were climbing and tapping the bark, peering into the opening, listening for the change in tone that tells you whether wood is sound or empty.

They had forgotten, in their excitement, that the hole was a front door.

The bees reminded them.

An angry buzz burst out like a thrown net. The air filled instantly with living darts. Hands flailed; cries rose; the boys leapt back, swatting at their own hair and collars. The bees swarmed their faces and necks, stung knuckles and ears, and pursued them in a furious cloud as they ran to me for help.

It was hard work to clear the insects and harder still to calm the boys. Jack, first at the hole, suffered worst. His face swelled so quickly it seemed to change shape before our eyes, and the pain made him wince even when he tried to be brave. Cold earth—cool, damp, and soothing—was the only thing that eased him. For an hour or more they could do little but endure the smarting and watch me, their anger toward the bees growing by the minute.

They wanted a battle at once. I wanted a harvest—and a solution.

While the bees still circled, I prepared. I took a large calabash gourd and made, from its smooth round body, a hive. I flattened the lower half so it would sit steady. I cut an arched opening for a doorway, and made a straw roof to keep off rain and sun. In the end it looked almost like a toy house—except that I knew it would soon hold a kingdom of workers, and the sweet store they make from blossoms and time.

Nothing more could be done by daylight. The bees were too angry, and I will not waste courage where patience will do better. When darkness fell and the island’s chorus changed—day birds silent, night insects beginning their thin steady piping—I went with Fritz to the tree. We stopped every hole we could find with wet clay, sealing the trunk so the bees could not pour out at dawn before we were ready.

We rose very early. The air was cool, the forest still half-asleep, and the only loud sound was our own breathing as we worked. I took a hollow cane, pushed one end through the clay into the tree, and with pipe and tobacco smoked as furiously as if I meant to fog the entire island.

Inside the trunk the humming began at once—tremendous, indignant, confused. They could not understand this choking, sour cloud invading their house. I finished one pipe. With my thumb over the cane’s end, I handed it to Fritz to refill. He did so, quick and quiet, and I smoked again.

The roar of buzzing lessened. It sank to a heavy murmur. By the end of the second pipe, all was still. The bees were stupefied.

“Now, Fritz,” I whispered, “hammer and chisel. Stand close.”

Together we cut a small door beside the hole—not removing it entirely, only leaving it attached by one corner, so it could be shut in an instant if needed. I gave one final dose of smoke, and then we opened.

The bees clung in clusters along the inner walls. Carefully, quickly, we lifted them away and placed them into the gourd hive. The work had to be brisk, but gentle; one careless jolt and the whole mass could wake and turn. As soon as the living clusters were transferred, I gathered every atom of wax and honey from their storehouse and packed it into a cask I had ready.

Yet I did not trust that, once revived, they would accept their new home. Bees have a memory of place as stubborn as any farmer. To prevent them returning, I laid tobacco on a board nailed within the trunk, lit it, and let it smoulder so the bitter fumes filled the hollow.

It proved wise. As the bees stirred back into life, they left the gourd and flew straight to the old tree. They found it uninhabitable now—stinking of smoke—and their indignation filled the air with a loud, churning hum. Back and forth they went between gourd and trunk, settling here, lifting again, as if arguing in a language no one can translate. At last, after what looked like due consultation, they accepted the hive and abandoned their former lodging to us.

By evening they were quiet enough for us to open our cask of plunder.

We treated the honey as my wife would treat preserves at home—with care, cleanliness, and no waste. We separated the clear honey from the comb and poured it into jars and pots. The remaining comb we broke into a vessel of water and set it over a slow fire. It melted into a fragrant, golden liquor, steam rising with a warm sweetness that seemed to thicken the air. We poured the whole into a clean canvas bag and pressed it hard. The honey ran out, darker and less delicate than the first, but still excellent. The wax left behind we saved with particular attention; I was already thinking of candles, and of how the night feels when you can keep the dark at a respectful distance.

After such a day, we slept as only those sleep who have earned rest by both danger and labour.

The next morning we returned to the tree’s inner architecture. First we cut away bark at the base and made an opening exactly fit for the door we had brought from the captain’s cabin, hinges and all, ready to hang. Then we began clearing the rotten wood from the centre. It took time—digging, scraping, levering out soft, damp fibres that smelled of old rain and decay. At last the cavity was clean. Standing below, we could look up the trunk like a great smooth funnel and see a circle of sky far overhead.

Now we built the staircase. In the centre we set a stout sapling as an axis, notched it for steps, and cut corresponding notches in the inner wall of the trunk to support the outer ends. The steps we shaped from planks saved from the wreck—wood that had known waves and now would know feet—and we clenched them into place with stout nails.

Upwards we went, step after step, the spiral tightening and widening in rhythm like a shell. As we climbed with our work, we cut windows in the trunk to admit light and air, so the interior would not become a dead, muffled shaft. Sound matters in a dwelling: a space that swallows all noise can make the mind feel trapped, as if your own heartbeat were too loud. Our windows let in wind, birdcall, the soft talk of leaves—domestic music, honest and unbought.

When we reached the top of the centre pole, we fixed another above it and continued the spiral until we reached the level of the nest floor. For safety and ease we added handrails—one coiling around the centre pillar, the other following the curve of the trunk.

The staircase cost us a whole month. By the end of it, we had grown so used to waking with a definite task before us that we began to feel uneasy without some new alteration to plan, as if work were the very rhythm by which we kept time on the island.

But we did not neglect the daily needs of our little colony. The goats and sheep gave us additions—small, frisky creatures that sprang and skittered like living sparks. To keep them from straying, we tied little bells around their necks—bells found in the wreck. Their tinkling became part of our soundscape, a gentle, wandering thread that told us where our wealth grazed. I often thought how different those bells were from the shriek of metal in the old world—here, sound served life instead of selling itself.

Juno, too, had a litter of puppies. The children begged to keep them all, but I would consent to only two; we could not feed a pack. The rest disappeared in that mysterious way by which unwanted puppies and kittens are said to leave the earth. Jack, to console Juno—though I suspect also to spare himself trouble—placed his little jackal beside the remaining pups. To his delight, she accepted it readily, as if motherhood were a language wider than species.

Our other pets flourished and were trained to be useful. The buffalo, after much trouble, became thoroughly domesticated. The wound in his nose healed completely, and the bar we thrust through the hole served as a guide, like a horse’s bit. I began his education with a broad girth of buffalo-hide and tied various articles to it so he would grow used to carrying burdens. By degrees he allowed it without resistance. Soon he carried panniers gladly, taking over loads the ass had borne.

Then I set Master Knips on his back, holding reins I had made, to accustom the buffalo to a rider. Finally Fritz mounted, and the great beast accepted him as if it had always been his purpose to serve.

We did not neglect Fritz’s eagle either. Each day he shot small birds for its food, and sometimes placed them between the buffalo’s horns or on the back of the great bustard, so the eagle would learn to strike prey from unusual perches. These lessons worked. Obeying voice and whistle, the bird soon began to bring down small birds on the wing, stooping and striking with a sportsmanlike precision. We kept it well away from the poultry-yard; art does not erase instinct, and we would not tempt the creature with easy slaughter.

Master Knips was put to work too. Ernest, now that he owned the monkey, wanted him useful, and with Jack’s help he made a small basket of rushes fitted with straps to sit on the monkey’s back. Thus equipped, Knips was taught to climb cocoanut palms and other tall trees and bring down fruit in his hamper.

Jack’s own training efforts went less smoothly. Fangs—the jackal—used his fangs magnificently, but only on his own account. Nothing could persuade him that a catch was not meant to be eaten at once; poor Jack could rarely rescue more than a tattered skin from those greedy jaws. Still, he refused to be discouraged and insisted he would train him in time.

In my smaller hours, I improved our candles. At first our homemade lights had delighted us, but soon we admitted their flaws: the light was imperfect, and the candles themselves unsightly. My wife begged me to find some substitute for the threads of our cotton neck-ties, which I had used as wicks.

To give the candles proper shape and smoothness, I turned to the bamboo moulds I had prepared. At first I thought to pour wax in and slide the candle out when cool, but I quickly found it would not work. So I split the moulds lengthways, greased them, bound the halves tightly together, and poured the melted wax inside; when cooled, the mould could be opened without injuring the candle.

Wicks were the harder question. My wife flatly refused to sacrifice ties and handkerchiefs. I cut long slips from a piece of inflammable wood from an Antilles tree and fixed these splints in the centres of the moulds. My wife prepared rival wicks from the fibres of the karata tree, confident they would surpass mine.

We tested them like judges at a fair. Over a large fire we melted a mixture—half beeswax, half wax from candleberries—its scent warm and faintly resinous, like summer trapped in fat. The moulds stood on end in a tub of cold water, ready. Some held karata fibre; some my wooden splints. We filled them, cooled them, opened them, and held the finished candles up for inspection.

At night we lit them and watched their flames. The verdict was unanimous: neither matched the old cotton wick. But even my wife admitted my wooden splints were better than her karata fibres. Mine flared a little yet burned bright and steady; hers gave a weak, flickering flame, almost useless.

After that I turned shoemaker, determined at last to make waterproof boots. I filled a pair of socks with sand and coated them with a thin layer of clay to form moulds. The sun hardened them. Then I brushed on layer after layer of caoutchouc, letting each dry before applying the next, until the thickness pleased me. When dry, I broke out the clay, nailed buffalo-hide soles on, brushed those too with caoutchouc, and ended with comfortable, durable, respectable-looking waterproof boots.

I was so delighted that orders poured in at once. Before long every member of the family had a pair, and our steps no longer feared wet ground.

One great inconvenience at Falconhurst remained: no spring close by. The boys had to fetch water daily from the stream, and the labour wasted time and strength. So we devised a plan to carry water by pipes. Upstream, we threw a dam across the river to raise the water enough to run down to our dwelling. From the reservoir we led it through pipes into the turtle-shell trough placed near the house; the superfluous water flowed away through the hole Fritz’s harpoon had once made.

This was a blessing. We inaugurated the trough in proper domestic fashion by washing a whole sack of potatoes in it—the water running over the rough skins, carrying away grit and earth, leaving them clean and pale as if newly dug. Work, food, and home—each day we braided them together. We had little time to be idle or to mourn our separation from the world.

One morning, as we were finishing the spiral staircase and giving it as much neatness as our hands could manage, a sound tore through the forest—so loud, so sudden, so unlike the ordinary voices of our island that it seemed to strike the very air into pieces. It was a roaring, bellowing noise, the cry of some beast. The acoustic peace we relied on was shattered; even the birds fell silent, as if the whole place listened.

Jack swore it must be a lion. Fritz guessed some great ape. Ernest, with his bookish knowledge, suggested a hyena—and I thought he might be right.

“Whatever it is,” I said, “we must be ready. Up to the nest—quick—while I secure the door.”

We fitted the dogs with their collars and sent them out to protect the animals below. Then I shut the door and joined my family above. Every gun was loaded. Every face was turned toward the openings. The sound came nearer… then stopped. Nothing moved. No shadow crossed the grass. Only the uneasy quiet remained, the kind that makes your own breathing sound like an intrusion.

I decided to descend and reconnoitre. Fritz came with me. We crept down and moved among the trees, guns at full cock, stepping as softly as we could on leaf and root. We pressed on further, listening, eyes searching.

Suddenly, close by, the terrific sound burst out again.

Fritz lifted his gun—and as quickly dropped it, breaking into laughter so sudden and full that it startled me more than the roar had done.

There was no mistaking those dulcet tones: hee-haw, hee-haw, hee-haw, echoing through the wood.

Our ass came into view, braying his approach with hearty enthusiasm. But he was not alone. Behind him trotted another animal—an ass as well, yet slim and graceful as a horse, her neck arched, her movements quick with wildness. We watched, wary now for a different reason.

“Fritz,” I whispered, “that is an onager. Creep back to Falconhurst and bring me a piece of cord—quietly.”

While he was gone, I cut a bamboo and split it halfway down to make pincers, knowing I might need to seize the animal’s nose without risking my hands. Fritz returned swiftly, and I was pleased to see he had brought not only cord but oats and salt as well—good bait and better sense.

We tied one end of the cord to a tree and made a running noose at the other. Then we waited. The two animals approached, browsing calmly. Fritz rose with the noose in one hand, oats and salt in the other.

Our old ass, recognising his favourite food, came forward without fear and began munching contentedly. The stranger started back at the sight of Fritz, but seeing her companion unalarmed, she took courage. She came nearer, sniffing, nostrils flaring, and reached for the offered grains.

In an instant the noose flew from Fritz’s skilful hand and dropped over her neck. With one bound she sprang backward to the full length of the cord; the noose tightened and she fell, half strangled. I ran in at once, loosened the rope, replaced it with a halter, and set the bamboo pincers upon her nose. With two cords fastened between two trees, we secured her and left her a moment to recover.

Everyone came running to admire the animal as she rose. She threw fiery glances around, lashed out with her heels, snorted and struggled, her sleek flanks quivering with rage and fear. But the cords held firm. After a time she stood, exhausted, trembling, yet upright—wild pride still in her posture.

I approached carefully. At last she allowed me to lead her to the roots of our great tree, which for now served as our stable. There I tied her close beside the donkey—who, in his own simple way, had brought us this new prize—and secured him too, to prevent any further adventurous wanderings.

Spring did not arrive on our island with ceremony. It came first as a hush: the wind that had worried our roof-branches fell still, the clouds tore open like wet cloth, and sunlight poured through in broad, honest beams. The rain stopped as if someone had shut a door. We stepped out of our winter refuge blinking, half-dazed, as though released from captivity; and what restored us most quickly was not only the green—new, tender verdure everywhere—but the sound. After months of drumming water and groaning trees, the world had changed its music. The air was full of small bright notes: birds calling in layers, one answer stitching to another, until the whole valley became a living chorale. Even the dogs paused, ears pricked, tasting the new season with their hearing.

It was impossible not to feel how much our minds depended on that gentler soundscape. The storm had been a kind of tyranny, loud enough to flatten thought; spring, by contrast, let each modest noise find its proper place—the flick of a leaf, the click of a beak, the soft scrape of our boots in damp sand. I wondered, as I listened, how people in crowded cities endured the ceaseless metallic clatter of wheels and hammers, and how quickly such noise must drive out the older, quieter memory of how a morning ought to sound.

Our plantations had taken heart. The seed we had put into the moist earth was already pushing up, green points pricking through the dark soil. Nature looked washed and newly varnished. But our own nest—our poor winter lodging—was the first thing that demanded attention. The wind had worried it, the rain had filled it with leaves, and the whole place looked like a bird’s home after a quarrel. We repaired it with haste, and within a few days it was again fit to shelter us.

Then my wife, who never let the needs of the table and the household drift far behind any adventure, begged that I would set her to work with the flax without delay. She spoke of linen as other people speak of treasure. Cloth, for her, was not merely a covering; it was comfort preserved, labour remembered, a kind of domestic history you could fold and store.

So I built a drying-oven as early as possible, and prepared it for her use. With more trouble than I expected, I manufactured a beetle-reel and a spinning-wheel. Soon she and Franz were absorbed in the sweet, steady labour—she drawing out the fibres with patient fingers, turning them into thread, and Franz, proud of his importance, reeling off the slender line as it came. The soft whirr of the wheel joined the spring birdsong, a homely counter-melody that made our camp feel less like a temporary accident and more like a place where years might gather.

Yet my mind kept turning toward Tentholm. We had stores there—our hard-won riches from the wreck—and the winter storms had been too violent for comfort. I feared damage. Fritz and I therefore set out to examine our outpost.

What we found made Falconhurst look almost untouched by comparison. The tent was blown flat, its canvas torn into rags; provisions were soaked through; and worst of all, two casks of powder were ruined beyond recovery. We spread everything we still hoped to save out in the sun, laying cloth, rope, and whatever would dry on stones and sand. The pinnace, thank Heaven, was safe; but our faithful tub-boat lay dashed to pieces like a broken shell. The sight of that wrecked little craft, which had served us so loyally, struck me harder than I expected. It made plain what we could not afford: another winter in fragile quarters.

On the way home Fritz proposed a bold remedy. We should hollow a cave in the rock, he said—a fortress for the rainy season. The difficulties seemed nearly insurmountable, and yet the thought took hold of me at once. If we could not hew a room, we might at least create a cellar: a cool, safe place for what was most precious and perishable.

A few days later we left Falconhurst with the cart loaded—spades, hammers, chisels, pickaxes, crowbars—our whole noisy arsenal of labour. On the smooth perpendicular face of the rock I drew, with chalk, the dimensions of the entrance we intended, and we began to batter away.

Six days of hard, incessant toil made scarcely any impression. I think Master Knips himself could hardly have squeezed into the hole we had gained. But we would not surrender. By the tenth day, as our blows kept time—iron on stone, stone answering with dull protest—we were rewarded. The rock changed. What had been stubborn became more yielding; the tool bit more deeply, and hope returned with every shower of grit.

It was then that Jack, who had been working with a hammer and crowbar, suddenly shouted with such triumph that the sound rang off the cliff like a bell.

“Gone, father! Fritz—my bar has gone through the mountain!”

“Run round and fetch it,” Fritz called, laughing. “Perhaps it’s dropped into Europe. Don’t lose a good crowbar.”

But Jack was not jesting; his eyes were bright with certainty. “It is through! I heard it crash down inside. Come—do come and see!”

We sprang to him. I thrust the handle of my hammer into the opening. It met no resistance. I could turn it as if in empty air. Fritz handed me a long pole; I tried the depth and felt nothing. A thin wall, then—only a thin wall between us and a vast hollow.

Joy made the boys’ arms redouble their strength. They battered, and piece by piece the rock gave way until the hole would admit us. I stepped near the aperture to look within—and instantly a rush of foul air struck my face. The world swam; my stomach lurched; I clutched the rock and cried out for the boys to stand back.

When I had recovered, I explained the danger. A cavern long sealed holds air that has been stagnant—vitiated—deadly to breathe. “Unless air is renewed,” I told them, “it becomes a trap. Fire may help awaken it.” We threw handfuls of blazing hay into the opening, hoping flame would purify and stir the air; but each bundle went out at once, snuffed as if pressed between wet palms.

I saw we needed something more forceful. I sent them to fetch a chest of signal-rockets from the wreck. Soon we were firing them—dozens of fiery serpents hissing into the dark. They whizzed and vanished at what seemed an immense distance. Some curved and spun, casting sudden light across a roof that glittered like a magician’s hall. In those brief illuminations the cavern looked alive with jewels, and the rockets themselves seemed like bright, wrathful dragons driving a malignant spirit from a beautiful palace.

We waited a little. Then I threw in lighted hay again. This time it burned clearly. The cave, so long sealed, could be entered.

While Fritz and I enlarged the opening, Jack sprang on his buffalo and thundered away toward Falconhurst to carry the astonishing news. The bridge timbers soon resounded again beneath Storm’s heavy gallop, and before long the rest of our family came up in the cart, faces eager, voices tumbling over one another.

Jack had packed every candle he could find. We lit them, took our arms—for prudence, even in wonder—and entered. I led, sounding the ground with a long pole, unwilling that a hidden chasm should swallow any of us in our first steps.

Silently we advanced. Even the dogs moved as if they feared to disturb something sacred. The cave widened around us into a world of glimmering crystal. The candles threw gold onto the walls; points of light multiplied and trembled like stars seen through water. Great pillars rose from the sandy floor like trees in a petrified forest; high above, branches turned to stone drooped in stalactites, each one catching flame-colour—red, green, violet—so that the air itself seemed strewn with rainbows.

The floor was hard, dry sand—so dry that I knew at once we might live here without dread of damp. I broke off a fragment from the glittering wall and put it to my tongue. The taste was unmistakable.

Rock-salt.

An unlimited supply of pure salt—no longer a rationed treasure but a gift spread before us like a tablecloth. My wife’s eyes shone at that discovery, for she understood immediately what it meant: meat preserved, fish cured, winter made less hungry; and with salt, even the simplest broth becomes more than warm water—it becomes comfort with a name.

Only one thing troubled my delight. Here and there lay large crystals that had fallen from the roof. If more should drop, the palace might become a death-trap. I examined the fallen masses and saw that the breaks were fresh. The rockets’ concussion, I concluded, had shaken them loose. To make sure nothing else hung ready to fall, we discharged our guns from the entrance and watched.

Nothing more came down. The roof held firm. Our magnificent shelter was safe.

We returned to Falconhurst full of plans. Now nothing was discussed but the new house—how it should be arranged, how we should live in it, what work might be done there, what stores kept there. Falconhurst, which had seemed so secure at first, shrank in our imagination. It would serve, we decided, as a summer residence; the cave should be our winter house and, if need arose, our impregnable castle.

Work began at once. We needed light and air, so we hewed a row of windows in the rock and fitted into them the window-cases we had saved from the officers’ cabins. We brought the door from Falconhurst and fixed it into the entrance. The opening in the trunk of the tree, by which we had first approached the rock face, I resolved to conceal with bark, so that wild beasts—or worse, human strangers—might be less likely to notice the place in our absence.

Inside, we divided the cave into four parts. In front was a large compartment where the door opened; this we subdivided into sitting, eating, and sleeping spaces. On the right we set our kitchen and workshop; on the left, the stables. Farther back, in the darker recesses, we placed our storehouse and powder magazine. Having endured one rainy season already, we planned with the memory of discomfort fresh upon our skin. We would not be smoke-dried again. We built a proper fireplace and chimney. We arranged the stable more sensibly. We left ample space in the workshop for the kind of labour that makes a household independent.

It was during our frequent journeys to Tentholm that we discovered unforeseen advantages. The shore there became a kind of generous market, arriving without coin and without advertisement. Great turtles came ashore to lay their eggs in the sand, and their rich flesh gave us many a sumptuous meal. When more than one appeared, we cut off their retreat to the sea and turned them on their backs, fastening them to a stake near the water’s edge with a cord through a hole in the shell. Kept thus, they remained in good condition for weeks, so we had fresh turtle whenever we wished. Lobsters, crabs, and mussels abounded as well, their shells clicking softly in the shallows like castanets when the tide moved them.

Then one morning, as we approached Tentholm, the sea itself performed a spectacle. Far out, the surface heaved and boiled as if something vast were stirring beneath. The morning sun struck the disturbed water, and flashes leapt across it like fire. Above the shimmering patch hovered hundreds of birds, screaming, plunging, rising again in harsh, frantic chorus. The whole scene was a moving storm of sound and light—a wild orchestra chasing its own feast.

The shining mass rolled on toward our bay, the birds following as if tethered by hunger. We hurried down.

As we neared, the truth shaped itself clearly. “A shoal of herrings,” I said.

At once questions flew at me. What made such a bank? Why did it move so? How could so many fish be gathered into one living body?

I explained as best I could: that a herring-bank is an immense multitude swimming together, extending over a great area and many fathoms deep; that ravenous fish follow and devour them from below, while birds strike from above; and that, pressed between enemies, the shoal makes for shallow water where the great fish cannot follow. “But there,” I told them, “they meet a third enemy—man. They may escape tooth and talon, but ingenuity is harder to flee. And yet, for all the millions caught, the roe of only a small number could replenish the sea again.”

Our fishery began at once. Jack and Fritz waded in with baskets and baled out fish as one bales water, hurling them up to us on shore. We worked with the brisk rhythm of necessity: gutting, rinsing, packing. And there, in the midst of the noise—birds still screaming, surf murmuring—we made our own domestic music: knives tapping on stone, salt pouring like dry rain, barrel staves creaking as we pressed the layers down.

We salted them properly: first a layer of salt, then herrings, then salt again, until the casks were full. The sharp clean scent of the salt rose into our faces; the fish gave off their cold sea-smell, bright and metallic. We closed the barrels carefully and rolled them into the cool vault at the back of our cave.

Good fortune did not stop there. A day after the herring shoal left, seals appeared, drawn by the refuse we had thrown into the sea. I doubted whether their flesh would suit our table, but we secured a score or two for their skins and fat. We drew off the skins with care for harness and clothing; we boiled down the fat for oil, which we stored in casks for tanning, soap-making, and lamps. Thus one harvest fed another: fish gave us seals, and seals promised light when the long evenings returned.

These labours delayed our work at Rock House for a time, but as soon as we could we returned to it with renewed vigour. I had noticed that beneath the salt crystals lay a kind of gypsum, which I knew could serve us as plaster. I broke off pieces, heated them strongly, reduced them to powder, and mixed it with water. The plaster came out smooth and white. With no better use at the moment, I plastered over some of the herring casks to make doubly sure that air was excluded; the remainder I left unsealed, intending to preserve their contents by smoking.

For the smoking we built a small hut of reeds and branches. We strung the herrings on lines across the roof, and on the floor we lit a great fire of brushwood and moss. Dense smoke rose and curled around the fish, wrapping them in a fragrant fog. Over several days the flesh darkened and tightened; when we handled it, it felt firm and well-cured, the smell rich and savoury, promising meals that would taste of summer even in bad weather.

About a month after the herrings, other shoals visited us. Jack saw them first and shouted that young whales were off the coast. We ran down and found the bay alive—not with whales, but with great fish: sturgeon, salmon, trout, all making for the mouth of Jackal River to ascend it and deposit their spawn among the stones. The river itself seemed to hum with their movement, water thick with muscular backs and quick fins. The birds’ cries returned, the surf kept its steady bass, and beneath it all the fish made a hidden rushing sound like wind through reeds.

Jack was in raptures. “Here are proper fish!” he cried. “None of your paltry fry. How do you preserve these—potted, salted, or smoked?”

“Not so fast,” I said. “First tell me how to catch them, and then I will tell you how to cook them.”

“Oh, I’ll catch them fast enough,” he answered, and darted off to Rock House.

He returned with his fishing apparatus: a bow and arrow and a ball of twine. He had fastened a barbed spike to the arrow-head and secured the arrow to the string. Armed with this, he crept to the river’s edge and took aim.

The arrow flew—and struck one of the largest fish in the side.

“Help, father, help!” Jack shouted at once, as the fish surged away, dragging arrow and line. “Help! Or he’ll pull me in!”

I ran to him. Together we held the cord, bracing our feet in the sand as the creature fought. The fish lashed water into foam, tugging with astonishing strength, but we did not let go. Slowly its struggle weakened. At last, exhausted and bleeding, it allowed us to draw it ashore.

It was a noble prize. Fritz and Ernest arrived just as we finished and looked at Jack with envy sharp enough to season a stew. Not to be outdone, they rushed off for weapons. Soon we were all engaged—Fritz with a harpoon, Ernest with rod and line, and I, like some improvised Neptune, with an iron trident that was in truth nothing more heroic than a pitchfork. Monster after monster came to land until the shore was strewn with shining bodies, their scales catching sunlight like coins.

Then Fritz harpooned a sturgeon eight feet long—so heavy we could not drag it ashore. We pulled together, straining until our arms shook, but the beast held stubbornly in the shallows.

“The buffalo!” my wife proposed at once—practical as ever, her mind always finding the hinge that would move the door.

Jack ran for Storm. We harnessed the buffalo to the harpoon rope, and soon the monstrous fish lay panting on the sand.

When we had taken as many fish as we could possibly use, we set about cleaning and preparing them. Here my wife became the true commander of the day. Knife-work, washing, sorting—each task had its order, its economy. Some fish we salted; some we dried as we had dried the herrings; some we treated like Mediterranean tunny, preserving them in oil, sealing flavour and richness into casks like a promise.

From the sturgeon roe I decided to make caviare. I removed the membranes, washed the roe in vinegar, salted it, pressed out the moisture drawn by the salt, and packed it into small barrels for our storehouse. In that moment the cave felt less like a mere shelter and more like an archive of tastes—foods carrying names from far countries, lodged now in our island life. I thought how easily such names disappear when no one speaks them, how a dish can vanish from memory simply because the word for it no longer passes from mouth to mouth. Here, at least, we kept the word alive with the work of our hands.

I also knew that the sturgeon’s bladder yields the best isinglass. Carefully collecting the air-bladders, I washed them and hung them to stiffen. Then I peeled off the outer membrane, cut the rest into strips—staples—placed them in an iron pot over the fire, and boiled them to the proper consistency. I strained the glue through clean cloth and spread it thin on a stone slab to dry. The transparent substance that resulted promised to serve as a substitute for glass in our window-frames—another small victory of necessity over lack.

In this climate, our kitchen garden demanded scarcely any attention. Seeds sprang up and flourished as if time itself had become generous. Peas, beans, wheat, barley, rye, and Indian corn seemed always on the verge of ripening; cucumbers, melons, and other vegetables grew luxuriantly. The success of our patch at Tentholm encouraged me to hope my experiment at Falconhurst had prospered too, and one morning we went to see.

As we passed the field from which we had dug the potatoes, we found it covered with barley, wheat, rye, and peas in profusion. I stopped, astonished.

“Where has this fine crop sprung from?” I asked.

“From the earth,” my wife replied, laughing—yet with the quiet pride of one whose labour often appears as if by magic because it is done without fuss. “Franz and I sowed the seed I brought from the wreck. The ground was already tilled by you and the boys. All we did was scatter.”

Delighted, I hurried to the maize plantation. It had grown well—and it was not unappreciated. A tremendous flock of feathered thieves rose as we approached. Among them Fritz saw a few ruffed grouse. Quick as thought he unhooded his eagle, sent it in chase, and sprang onto his onager to follow at a gallop. The noble bird marked the finest grouse, soared above it, and then stooped, striking it to the ground.

Fritz was close enough to spring through the bushes and save the grouse from death. He hooded the eagle again and returned triumphantly, prey in hand.

Jack, meanwhile, had not been idle. He slipped his pet Fangs among the quails still feeding in the field; and to my surprise the jackal secured a dozen, bringing them faithfully to his master’s feet.

We turned toward Falconhurst, hungry with honest labour. There my wife refreshed us with a drink so delicious and strange it deserves remembrance: she crushed the stems of young Indian corn, strained out the sweet green juice, and mixed it with water and the juice of sugar-cane. It tasted of spring itself—fresh, grassy sweetness, with a deeper honeyed note from the cane. While we drank, the world outside hummed gently, and the birds continued their bright argument in the trees.

The next day we prepared for an excursion. I wished to establish a sort of semi-civilized farm at some distance from Falconhurst, where we might place animals that had become too numerous for our limited supply of fodder. In the large cart—harnessed to the buffalo, the cow, and the ass—we loaded a dozen fowls, four young pigs, two couple of sheep, as many goats, and also a pair of hens with one cock grouse. Fritz led on his onager, and we forced a new track through woods and tall grasses toward Cape Disappointment.

At last we emerged onto a large plain covered with curious little bushes. Their branches and the ground around them were strewn with pure white flakes.

“Snow! Snow!” cried Franz. “Oh, mother, come down and play snowballs! This is jolly—much better than the ugly rain!”

His mistake was understandable; the flakes did resemble snow. Before I could speak, Fritz declared it must be a kind of dwarf cotton-tree. We approached and found he was right. Soft fine wool lay in pods still clinging to the bushes and scattered everywhere on the ground. We had discovered cotton. My wife’s delight was like a sudden warmth—another domestic future made possible. She gathered a great quantity into three capacious bags, and we resumed our journey.

Crossing the cotton-field, we climbed a pretty wooded hill. From its summit the view opened magnificently: luxuriant grass ran down the slope, dotted with shady trees; a sparkling brook gushed and sang as it descended; below lay rich green forest, and beyond it the sea, steady and shining. The place had everything—pasture, water, shade, shelter. It seemed made for our new farm, as if some careful elder had chosen it long ago and left it waiting.

We pitched our tent and built our fireplace. My wife set about preparing our repast—her hands always turning what the island offered into something that tasted like home—while Fritz and I chose a site for a shed. We found a group of trees so placed that their trunks could serve as posts. We carried tools there, then returned to supper and afterward lay down on beds my wife had prepared with the newly gathered cotton—softness after months of rough straw and damp leaves, as if the island itself had finally learned our needs.

The trees formed, by happy chance, a regular rectilinear figure, with the longest side facing the sea. I cut deep mortices in the trunks about ten feet from the ground, and again ten feet higher to form a second storey. Into these I set beams, creating the framework. For a roof I laid laths and overlaid them with bark stripped from a neighbouring tree, fastening it with acacia thorns. It would shed rain like a well-made cloak.

As I cleared scraps of bark and rubbish for fuel, a peculiar smell rose—clean, resinous, and bright, as though the air itself had been seasoned. I stooped and picked up pieces. To my surprise, some were from the terebinth tree, and the rest from the American fir. The goats, snuffling and routing through the same heap, made an important discovery of their own: pieces of cinnamon, fragrant and warm, a spice that seemed almost too civilised for our wild life.

I showed the boys what we had found. “From the fir,” I said, “we may obtain turpentine and tar. So we can prepare pitch for our yacht with tar and oil—and cart-grease with tar and fat. As for the terebinth, a gum issues from incisions in its bark, hardens in the sun, and becomes as transparent as amber. Burned, it gives a most delicious perfume; dissolved in spirits of wine, it forms a clear varnish.”

Thus, at the end of a day of building and finding, of hauling beasts and gathering cotton, the island offered us not only shelter and food, but scent—cinnamon on our fingers, resin in the air—like a reminder that even in exile, a household can keep its old arts alive: to cure, to preserve, to season; to listen for the right sounds; and to give names, carefully, to what sustains us.

Morning broke clear and brisk, and we were up with the first chorus of the island—reef-birds calling across the cliff, the river talking steadily below, and, in the nearer trees, a soft, busy murmur as the pigeons dropped in to feed. After breakfast—still warm breadfruit cakes and a little goat’s milk—there was no loitering. We set ourselves to the bird-lime.

The mixture, worked the evening before, had taken the right temper: caoutchouc oil for body, turpentine for bite, beaten and warmed until it clung with that stubborn, elastic greed that promises success. The boys brought slender rods, and I smeared each one carefully, turning it as I would turn meat before a flame, so the coating lay even and smooth. Then I sent them up among the upper branches where fruit was thickest and the birds gathered as if the trees themselves were granaries.

The multitudes astonished me—far beyond last year’s straggling remnant. Now whole boughs dipped under the weight of feeding bodies; the air was alive with wings and the low, rolling speech of pigeons. Nearby, the evergreen oaks were dropping their sweet acorns, and the ground beneath was a hushed litter of shells and husks—proof enough that at night the birds roosted overhead in great companies. Seeing it, I resolved on a torchlight raid such as the colonists of Virginia practise: light to dazzle, sticks to fell, sacks to gather.

Meanwhile the lime did its work. A pigeon would alight, confident—then the sudden frantic flutter, the feet glued fast, the wings snagging the rod as it struggled. The more it fought, the more the tenacious paste climbed over feather and claw, until, with a piteous cry, bird and stick together tumbled to the ground. Fresh lime, reset the snare, again and again. The boys learned quickly; soon their hands moved with the steady rhythm of a kitchen task—set, wait, lift, clean, smear, return—so I left them to it and went to prepare our torches.

Pinewood split fine and straight, dipped in turpentine, bound firm: torches that would flare bright and sharp, hissing like fat in a hot pan when the resin caught. While I worked, Jack came up cradling a pigeon unlike the rest, its look too familiar, too homely for these wild flocks. He could not bear to kill it. At once I saw it—one of our own European breed, the very kind we meant to keep and multiply. I took the trembling thing, and with oil and a little ashes, rubbed away the sticky filth from feet and wings, gentle as if I were cleaning a child’s hands after berry-picking. We put it into a wicker cage, and I told Jack to bring any others like it.

He did, and we secured several pairs. I felt a plain domestic triumph in that—less the thought of meat than of continuity: a cot of our own, familiar birds in a strange land, and the promise (when numbers allowed) of pigeon-pie, rich and peppered, with a crust that crackles under the knife.

By evening we set out for the oaks, carrying only long bamboo canes, our torches, and canvas sacks. The children had looked at our equipment as if we went to war with kitchen implements; but the island teaches the use of simple things. Darkness fell almost at once in the wood. I struck fire; the torches took with a rush, and their light leapt up into the branches.

Every bough was thick with roosting life—ortolans and wild pigeons, pressed close and drowsy. The sudden glare broke their peace like a cymbal crash in a quiet room. Confusion ran through the canopy: wings beating blind against leaves, bodies dropping in fright. Some fell before we even lifted a cane, and we scooped them into the sacks. Then we began to beat the branches—quick, controlled blows that shook the birds down in showers. My wife and Franz could scarcely gather fast enough. The sacks grew heavy, their canvas sides rounding with warm, feathered weight.

We turned home. At Falconhurst we secured the booty and, tired to the bone, withdrew to rest with the night sounds settling again into their natural order.

The next day belonged entirely to the harvest. There was no idle hour—plucking until the air was full of drifting down, boiling to loosen feathers and sweeten tough flesh, roasting for those we would eat at once, stewing the rest with herbs and a handful of crushed sweet acorns for a faint nutty depth. The kitchen smelled of hot fat and smoke, and under it all the clean, iron scent of blood and broth. Even the work had its music: knives tapping boards, pots bubbling, the crackle of firewood, and, beyond our walls, the sea’s long breathing.

But the morning after, we arranged a greater expedition—to Woodlands—so that the monkey invasion might not be repeated. I hoped, if we could catch the mischievous rascals in the act of ruin, to chastise them so thoroughly they would shun our farm thereafter.

My wife packed us provisions enough for several days, and stayed behind at home with Franz and Turk. I took stronger bird-lime than we had used for pigeons, short posts, plenty of string, and a supply of cocoanut shells and gourds. The buffalo carried the load and one or two of the boys besides. I rode the ass.

Near Woodlands, in a spot well screened by thickets, we made a small encampment—pitched the tent, tethered the animals. The dogs were tied up too; their very eagerness could betray us. The cottage itself stood quiet, deserted, as if the forest held its breath.

We set about our trap. Lightly driven stakes formed an irregular paling around the house; string was wound in and out among them, crossing and recrossing until the space became a labyrinth. No creature could pass without touching stake or cord. Everything—wood, twine, even the bowls—was smeared thick with our adhesive paste. Basins of bird-lime stood about, baited with rice, maize, and other tempting morsels.

Night came without interruption. We lay down under the tent, listening. In such waiting, one notices sound as one notices seasoning—too much ruins the mind, too little leaves it anxious. Here the island gave us a clean silence, broken only by leaf-stir, insect strum, and the distant, steady rush of water. I thought of the world we had left behind—its clang and hammer, its wheels and whistles—and felt, as I often did, how noise can tyrannize a spirit until it forgets the shape of peace.

At earliest dawn, the peace shattered.

A confused rustle and crackle swelled through the forest—creaking boughs, frantic chatter, shrieks that seemed to scrape the air. We armed ourselves with stout clubs and cudgels, held the dogs straining at the leash, and slipped behind the thickets where, unseen, we could command a view.

Out poured the troop—an unruly flood of monkeys, scrambling and springing from tree to tree, racing and tumbling into the open towards the house. They were drawn at once to the bowls and jars. They seemed innumerable, their bodies flashing between shadow and sun, too quick and confused to count.

Some dashed straight through the palings, some scrambled up to the roof and began tugging at pegs to force entrance. But as they swarmed over the place, each in turn touched our smeared cords and stakes—head, paws, back, breast—until the bird-lime marked them like a curse. The predicament deepened with every frantic motion.

Some sat and tried to clean themselves with absurd, earnest gestures, only to spread the paste further. Others became entangled in stake and cord, dragging the whole contrivance behind them, the very picture of bewildered despair. Some tried to help one another and stuck together; the more they pulled and kicked, the worse their plight became.

Many seized the gourds and cocoanut shells baited with grain; their paws were caught, and the shells clattered as they bolted—an ugly, hollow percussion, like pots kicked through a pantry in the dark. Most ridiculous—and, in its way, most pitiable—was an old fellow who found a calabash of palm wine. He drank greedily, and the shell stuck to his forehead and whiskers like a mask, blinding him. He staggered and capered wildly, striking at empty air, the troop shrieking and scattering around him.

Then panic spread. Some fled toward the trees—only to find bird-lime waiting there too. They hung from branches, fixed fast, struggling and shrieking, their cries cutting through the clean morning like a saw.

At that point I loosed the three dogs, whose impatience had been almost uncontrollable. They rushed in as if burning with zeal to execute justice. We were forced to do our part too—clubs rising and falling—until the din of howling, yelping, barking, and screaming in every tone of rage and pain collapsed into an awful silence.

When it was over, the place looked like a battlefield. At least forty apes lay mangled and dead. The boys grew sad and downhearted, and I shared the heaviness with them; necessity is a hard master, and even just punishment leaves a bitter taste. I turned them at once to active work: we removed and buried the slain, and then burned the stakes, cordage, bowls—everything connected with our deadly stratagem—so that the very instruments of that hour would not linger to sour our minds.

Afterward we restored order to the dismantled cottage, and went seeking the scattered sheep, goats, and poultry. By degrees we gathered them, quieted them, and hoped to settle them again in their yards and sheds.

While we worked, we heard repeatedly the heavy thud of something dropping from a tree. We looked and found three splendid birds caught on limed sticks we had placed loosely among the branches. Two were a variety of Blue Molucca pigeon; the third, with resplendent green, bronze, and steely-blue plumage, I took to be the Nicobar pigeon. At once my mind leapt to domestic possibilities: not merely their beauty, but their future as breeding stock, as living wealth, as new tenants near our cave.

“First tenants, father!” Fritz said. “Do you expect to catch more like these?”

“Not exactly catch them,” I replied. “I mean to practise a secret art. Much can be done by magic, Fritz!”

I gave no more explanation.

In a few days Woodlands was set right again—comfortable, ordered—and we returned without further adventure to Falconhurst, where we were welcomed as if we had come back from a campaign.

All agreed we must go at once to Tentholm and make the pigeon-house in the rock. Other matters required attention there too, so we arranged for a prolonged stay.

My plan was to hollow out an ample space in the cliff facing Jackal River, close to our rocky home, then fit it with partitions, perches, nesting-places, and a broad platform before the openings. A wooden front would close it, with entrance-holes and sliding shutters.

After weeks of labour we judged it ready. I set the other children to work at some distance, then summoned Fritz.

“Now, my faithful assistant,” I said, “it is time to conjure the new colonists to their settlement here.” He stared, and I laughed. “A pigeon-dealer’s trick, nothing more. We shall compound a food from soft clay, aniseed, and salt—something our birds will relish. The scent will bring others to share it.”

“I can get those,” Fritz said at once.

“I shall want oil of aniseed besides,” I told him, “to rub about the pigeon-holes, so that the birds brush it as they pass in and out, and their feathers carry the perfume to the wild ones. We’ll make it by pounding aniseed. Bring me the mortar and some oil.”

When the oil was strongly impregnated, I strained it through cloth, pressing it hard. The scent rose sharp and sweet—like a spice chest opened in a quiet room—and I knew it would cling for days. We smeared the entrances, prepared the clay mixture, and installed our pigeons in the new residence, closing the slides.

By then, our European birds were friendly with the three beautiful strangers. When the other boys came home and scrambled up the ladder to peep through a little pane of glass I had fixed, they saw them contentedly picking grain and pecking at the “magic food,” as Fritz called it—though he kept my “secret arts” to himself.

Early on the third morning I roused Fritz and had him ascend the rope ladder, arrange a cord on the sliding door so it could be opened or shut from below, and pour fresh aniseed oil about the entrance. Then we woke the rest of the family and told them, if they made haste, they might see me let the pigeons fly.

Everyone gathered before the dove-cot as if for a festival. I waved a wand with mock solemnity, muttered a ridiculous incantation, and gave Fritz a sign. He drew up the sliding panel.

Pretty heads appeared. Soft eyes blinked into the morning. They withdrew, returned, ventured out upon the platform—“the verandah,” little Franz called it. Then, as if startled by their own freedom, the whole party took wing. Their flight made that shrill, whizzing sound peculiar to pigeons; they circled above us, climbed higher and higher, then darted out of sight.

We stood gazing after them. Presently they reappeared and settled quietly on the dove-cot, and we congratulated ourselves—until the three blue pigeons, the noble foreigners for whom chiefly I had built the house, sprang up and rose in wide circles, winging straight towards Falconhurst. Their departure had a resolute air that made my heart sink; I feared them lost for ever.

We tried to console ourselves by feeding and petting the four that remained, but disappointment sat with us the whole day, and the dove-cot became the centre of every glance and errand.

Nothing more was seen until about the middle of the next day. We were working inside the cavern when Jack burst in, flushed and breathless.

“He is there! He is come! He really is!”

“Who? Who is there?” we cried.

“The blue pigeon, to be sure! Hurrah! Hurrah!”

Ernest scoffed that Jack wished to trick us, but I rose at once. “Why should it be nonsense? I fully believe we shall see them all soon!”

We ran out. There he stood—the handsome blue fellow—and he was not alone. He was billing and cooing to a mate, a stranger of his own breed, bowing and sidling with comical elegance, popping in and out of the door as if to say, See how safe, see how fine, until the shy little lady yielded and stepped daintily within.

“Now shut the door! Pull the cord!” the boys shouted, rushing at the string.

“Stop!” I cried. “Let it alone. Don’t frighten the little darlings. Besides—the others will be coming. Would you shut the door in their faces?”

“Here they come!” Fritz exclaimed, his keen eye catching movement far off.

To our delight the second blue pigeon arrived, also with a mate. After a pretty scene of coaxing and feigned reluctance, he led her in. The third and handsomest was last. Perhaps he had greater difficulty in finding a bride fit for his splendour; but by then we spoke of “Mr. and Mrs. Nicobar” as if the matter were settled by law.

Late in the day, Franz and his mother went out to provide for supper, but the child returned almost at once, crying that we must hasten to the dove-cot to see something beautiful. We rushed out and found the third stranger returned with a lovely mate. Encouraged by the others already housed, they soon made themselves at home.

Nest-building began quickly. Among the gathered materials I noticed a long grey moss or lichen, hanging in beard-like tufts from old trees, and I thought of that West Indian growth used instead of horse-hair for stuffing mattresses. The moment my wife heard of it, her mind—always turning household needs into plans—leapt ahead. If we collected enough, she would clean and sort it; there would be bolsters, pillows, saddle-pads, cushions. Even here, among cliffs and wild birds, her imagination returned to the art of comfort: a home is built as much by what you can rest your head upon as by what you can shelter under.

We had also to thank the pigeons for nutmegs, which we planted carefully in our orchard, and so the days went on without event—until Jack, as usual, got himself into a scrape that set all hearts beating.

He had gone early on one of his private expeditions—his habit, that he might surprise us with some new acquisition. This time he returned in miserable plight: covered with mud and green slime, a great bundle of Spanish canes on his back muddy and green like himself, one shoe missing, and misery on his face so plain we could not laugh, though he looked a perfect caricature of disaster.

“My dear boy! What has happened? Where have you been?”

“Only in the swamp behind the powder magazine, father,” he said, near tears. “I went to get reeds for my wickerwork—baskets and hen-coops—and I saw such beauties farther in, much finer than those at the edge. I tried to get to them. I jumped from one firm spot to another till I slipped and sank over my ankles. I tried to go on—the reeds were close—but I went deeper and deeper till I was above the knees in thick soft mud, and there I stuck!

“I screamed and shouted. Nobody came. I was in a regular fright. At last who should appear but my faithful Fangs! He knew my voice and came right over the swamp, but all he could do was help me make a row; I wonder you didn’t hear us. The very rocks rang, but nothing came of it, so at last I thought of an expedient. I cut down all the reeds I could reach around me and bound them into this bundle. That made a firm place to lean on while I worked and kicked to free my legs. After much struggling I managed to get astride on the reeds.

“There I sat above the slime, while Fangs ran yelping between me and the bank, surprised I didn’t follow. Then I thought of catching his tail. He dragged and pulled, and I sprawled and crawled and waded—sometimes on my reeds like a raft, sometimes lugging them—till we got back to firm ground. But I had a near squeak for it.”

“A fortunate escape indeed,” I said, and thanked God from my heart. “Fangs has acted the hero, and you have shown presence of mind. Now go to your mother and get rid of those slimy traces. But you have brought splendid canes—exactly what I want for a new scheme.”

For I meant to construct a loom for my wife. I knew she understood weaving, and I had long wanted to give her the means to make cloth rather than only mend and patch. I chose two strong reeds, split them carefully, then bound them back together so that when dry they would be straight and equal for a frame. Smaller reeds we cut and sharpened for the teeth of the comb. The boys did this merrily without suspecting the purpose, calling them “father’s monster toothpicks,” and laughing at the idea of anyone with a mouth big enough to use such things.

As the parts were made ready and put together, I kept the secret from my wife. To the children’s questions I replied with mock mystery, “Oh, it is an outlandish sort of musical instrument; mother will know how to play upon it.”

When at last I presented it, her joy was reward enough—joy, and the sight of her hands moving with skill and familiarity over the new contrivance, while the children watched as if she truly were playing an instrument. The loom’s soft clack and steady beat became part of our acoustic world—an honest domestic sound, neither shriek nor clang, but a rhythm that promised warmth for our bodies and continuity for our ways.

About this time a beautiful little foal, son of the onager, was added to our small stud. He promised to grow strong and tractable, and we named him Swift, intending to train him for my riding.

Our cave arrangements were now well advanced, and I applied myself to contriving an aqueduct, for it was a long and troublesome journey to fetch water from Jackal River, especially on washing days. I wanted it finished before the rainy season set in, so I began at once. Hollow bamboo pipes served well, and a large cask became the reservoir. The supply proved good; the comfort of having fresh water close at hand was so great that my wife declared she was as pleased with our engineering as if we had given her a marble basin with dolphins and mermaids—though she valued far more the simple sound of water near her hands.

We pressed forward all work connected with stores for the winter rains. Roots, fruits, grains—potatoes, rice, guavas, sweet acorns, pine-cones—came load after load to the cavern. My wife’s needle never rested, for the demand for sacks and bags was incessant. Casks and barrels of every size were pressed into service, until at last even the raft was knocked to pieces so its tubs might do duty in the store-rooms.

The weather grew unsettled. Heavy clouds banked on the horizon. Storms swept over us with gusting wind, thunder, lightning, and sudden torrents. The sea heaved in frequent commotion; groundswells drove masses of water hissing and foaming against the cliffs. Everything in nature seemed to join in a solemn overture to the grand work of the year—the long season of rain. It was near the beginning of June, and we had twelve weeks of bad weather before us.

We moved some of the animals to the salt-cave with us. The cow, the ass, Lightfoot, Storm, and the dogs were necessary; Knips, Fangs, and the eagle promised amusement in the long evenings when the rain would drum and the wind would prowl round our rock.
