The boys would ride often to Falconhurst to see all was in order and fetch what we needed.

Much remained to be done to make the cave truly comfortable, and now that we must live indoors, comfort mattered more. The inner darkness annoyed me. After thinking, I called Jack to help. We found a tall, strong bamboo that reached to the vaulted roof. We planted it firmly in the earthen floor, wedging it fast.

Jack climbed it cleverly with hammer and chisel, enlarged a crevice in the roof, and fixed a pulley. When he descended, I drew up a large ship’s lantern well supplied with oil. With its four wicks it gave a fair light, and the cave’s darkness softened into a usable dusk where faces could be seen and work could be done.

Then we set to arranging rooms. Ernest and Franz undertook the library, fixing shelves and setting the books in order—careful as monks preserving a fragile inheritance. Jack and his mother took the sitting-room and kitchen. Fritz and I, better fitted for heavy work, arranged the workshops: the carpenter’s bench, the turning lathe, the great chest of tools placed where hands could find them even when the rain raged outside and the sea threw its endless roar against the rock.

“Now for the finishing up of this dirty job,” I cried at daybreak, forcing cheer into my voice as we rose and set ourselves again to the whale.

We built a rough stand of poles and crosspieces, a sort of scaffold where we could heap the tubs of blubber high. Then, with all the weight we could contrive—stones, levers, and our own hands—we pressed it hard, and the first pure oil came obediently, slipping over the lip in slow, glossy runnels into vessels set beneath. It was the only pleasing part of the business: that clean, pale flow, like clarified butter when one has skimmed it carefully and kept the fire low.

Afterward there was nothing but stench and labour. We carried what remained to the cauldron we had placed well away from our home, for the very air around it seemed to turn greasy. The fire had to be steady, not raging—too much heat scorches and spoils, too little leaves the fat cloudy—so we fed it patiently, and as the blubber loosened and melted we skimmed, strained through coarse cloth, and watched the oil settle clearer with each careful pass. The vapour that rose was thick and sickening, an oily breath that clung to hair and clothes and tongue; even the sea-wind could not wholly drive it off. The shore birds kept their distance, and the usual friendly chorus of our little world—goats bleating, hens clucking, the soft busy chitter of the boys—seemed muted beneath that oppressive smell, as though the island itself were holding its breath.

My poor wife bore it with her usual quiet courage, though I saw how it tried her. When at last we had filled casks and even bags stitched from intestines—tight, tough vessels, unpleasant to handle but useful beyond price—we stowed them in what the children called our “cellar,” our roughest store-room. Only then did she speak her mind plainly.

“This new island ought to be the headquarters for another little colony,” said she, wiping her hands and looking toward the water. “Any animals left there would be safe from apes and other plunderers—and you would find it so convenient to boil whale-blubber and strain train oil and the like, without poisoning the air of our home.”

The boys welcomed the scheme at once, as they welcomed every plan that promised new ground and new doings. Yet when I reminded them of the carcass still lying there—rank, enormous, a thing that would turn the breeze foul for days—they agreed it was wiser to let wind and storms, birds and insects cleanse the place, to strip it down to a bare, harmless skeleton before we made it our outpost.

All that day, even while the smell still seemed lodged in my nostrils, my thoughts kept returning to an idea that had seized me: a rowing-machine, something to spare our arms and lengthen our range. I set to work with an iron bar laid across the middle of the boat, projecting a foot each way. In the centre I fitted ribbed machinery; at each end I contrived a kind of nave, like that of a cart-wheel, and fixed into it four flat paddles set obliquely, meant to bite the water as oars do.

Then I arranged the jack so that its strong cogwheel would catch firmly upon those ribs. When wound, it drove the cross-bar round with speed, and with it the paddles at both ends, beating the sea in quick, regular strokes. The contrivance was rough, and in many ways begged improvement, but when Fritz and I wound it and pushed off for a trial, the boat splashed forward at such a rate that the very shores rang with the family’s cheering and clapping. Their delight rolled across the bay and came back to us on the clean morning air—sound as nourishing as any meal, a reminder that even here our well-being depended not only on food and shelter, but on the living music of those we loved.

Everyone clamoured to go at once, yet the day was already declining, and I would not risk a late return. I promised instead that on the morrow we should cruise to Cape Disappointment and the little settlement at Prospect Hill.

That evening became a small household festival of preparation: clothes laid out, arms examined, food packed. My wife’s hands moved with the calm precision of one who can make a table out of a rock ledge and a feast out of scant stores, yet she never let our meals become mere fuel. She wrapped what was needed, chose what would travel, and spoke of flavours as if naming them preserved something of home that no shipwreck could take. We went early to rest.

At dawn we left the house in good order, meaning to be away all day. Besides our usual stores we took spades and mattocks, for I wished to bring back young cocoanut trees and shrubs; and on our return, to land on Whale Island and begin planting there.

We steered toward the opposite side of the bay. The sea lay smooth as poured oil—cleaner, blessedly, than any we had rendered—and the rowing-machine did its duty without complaint. Safety Bay and Shark Island slipped behind us; the coast spread out in a wide, bright panorama, every headland and belt of trees set in clear light, and the surf making its own constant low hymn along the outer rocks.

Near Prospect Hill we moored the boat and walked through the woods toward our little farm, gathering fresh cocoanuts and selecting young plants as we went. The grove had its own sound: leaves rubbing softly, insects ticking in the undergrowth, a bird’s call falling into the pauses like a drop into a bowl. Then, before we saw the cottage, we heard the cocks crow.

That sharp homely cry went through me with a force I had not expected. In an instant it carried me—painfully, vividly—into the memory of other mornings, of rides and walks in our former life, when such familiar sounds would greet us near some friendly house. There, that crowing would have meant people, warmth, talk, bread in an oven, a pot simmering. Here it rose from unconscious animals into a solitude so complete that the sound itself seemed like a ghost of language: recognisable, yet unable to translate the whole of what it once contained. We, who would have been welcomed with joy in so many homes, came upon our own cottage unnoticed by anything but beasts.

So long had we been absent that our arrival threw the place into panic. The older animals had forgotten us; their young—the lambs, kids, and chickens who had never seen a human face—took us for an invading troop.

The boys could not milk the goats at first. The creatures sprang away, wild-eyed, stamping and tossing. At last, using the lasso, they captured them one by one, bound their legs, and then—with a touch as old as shepherding—offered them salt to lick. The change was immediate; the goats, busy with the savour, stood quiet enough. Soon we had a supply of excellent milk, warm and fragrant, which we poured from the cocoanut shells we used into calabash flasks, to carry away what we would not drink at dinner.

The fowls were coaxed more gently, with handfuls of grain and rice. My wife, patient and quick, caught as many as she wished, her hands firm but not cruel. She moved among them like someone who understood that food is not merely taken but negotiated, even in the simplest farmyard economy.

We were ready for dinner. The cold provisions were set out, and the chief dish—prepared at the boys’ insistence with great ceremony—was a piece of the whale’s tongue, cooked specially for this entertainment, as if the very boldness of the meal could turn our recent filthy labour into triumph.

But when it was tasted, triumph collapsed into misery. One after another, faces fell; each pronounced it “horrid stuff.” They begged for pickled herring to chase away the taste of train-oil that clung to it, and at last, with a unanimity that would have been comic if it had not been so sincere, they bestowed the cherished dainty upon Fangs. The dog accepted it without scruple, proving again that there are palates which survive any history.

Fortunately we had enough of other eatables; and the fresh cocoanuts—cool, sweet, faintly nutty, with that clean scent of split husk—and the goat’s milk restored everyone’s humour. My wife, who could not bear waste, carefully packed up what remained, while Fritz and I cut sugar-cane shoots I wished to plant. Then we returned to the shore and embarked again.

Before turning back for Whale Island, I felt a strong wish to round Cape Disappointment and survey the coast beyond. Yet the promontory earned its name: a long sandbank, hidden reefs, and treacherous rocks stretched far out. Fritz saw breakers ahead; we put about at once, and with the help of a light breeze made for Whale Island instead.

On landing, I began immediately to plant the sugar-cane shoots. The work was plain and satisfying—soil loosened, shoots laid at the right depth and angle, earth pressed firm, water carried where it would do the most good. For a little while the boys helped, then, as boys will, grew weary of steady labour and wandered off to hunt shells and coral. Their mother stayed with me, and together we finished the row.

Soon Jack came running back, shouting as if he had discovered an age of the world.

“Father! Mother! Do come and look. There is an enormous skeleton lying here—the skeleton of some fearful great beast—a mammoth, I should think!”

“Why, Jack!” I answered, laughing. “Have you forgotten our old acquaintance, the whale? What else could it be?”

“Oh no, father, it is not the whale. This thing has not fish bones, but real good, honest, huge, beast bones. I don’t know what can have become of the whale—floated out to sea most likely. This mammoth is ever so much bigger. Come and see!”

I was about to follow him when, from another direction, a cry broke out sharper and more urgent.

“Father! Father! A great enormous turtle! Please make haste. It is waddling back to the sea as hard as it can go, and we can’t stop it!”

That mattered more than Jack’s marvel. Snatching up an oar, I ran.

Sure enough, a huge turtle was scrambling toward the water and was within a few paces of it, though Ernest—brave beyond his strength—clung to one hind leg and was dragged along in the sand. I sprang down the bank; using the oar as a lever, we managed with difficulty to turn the creature on its back. It was an immense specimen, fully eight feet long; once overturned it was helpless, legs beating the air like slow paddles.

We left it sprawling and went to inspect Jack’s “mammoth,” which proved, of course, to be nothing but the whale’s skeleton. I convinced him by pointing out our own footmarks still impressed in places and the broken jaws where we had hacked out the whalebone.

“What made you take up that fancy about a mammoth, my boy?”

“Ernest put it into my head, father. He said there seemed to be the skeleton of an antediluvian monster there, so I ran to look closer, and I never thought of the whale when I saw no fish bones. I suppose Ernest was joking.”

“Whales are often called fishes by those little acquainted with the animal kingdom,” said I, “but they belong to the class of mammals—man, monkeys, bats, dogs and cats, all hoofed animals, whales and their allies; the last on the list being the sloth. Their name comes from the Latin ‘mama,’ a breast, because all in this class nourish their young with milk. The whale’s bones differ chiefly in being hollow and filled with air, making the carcass more buoyant; birds’ bones are hollow for a similar reason.”

Fritz stared at the immense pale framework half buried in sand.

“What a marvellous structure it is, father! What a ponderous mass of bones! Can we not make use of any of them?”

“Nothing strikes me at this moment,” I said. “We will let them bleach awhile. Perhaps later, sawn up, they may serve for chairs, or a reading-desk for the museum. But now we must return home. Bring the boat round to where the turtle awaits his fate; we must settle how to deal with him.”

It was soon decided that he must swim—for our convenience as much as for his. I fastened an empty water-cask to a long line; one end I made fast to the bow of the boat, the other I passed carefully around the turtle’s neck and fore-paws. Then we lifted him enough to let him regain his feet. The instant he could, he made for the sea, plunged in, and the cask floated after him, preventing him from sinking beneath the burden he now towed.

We were all aboard in a moment. The worthy fellow, after trying vainly to dive, set himself doggedly to swim straight forward, towing us comfortably. I kept ready to cut the line at the least sign of danger and guided him by striking the water with a boat-hook right or left when he was disposed to turn too far one way. The boys delighted in it, shouting comparisons of me to Neptune in his car, drawn by dolphins and attended by Amphitrite and Tritons. Their laughter skittered over the water, and even the turtle’s steady splashing became part of a strange little procession-song, as if myth had decided to make itself useful for once and haul a boat.

We landed safely near Rockburg. The turtle was condemned and executed soon afterward. The shell—eight feet long and three broad—once cleaned and prepared, was destined to become a trough for our water supply at the cave. The meat we salted carefully and stored for many a savoury meal. My wife oversaw the salting with the gravity of a keeper of records: thickness of cut, proportion of salt, the order of layering—these were not small details to her but a kind of domestic history, a way of making the future taste dependable.

It had been my intention to bring a piece of land under cultivation before the next rainy season and sow it with different sorts of grain. But unforeseen circumstances had delayed us, and our animals, unaccustomed to the yoke, could not yet serve for the plough. I gave up the idea for the present and applied myself, with Ernest’s help, to completing the loom. Though clumsy in workmanship, it became fit for use. In my youth I had spent many hours in the workshops of weavers and other artisans; that half-forgotten knowledge, once a mere curiosity, now returned like a saved language—imperfect, yet priceless.

We needed paste or size to smear over the threads, but we could not spare flour. I used isinglass instead, which kept the warp moist perfectly well and spared us the necessity of setting the loom in some damp uncomfortable place merely to prevent overdrying. From the same isinglass I made thin plates to serve as window-panes. They were at least as transparent as horn, and when fixed deep in the rock and beyond the reach of rain they admitted light faithfully, a quiet improvement that changed the very tone of our days inside the cave.

Encouraged, I took up harness-making next. Our hunts had provided plenty of leather. I covered light wooden frames with it, stuffing them with hairy moss or lichen, and before long our animals were equipped with saddles, stirrups, bridles, yokes, and collars, to the great satisfaction of their youthful riders and drivers.

This was followed by busy days with the annual return of the herring shoals, and after them, as before, shoals of other fish and many seals. We had learned too much of want to let such wealth pass unused. We captured large numbers and salted, dried, and stored, turning the sea’s brief generosity into lasting security. The shore, for a time, was full of sound: the slap of fish, the cries of birds wheeling overhead, the wet shuffle of seals, the boys calling to one another, knives tapping boards. It was noisy work, but it was living noise—sound that meant food, warmth, continuance—not the harsh, pointless clatter of machines made only to drown thought.

When the boys began to beg for another shooting expedition, I asked for patience. Before we went, we needed baskets; our sacks were failing, and there was constant demand for strong, airy containers to carry and keep our roots and fruits. We tried our hands at basket-making. The first attempts were clumsy, the weave uneven, the rims weak, but perseverance brought improvement. We produced a good supply of all sorts and sizes. One very large basket I furnished with openings so a strong stick could pass through; thus, when laden, it could be carried by two persons without tearing the sides.

The moment the children grasped the idea, they fetched a bamboo pole, set it through the openings, popped little Franz into the basket, and carried him about in triumph. His laughter, rising and falling with the swing, made my wife shake her head and smile despite herself.

That sport suggested a new notion to Fritz.

“Oh, father,” he cried, “don’t you think we might make something like this for mother, and carry her much more comfortably than jolting along in the cart?”

The boys shouted with glee. Their mother admitted the plan might be feasible, but confessed she did not much like the thought of sitting in the middle of a basket and only peeping over the rim now and then.

I promised it should be a well-shaped, comfortable sedan-chair—or litter. Then came the question of how it should be carried, since the boys could not play at palanquin-bearers without danger to their mother and misery to themselves.

“The bull and the buffalo!” cried Jack. “Why not use them for it? Let’s go and try them now!”

Off they ran. Soon the great basket was securely hung between Storm and Grumble. Fritz and Jack sprang into their saddles; Ernest, with much caution, deposited himself into the “cradle,” as Franz called it. They set forth at a sober pace. The animals were perfectly docile, only a little surprised at their new burden.

“Oh, it is so pleasant, mother—it is a delightful motion,” Ernest called as they passed us. “It swings and rocks really soothingly. Quicker, Fritz! Go quicker!”

The trot pleased him too, and the pace quickened. Before long Storm and Grumble were moving fast enough to shake and jolt the basket fearfully. Ernest screamed for them to stop, but his brothers thought it excellent sport to “shake up” the professor. They made a circuit of the level ground near Rockburg and finally pulled up before us like performers stopping for applause.

It was impossible not to laugh; the sight was too ridiculous. But Ernest was angry, his reproaches sharpened, his brothers answered with high words, and a quarrel began to rise like a sudden gust. I stepped in at once and showed them how easily a joke carried too far becomes bad feeling, and how dangerous any breach of good fellowship and brotherly love would be to us all—our true strength in this strange land.

Good humour returned quickly. Ernest himself helped to unharness the beasts and rewarded them with handfuls of salt and barley, adding—half dignified, half forgiving—that they must have more palanquin practice another day.

Later I sat with my wife and Fritz under the verandah’s shade, our hands busy with wicker-work, our talk easy. The reeds by Jackal River made their soft continual whisper, the kind of sound that settles the mind. Suddenly Fritz stood up, peering down the avenue that led from the river.

“I see something so strange in the distance, father! What in the world can it be? First it seems drawn in coils on the ground like a cable, then it rises as it were a little mast, then that sinks, and the coils move along again. It is coming towards the bridge.”

My wife turned pale at once. Calling the other boys, she retreated into the cave. I told them to close the entrances and keep watch with firearms at the upper windows—openings we had made in the rock, reached inside by steps and a gallery that ran along the front of our rooms.

Fritz stayed by me while I raised my spy-glass.

“It is, as I feared, an enormous serpent!” I cried. “It advances directly this way, and we shall be in the greatest possible danger, for it will cross the bridge to a certainty.”

“May we not attack it, father?” Fritz asked, brave and eager.

“Only with the greatest caution,” I said. “It is too formidable and too tenacious of life for rash attempts. Thank God we are at Rockburg; we can keep safely in retreat while we watch for a chance to destroy it. Go to your mother and help prepare the firearms. I will join you directly, but I must observe its movements.”

Reluctantly he went. Alone, I watched the monster advance—gigantic, already too near the bridge for us to remove it in time. I remembered with dread how easily such a creature might force its way through walls. It moved with slow, writhing undulations, and from time to time reared its head fifteen or twenty feet, turning it gradually as if listening for life. Even its motion seemed to have a sound in it: a faint sliding whisper through grass and earth, a dry rubbing that made the skin tighten on one’s arms.

When it crossed the bridge with a suspicious slowness, I withdrew and joined my family, who were ready at the upper openings—our little fortress garrisoned, though their faces showed fear. My presence steadied them somewhat.

We waited, unseen, hearts beating. The serpent soon became visible below. It seemed puzzled by the signs of habitation, turning and coiling in the space before the cave, repeatedly lifting its head. Then, as if the sight were too much to bear, the boys fired one after another, and even their mother discharged her gun. The shots did little beyond startling it; its movements quickened. Fritz and I fired too, with steadier aim, yet without effect. With a gliding rush it entered the reedy marsh to the left and disappeared.

A weight lifted from us all at once. We spoke eagerly of its awful length and magnificent terror. I recognized it as a boa constrictor—an immense specimen, upwards of thirty feet.

I explained that in South America it is called Boaguacu; the first syllable, with the Latin addition indicating its manner of killing by pressure—constriction—gives the name by which it is commonly known.

But knowledge did not bring comfort. The nearness of such a reptile filled me with anxiety. I forbade anyone to leave the house on any pretence without my express permission.

For three whole days we lived in suspense, not daring to go more than a few hundred steps from the door. The enemy showed no sign; yet the blockade felt real, as if our lives had shrunk to the cave’s dim chambers and the narrow strip of safe ground outside. Even our soundscape changed: fewer wandering footfalls, fewer calls across open air; the animals’ restlessness sounded sharper in confinement, hooves clicking, wings fluttering in tight spaces.

We might have persuaded ourselves the boa had crossed the swamp and found some cleft through the cliffs beyond, if not for the behaviour of our geese and ducks. They were accustomed to rest at night among the reeds, and now they swam anxiously, clapping wings and cackling in disturbed bursts. At last, in a panic of instinct, they took wing, crossed the harbour, and settled on Shark Island. Their fear was a message written in sound: the serpent still lurked there.

My perplexity deepened. To attack such a monster in dense reeds amid dangerous swamps with insufficient force would be madness; yet to live shut up, cut off from our work, enduring the cave’s unnatural light, our minds strained by constant alarm—this was scarcely less dreadful.

We were delivered at last, and by none other than our good old donkey Grizzle—not by any virtue, but by sheer stupidity.

Our stores were low; hay for the animals failed on the evening of the third day. I resolved to send them, under Fritz’s guidance, across the river at the ford where they could feed in safety. Fritz was to ride Lightfoot, and the animals were to be fastened together until all were over.

Next morning we began to tie them in a line. While we worked, my wife opened the door. Grizzle, fresh and frolicsome after long rest and regular feeding, suddenly broke away, cut a few absurd capers, and bolted out at full gallop straight for the marsh.

We shouted after him. Fritz would have dashed in pursuit, but I held him back. In another moment the ass was near the reeds—and then, with a cold shudder, we saw the snake rise from its lair. Its fiery eyes swept; its dark jaws opened wide; the forked tongue darted out greedily. Grizzle’s foolish gaiety died in a single beat of horror.

Aware of danger at last, he stopped short, braced all four legs, and let out the most piteous, discordant bray that ever wrung echo from rocks—an ugly sound, yet heartbreakingly alive, like a complaint that could not be translated into anything but pain.

Swift as a fencer’s thrust, the destroyer was upon him. It wound round him—entangled, enfolded, compressed—cunningly avoiding the convulsive kicks of the agonized animal.

A cry of horror rose from my family.

“Shoot him, father! Oh, shoot him—do save poor Grizzle!”

“My children, it is impossible!” I cried. “Our old friend is lost to us for ever. I have hope, however, that when the snake is gorged with his prey, we may be able to attack him with some chance of success.”

“But the horrible wretch is never going to swallow him all at once, father?” Jack cried. “That will be too shocking!”

“Snakes have no grinders, but only fangs,” said I, forcing myself to speak steadily. “Therefore they cannot chew and must swallow their food whole. The idea is startling, but not more truly horrible than the tearing and bloodshed when lions or tigers seize their prey.”

“But,” said little Franz, clinging to his mother, “how can the snake separate the flesh from the bones without teeth? And is this kind of snake poisonous?”

“No, dear child,” I answered. “Not poisonous—only fearfully strong and ferocious. And it has no need to separate flesh from bones. It swallows them—skin, hair, and all—and digests everything in its stomach.”

“It seems utterly impossible,” Fritz exclaimed, staring as if reason itself had been offended, “that the broad ribs, the strong legs, hoofs and all, should go down that throat.”

The peril that had lately coiled itself into our lives was, for the moment, ended; yet fear has a way of lingering like the after-scent of a serpent’s track—faint, sour, and stubbornly present in the mind. I could not shake the thought that another of those stealthy enemies might have slipped, unseen, into the morass; or that, as the last had done, it might have glided in from beyond Falconhurst, using that narrow throat of land we called the Gap.

So I planned two journeys, one nested inside the other as daily labor nests inside prayer: first, a close and methodical search of the thicket and swamp; and next, without delay, an expedition to the Gap itself—the one passage by which such a creature could enter our coast territory.

When I called my sons to accompany me into the marsh, I found reluctance in two faces at once. Ernest, who could argue a whole afternoon away on the names of plants, looked as though the reeds themselves might rise to refute him; and Jack, brave in bright weather, swore his ribs still ached at the memory of the last lash of that monstrous tail. He confessed—half angry at himself, half pleading—that cold shivers ran him through whenever he imagined the blow returning.

But there are moments in a household where kindness must wear the stern mask of necessity. I would not yield. We set ourselves to crossing the marsh as we had learned to do: laying planks and wicker hurdles down upon the sucking ground, stepping forward, then lifting what lay behind us to place again before—an awkward, patient dance over treacherous earth, the wet making small, greedy sounds beneath every shift. Even the marsh had its voice: the whisper of reed-tops rubbing, the soft gulp of mud, the occasional plop of unseen frogs—soundscapes that, in their purity, steady the heart far more than the clangor of any city street ever could. In that wild quiet, danger announces itself honestly; it does not hide under iron wheels and shrieking metal.

We discovered nothing new beyond the signs of what had already been: tracks pressed deep into the reeds; the beaten-down lair where rushes, grasses, and bog-plants lay flattened as if a heavy rope had been dragged in circles. The place stank faintly of damp animal heat long gone cold.

At length we emerged beyond the thicket onto firmer ground near the sheer rock wall, and there—like providence poured into a channel—we saw a brook, clear and sparkling, threading out of an opening in the stone. The opening proved to be a cave of considerable size.

We entered. The roof arched above us in pale grandeur, hung with stalactites like frozen drips of time; in places they joined the floor to form pillars that seemed to hold up the very mountain. Underfoot lay a snow-white earth, fine and soft, with a smooth, soapy feel that clung to the fingers.

“This,” I said, rubbing it between thumb and forefinger and feeling it slip like cream, “is fullers’ earth. A fortune, in our way. As good as soap for washing, and it spares me the trouble of turning soap-boiler.”

My wife’s concerns were never far from my mind; clean linen, in a rough life, is not vanity but health. Even the simplest domestic want—fresh water, clean cloth, untainted food—becomes a kind of sacred text, written in the body’s comfort.

The brook slipped onward through an inner opening. Fritz, quick as ever to follow what runs and calls, passed through to trace it, and almost at once his voice came back, magnified by stone.

“Father! The passage widens greatly—come and see!”

I left Ernest and Jack in the outer cave and followed Fritz into the deeper dark. Before we advanced far I fired a pistol-shot; the report leapt away into the black, and the echoes returned in rolling waves as if the cave itself were a great drum. Jack would later mistake those reverberations for falling rock; to my ear they were a measure of distance—a kind of natural acoustics, solemn and uncorrupted. No manufactured hall ever speaks so truthfully as stone that has never known chisels.

I lit the stub of candle I always carried. Its flame burned clear and steady, yet it seemed pitifully small in that vastness. Still, it gave just enough light to catch the gleam on the walls.

Suddenly Fritz stopped, his breath audible in the hush.

“I verily believe this is a second cave of salt! See how the walls glance—how the roof throws back the light!”

I touched the slick surface, then tasted the wetness left by the stream.

“Not salt,” I said. “The water leaves no track and tastes sweet. I rather think we have penetrated a cave of rock crystal.”

Fritz’s eyes shone as if the candle had slipped inside them. “Then we have discovered a great treasure!”

“A treasure,” I agreed, “if it serves us. Otherwise it is like Crusoe’s lump of gold—pretty to behold, useless to eat.”

Yet even as I spoke, I knew the human heart keeps catalogues besides the pantry: wonders nourish a family too, though differently.

Fritz took up a stone. “At least I’ll break off a specimen.”

He struck, and a piece came away—dull and cloudy.

“What a pity,” he said, frowning. “I must knock off another.”

“Gently,” I warned. “You have spoiled the form. These crystals grow in pyramids—six-sided facets. You must not smash what nature has taken ages to shape.”

We explored as far as the candle allowed, turning this way and that, the floor crunching softly underfoot with scattered grit. Fritz secured at last a large lump that showed several crystals in near perfection—sharp as frozen sunlight. Then, our light sinking low, we withdrew. Fritz, unable to resist, fired one more “farewell” shot; the echoes answered like distant thunder trapped underground.

When we returned to the open cave and daylight, we found Jack sobbing bitterly. The moment he saw us he ran, seized me, and clung as if I might dissolve again into stone-shadow.

“My child—what is it?” I asked, alarmed.

“Oh!” he cried, gulping breath. “I thought you were lost. Twice I heard a noise as if rocks had shattered down, and I thought you and Fritz were crushed. It was horrible. Oh, how glad I am to see you!”

I held him and soothed him, explaining that the “shattering” had been only our pistol-shots, magnified by the cave’s throat. His fear, born of that echoing wilderness, touched me; sound is not merely sound—it is a force that shapes the spirit. A clean, honest echo may frighten a child, but it does not grind him down the way constant, meaningless clamor does. There is mercy even in wild alarm, because it ends, and silence returns.

When Jack had steadied, I asked why he had been alone.

“Ernest is over there among the reeds,” he said, pointing. “I daresay he did not hear the shots.”

I found Ernest indeed, seated with calm concentration, weaving reeds into a basket. He had fashioned it cleverly for fishing: a funnel-shaped entrance that invited the fish in and made escape difficult, the wide belly of the basket turning into a living trap. The work smelled of green sap and lake-water; the reeds rasped softly against one another in his hands.

“I shot a young serpent while you were away, father,” he said, with a seriousness that made my blood turn for a moment. “It lies there, covered with rushes. Nearly four feet long, and as thick as my arm.”

“A serpent!” I cried, and hastened to the place, heart hammering with the dread of a brood in the swamp.

But when I drew back the rushes I saw at once the sleek, dark body, the finlike edges—no scales, no coils of venom.

“A fine large eel, my boy,” I said, letting my breath go. “This will provide excellent supper tonight. I am glad you had the courage to kill it instead of fleeing from a supposed serpent.”

Ernest, a little pale still, answered honestly, “I thought it would be so horrid to be pursued and caught that I preferred facing it. My shot took effect—but it was very difficult to kill. It moved about even after the head was smashed.”

“The tenacity of life in eels is remarkable,” I told him. “Some say the best way is to grasp them by the neck and slap the tail smartly against stone or post.”

We returned by keeping close to the cliffs where the ground held firm. At the fountain we found my wife washing clothes, her hands red from the work, her face brightening instantly at our safe return. When she saw the white earth we brought, her practical joy was almost as great as Jack’s relief.

“Good,” she said, feeling it between her fingers. “Soap is nearly gone.”

That evening the eel was cooked for supper, and it proved richer and better than many fish, its flesh firm and white when properly handled. We skinned it cleanly, cut it into pieces, and simmered it with herbs and a pinch of salt until the air of our home grew warm with savory steam. Hunger in such a life is keen, but it is also discerning; after danger, food is never merely fuel—it is reassurance. Around that table the boys listened again to the tale of our plank-walk over the swamp and the crystal cavern’s cold glittering halls, while my wife measured each detail by what it might mean for the household: wash, store, preserve, endure.

Yet my mind returned, as it must, to the larger question: were we safe within the bounds between cliffs and sea? To answer it, preparations began at once for a broader search: beyond the river, as far as the Gap. I wished the whole family to go; and the decision pleased everyone, for travel together, even into uncertainty, was better than waiting at home for news.

Since we meant to be gone for several weeks, we took the small tent and a store of necessities—provisions, firearms, tools, cooking utensils, torches. Everything was packed onto the cart drawn by Storm and Grumble. Jack and little Franz mounted the load and played at once riders and drivers, their laughter adding a bright layer to the morning’s sound. My wife sat comfortably in the cart; Fritz rode ahead; Ernest and I walked. The dogs ranged at our flanks, and Fangs, our tame jackal, went with them—silent, watchful, like a shadow with ears.

We aimed first for Woodlands, and along the way we saw many traces of the serpent’s approach to Rockburg. Where soil was loose, the trail showed like a broad furrow, as if some great plough had passed without wheels.

At Falconhurst we halted and were welcomed, as always, by our poultry—clucking, fluttering, scolding with that domestic insistence that makes even a wilderness feel briefly civilized. The sheep and goats pressed in, nosing for salt and scraps.

By nightfall we reached Woodlands. All lay peaceful; no sign of the boa in that direction, no marks of apes. The little farm looked thriving—orderly as a well-tended kitchen shelf.

We spent the next day surveying the neighborhood and collecting cotton for pillows and cushions, for comfort too is a kind of strength. In the afternoon Franz accompanied me, carrying for the first time a small gun entrusted to him. The weight of it made him stand straighter; responsibility does that.

We took Fangs and Bruno and followed the left bank of the lake, threading among reedy thickets that forced us now and then away from the water. The dogs hunted in all directions, raising duck, snipe, and heron; but the birds flew directly across the lake so that Franz had no safe shot. He grew impatient and proposed firing at the black swans gliding on the glassy surface.

As he spoke, a harsh booming sound struck our ears, deep and hollow as if the earth itself were calling.

I paused, listening. Franz’s eyes widened. “Oh, father—can that be Swift, our young onager?”

“It cannot possibly be Swift,” I said. After a minute of attentive listening, I added, “I believe it is the cry of a bittern—a fine bird of the heron kind.”

Franz looked astonished. “How can a bird make such a roaring noise? One would think it an ox—more like lowing than braying.”

“The voice depends more on the windpipe and lungs, and the muscles that drive the breath, than on size,” I told him. “Consider the nightingale and the canary. Some say the bittern booms with its bill partly thrust into boggy ground, which deepens and muffles the sound.”

Franz longed for his first trophy to be so rare a bird. We sent the dogs into the wood and waited apart, each ready.

A sudden rustling shook the thicket. Franz fired, and his delighted shout rang out.

“I’ve hit him! I’ve hit him!”

“What have you hit?” I called.

“A wild pig!” he cried. “Bigger than Fritz’s!”

“Perhaps it is not a hog,” I said, hurrying to him. “Perhaps it is one of our little pigs from the farm. What will the old sow say to you then?”

But when I reached him I saw an animal certainly pig-like, yet not truly a pig: broad blunt snout, yellowish-grey bristles, no tail; and, on closer examination, webbed feet and curious teeth. It lay still, the life already gone.

“It is a capybara,” I said—“a water-loving creature of South America.”

Franz was beside himself with pride at having shot, as he called it, “a new creature.” But pride did not lighten the carcass. We soon realized carrying it whole would be difficult, and Franz, practical beneath his excitement, proposed opening and cleaning it to reduce its weight. We did so, hands working with the swift, competent motions necessity teaches. The smell was strange—earthy, animal, with an under-note that made me wary. Franz shouldered it in a game-bag until he grew tired, then asked if Bruno would bear it. The good dog accepted the burden willingly, and thus we reached Woodlands.

There we found Ernest surrounded by large rats—dead bodies strewn on the ground like fallen fruit.

“Where can all these have come from?” I exclaimed. “Have you and your mother been rat-hunting instead of gathering rice as you intended?”

Ernest answered with the air of one who has lived a tale and now recounts it.

“We came upon them unexpectedly. In the rice swamp, Knips sprang away to a long mound among the reeds and pounced on something trying to escape into a hole. He chattered and gnashed his teeth; the creature hissed and squeaked. When I ran up, Knips had a big rat by the tail. He would not let go, and the rat could not turn in the narrow entrance to bite him. I pulled it out and killed it with my stick.

“The mound looked curious, so I broke it open—hard work—and dislodged a dozen. Some I killed; many plunged into the water and escaped. Their dwelling was a vaulted tunnel of clay and mud, lined thick with sedges, rushes, water-lily leaves.

“There were other mounds close by. I stretched my game-bag across an entrance and hammered on the roof till a whole lot sprang out—several straight into the bag. I hit right and left, but repented when I found the whole community swarming about in excitement. Some escaped, many stopped in bewilderment, and some actually attacked me.

“It was anything but pleasant. I thought of Bishop Hatto in the Mouse Tower on the Rhine. Knips liked it as little as I did, skipping about desperately, though now and then he seized a rat by the neck. Just as I began to shout for help, Juno came dashing through reeds and water and made quick work. They fled at her. My mother forced her way through the marsh with difficulty and reached me at last. We collected the slain to show you—and for their skins.”

His story roused my curiosity, and I went to the place he described. There I found, to my surprise, a structure much like a beaver dam, though smaller and less complete.

“You have discovered a colony of beaver rats,” I told him. “They resemble beavers in skill and manner of life. Muskrat, musquash, and ondatra are other names. See—their webbed feet, their flattened tails. And we shall find two small glands containing musk. The sooner we strip the skins, the better; they will make caps.”

We returned to the house and met Fritz and Jack coming back from their own excursion. They reported no trace of serpents, great or small. Jack carried about a dozen eggs in his hat, carefully balanced; Fritz had shot two heath fowls, cock and hen.

At supper, Franz was eager to taste his capybara. Even he, in the first bite, could not keep his face from twisting at the peculiar flavor.

“It is the musk you taste,” I said, and I spoke of the odd presence of that scent in various creatures—the musk deer, musk ox, crocodile, and a muskrat of India said to taint even a corked bottle of wine if it merely runs across it—ending with the civet, or civet-cat.

“The civet is a handsome black-and-white animal,” I explained. “The perfume obtained from it was once considered a medicine; now it is chiefly used as a scent. It is formed in a double glandular pouch near the tail. The Dutch keep the creature in captivity for a continual supply. As it is quick and has sharp teeth, they place it in a long narrow cage where it cannot turn. A horn spoon is introduced, and the perfume—a thick oily stuff like butter—is scraped from the pouch. Then the civet is released until the supply is formed again.”

As I spoke, I could not help reflecting how words themselves can trap and handle living things. We name an animal, we classify it, we fit it into a foreign lexicon; yet the taste on the tongue—musk bitterness, leaf-spice sweetness—remains stubbornly personal, never fully carried by any term. Experience is always slightly untranslatable, even between father and son at the same table.

During the meal Jack suddenly ran to fetch his game-bag, producing fruit he had forgotten: several pale green apples, unknown to us. Their skin was smooth, their scent faintly warm, as if sugar and flower had agreed to whisper rather than shout.

“What are those?” I asked. “Are they good?”

“I hope so,” Jack said, “but Fritz and I were afraid of eating some awful poison, like the manchineel. So we brought them for inspection of the learned Master Knips.”

I cut one in two. It held a circle of seeds rather than the single stone of the manchineel. At that moment Knips sidled behind me, snatched up half, and began to munch with lively satisfaction. The boys, taking his appetite as proof, fell upon the fruit in a scramble so eager I had trouble saving two pieces for myself and their mother.

I believed it to be the cinnamon apple of the Antilles. Its flesh was tender and sweet with a faint spice—an aroma like distant baking, an imagined kitchen transported into jungle air.

Weariness settled over us at last. Mattresses and pillows were arranged, and the household of Woodlands slept.

At early light we set out again, steering toward a point between the sugar-brake and the Gap where we had once made an arbor of branches. It still stood in decent condition, so we spread sailcloth over it instead of pitching the tent and made comfortable quarters for the short time I meant to remain.

Our aim was to search for any trace of the boa constrictor or its kind. Fritz, Jack, and Franz went with me to the sugar-cane brake, and we convinced ourselves our enemy had not been there. It had been long since we enjoyed fresh cane-juice. We broke stalks, peeled away the tough rind, and chewed the fibrous sweetness until our mouths filled with cool syrup, spitting out the dry pulp. The air was full of green fragrance, and the canes rustled in the breeze like a thousand thin flutes.

Then the dogs began to bark violently, and we heard loud rustling and rattling through the cane thicket. We could see nothing a yard away. I hurried into open ground; the boys followed, guns ready. We stood, listening, the sound of movement coming nearer in a steady rush.

In a few minutes a herd of little pig-like creatures burst out, trotting briskly in single file as if obedience itself drove their legs. They were uniform grey, with short sharp tusks.

My double-barrel spoke twice; two fell. I was certain they were peccaries. The others did not scatter like deer; they continued after their leader, scarcely turning to avoid the dead, maintaining that same determined pace. Fritz and Jack fired as well and killed several more.

I remembered at once the odoriferous gland on the back must be removed immediately or the meat would be tainted beyond use. With the boys’ help I performed this operation without delay, hands working quickly before heat could set any rankness into the flesh.

Soon after, we heard shots in the direction of our hut where Ernest and my wife remained. I sent Jack to assist them and to fetch the cart for carrying our booty, while we opened and cleaned the animals to lessen weight.

Jack returned with Ernest and the cart. Ernest reported the procession had passed near the hut and that he, with Juno’s help, had secured three. This pleased me greatly, for I had resolved to cure a good store of hams. We loaded the cart; the boys crowned it with flowers and green boughs, and with songs of triumph that made the woods ring—music not purchased, not performed for applause, but rising out of work well done and hunger wisely answered.

Their mother waited anxiously; her relief at the sight of us safe was plain, but her eyes also measured the meat with a cook’s foresight: how much salt remained, how long smoke would hold, how many meals could be stretched across weeks.

After dinner we set to work. We singed and scalded off the bristles, the smell of singeing hair sharp in the air. I cut out the hams, divided the flitches, gave generous portions to the dogs, and cleansed and salted the meat with diligent care, rubbing crystals into every seam until the surfaces grew slick and pale. Meanwhile the boys prepared a shed where it would hang to cure in the smoke of fires made of green wood.

This unexpected labor detained us several days. On the second day, when the smoking-shed was ready, the boys begged to cook the smallest porker in the Otaheitean fashion. They dug a pit and burned dry grass, sticks, and weeds inside it, heating stones which they placed round the sides like a rough oven wall.

While the younger boys tended the fire, Fritz singed and washed the peccary thoroughly, then stuffed it with potatoes, onions, and herbs, adding a liberal sprinkling of salt and pepper. He sewed up the opening and wrapped the animal in large leaves to guard it from ash and dust—an improvisation that was, in its way, a form of culinary history: old techniques reborn in new hands, old knowledge surviving because it fed the living.

When the fire was no longer flame but embers, and the stones glowed hot, we laid the leaf-wrapped pig into the pit, covered it with hot ashes, then with earth, until the place looked like a great molehill.

Dinner was awaited with both appetite and curiosity. My wife, wary of our experiments, prepared plain food as a last resort. Yet even she watched the mound with interest; for a family’s culture is often preserved not in books but in what it dares to cook.

The curing-hut pleased her: roomy enough to hang all our hams and bacon. In the middle, on a wide hearth, we kept a large fire smouldering by heaping it with damp grass and green wood. The hut closed above so the smoke filled it and penetrated the meat. For several days the hams hung there, darkening slowly, absorbing that bitter-sweet preservation.

When Fritz at last announced he would open the earth-oven, everyone gathered. He removed earth and turf and stones, and a delicious odor rose as if summoned from the ground itself: roast pork—yes—but with a surprising spice, warm and intricate, that made my mouth fill with saliva at once. I understood then it came from the leaves.

The peccary was lifted out carefully. Once the cinders were brushed away, it looked beautifully cooked: skin browned, flesh tender, juices sealed within. Fritz received hearty praise—even from his mother.

The leaves, I believed, were from a tree known in Madagascar, called by the natives ravensara—“good leaf”—said to combine the scent of nutmeg, clove, and cinnamon. Its fruit is a nut with a gentler perfume, from which an essence is distilled and valued in native cookery. In speaking of it, I felt again that tug between languages: a native word carries a whole world of taste and practice; translated, it becomes merely “good leaf,” and the old music dims. How much disappears when the dominant tongue names and shelves everything as if it owns it.

During the days of curing, we roamed the neighborhood in all directions. We found no trace of the serpent, but we gained many useful things—among them gigantic bamboos, fifty to sixty feet long and proportionately thick. Cut across near the joints, they became casks, tubs, and pots; the long sharp thorns that girded the stem at intervals proved as strong and handy as iron nails.

One day we visited the farm at Prospect Hill and were provoked to find the vagabond apes had been there, doing mischief as they had done before. The animals and poultry were scattered; the cottage was torn and dirtied beyond any quick repair. With heavy hearts we left the disorder as it was, resolving to set it right later.

When all was ready for continuing our journey, we closed and barricaded the hut where we left the store of bacon. Then, arranging our march in our usual patriarchal fashion, we set out toward the Gap—the thorough defense of that defile being the chief object before us.

Because our last halting-place was hemmed in by shrubs, bamboos, and brushwood, we had during our stay cut a path through the cane thicket in the direction we now meant to travel. This proved invaluable; the loaded cart passed without impediment.

Beyond, the ground opened and lay tolerably level. In a few hours we reached the extreme limit of our coast territory.

We halted on the edge of a little wood. Behind it, to the right, rose the frowning cliffs of the mountain gorge; to the left ran the torrent, leaving between water and rock the narrow pass we called the Gap, and then hurrying onward to mingle with the sea. The place had its own stern music: water’s constant roar, wind stirring leaves, the occasional cry of a bird. Here, sound would warn us long before sight could.

The wood stood high and offered pleasant shelter within gunshot of the pass’s mouth. I chose it for our camp. We unpacked the cart and made our arrangements for safety and comfort, first examining the wood to see if it harbored dangerous animals. We found nothing worse than wild cats; we disturbed several hunting birds and small game, but they fled at our approach.

By the time dinner was ready we were fatigued. The day grew unusually sultry and oppressive, pressing heat onto our skin like a heavy cloth, so we rested until evening. When cooler air returned, strength returned with it. We pitched the tent and prepared for the next day, for I intended then to pass through the defile and make a longer excursion across the savannah than any we had yet undertaken.

Everything was made ready for an early start. My brave wife consented to remain in camp with Franz as her companion, while the three elder boys—and all the dogs except Juno—were to go with me. The night settled around our small human order: smoke from our fire drifting into the trees, salted meat hanging in memory and in store, and beyond the wood the Gap waiting, silent but not mute, its dark throat shaped to carry whatever sound the morning would bring.

Evening crept in on a slow, smoky tide, and with it the smell of supper—those bears’ paws laid to stew, giving up their rich, dark savour as patiently as a stockpot gives up marrow. The fire snapped and settled; fat hissed when it met the coals. We sat close, talking low, yet our ears kept ranging outward, as if we could pull our boys home by listening hard enough. Beyond the circle of light the island held its own music: wind combing the grass, distant surf working like a great mill, and—nearer—those small domestic sounds that had become our measure of safety: the cow’s occasional breathy low, the uneasy shifting of penned creatures, the dogs’ nails clicking and then stopping as they, too, listened.

At last, the sound we wanted broke through everything else: hoofbeats—first a faint drumming stitched into the night, then the unmistakable tramp and beat, quick and confident across open ground. We were on our feet at once. A shrill, ringing cheer came ahead of them like a thrown spark, and then the little troop appeared, cutting across the darkened plain at a sharp trot, the horses’ breaths visible in the firelight as they drew near.

They sprang down, laughing and talking all at once. The animals were loosed to crop and drink, and the riders crowded in, eager to show what they had dragged out of the day.

Franz and Jack looked as if they had dressed themselves in mischief: each had a young kid slung on his back, the four legs tied together so that the small hooves stuck out under their chins like ridiculous ornaments.

Fritz’s game-bag was queerer still—rounded lumps and sharp points, and now and then a distinct little movement that made the leather bulge.

“Hurrah, for the chase, father!” Jack shouted, his voice as loud as the dogs. “Nothing like real hunting after all. And just to see Storm and Grumble go along over a grassy plain! It is perfectly splendid! We soon tired out the little antelopes, and were able to catch them.”

“Yes, father,” Franz added, eager to keep pace with Jack’s triumphs, “and Fritz has two angora rabbits in his bag, and we wanted to bring you some honey. Only think! Such a clever bird—a cuckoo—showed us where it was!”

“My brothers forget the chief thing,” Fritz said, with that calm pride of his that always sounded like a plan already half-built. “We have driven a little herd of antelopes right through the Gap into our territory; and there they are, all ready for us to hunt when we like—or to catch and tame!”

“Well done!” I cried, and meant it with my whole heart. Yet gratitude rose first, before any delight in meat or trophies. “Here is indeed a list of achievements. But to your mother and me, the chief thing of all, is God’s goodness in bringing you safe back to us. Now, let us hear the whole story, that we may have a definite idea of your performances.”

Fritz began at once, orderly as a journal. They had ridden down Glen Verdant to the defile in our rocky barrier; the morning had been cool enough that the horses took the bit and wanted to fly. Beyond the Gap, they slowed and watched for game. From a grassy hill they had seen two herds grazing by a stream, not knowing at first whether they were goats, gazelles, or antelopes.

“We were about to gallop down and try to get a shot,” Fritz said, “when it struck me it would be wiser to drive the whole herd through the Gap into our own domain, where they would be shut up, as it were, in a park—free and yet within reach.”

He told it cleanly: the hard rush down the hill, the semicircle formed behind the larger herd, the dogs aiding with their sharp barks and quick feints. Shouts, cries, and the pounding of hooves kept the antelopes streaming along the water toward the narrow opening. Near the Gap they balked, bunching like sheep pressed toward a yard they did not trust.

“It was all we could do to prevent them from bolting past us,” Fritz said. “But at length one made a rush at the opening, and the rest following, they were soon all on the other side of the frontier—and inhabitants of New Switzerland.”

“Capital,” I said, clapping his shoulder. “Capital, my boy! But I don’t see what is to make them remain inhabitants of our domain, or to prevent them from returning through the Gap whenever they feel inclined.”

“Stop, father—you interrupted me too soon.” His eyes shone. “We thought of that possibility too, and provided against it. We stretched a long line right across the defile and strung on it feathers and rags and all sorts of other things. They danced and fluttered in the wind and looked so strange that I am perfectly certain the herd will never attempt to pass it. Levaillant says—”

He quoted from memory, pleased to prove that reading could be as useful as riding, and I praised him for it. Yet when the rabbits came out—two soft angoras, long-haired and trembling, their noses working fast at our smells—I could not keep from warning him.

“The antelopes are welcome,” I said, “but I cannot say the same for the rabbits you have there. They increase so rapidly that if you establish a colony of the little wretches your next difficulty will be to get rid of them.”

“True,” he admitted, “but my idea was to place them upon Whale Island, where they would find abundant food and in no way trouble us. May I not establish a warren there? It would be so useful. And my eagle caught them—three. I rewarded him with the one he killed, and these two I kept unhurt.”

Jack, who could never bear to be second in any telling, broke in. “Now, father, do listen to me and hear my story, or else Fritz will begin upon my adventures and tire you out with his rigmarole descriptions.”

“Certainly, Jack,” I said. “First and foremostly, how did you bring down those beautiful little animals you have there?”

“Oh, we galloped them down,” he said, as if that explained everything that ever needed doing. While Fritz was away after rabbits, the dogs had sniffed in the grass; then the little fawns sprang up and fled—bounding, skipping, swift as thrown stones. Storm and Grumble chased; the dogs soon fell behind; the boys pressed close on their horses until the fawns, terrified and exhausted, dropped into the grass and lay still.

“So we shouted to Fritz, and—”

“My dear boy,” I said, unable not to measure his numbers, “according to your statement Fritz must have been seven miles and a half off.”

Jack’s cheeks reddened. “Oh, well, father, perhaps we did not ride for quite a quarter of an hour, and of course I can’t say exactly how fast we were going; and then, you see, the fawns did not run in a straight line. At any rate Fritz heard us. And then we saw a wretch of a cuckoo, who led us ever so far out of our course by cuckooing and making faces at us and then hopping away. Franz declared it must be an enchanted princess, and so I thought I would rid it of its spell—but Fritz stopped me shooting it. He said it was a ‘Honey Indicator,’ leading us probably to a bees’ byke, so we spared its life. And presently—sure enough—it stopped close by a bees’ nest in a hollow tree.”

Jack’s face took on the greedy brightness of any boy promised sweetness. “We were in a great hurry to taste the honey, so I threw in a lot of lighted lucifer matches, but somehow it did not kill the bees at all—only made them awfully angry. They flew out in a body and stung me all over. I rushed to Storm and sprang on his back, but though I galloped away for bare life, it was an age before I got rid of the little wretches, and now my face is in a perfect fever. I think I will get mother to bathe it for me.”

Off he ran, still talking as he went, leaving Fritz and me to settle the fawns, examine the rabbits, and consider what our new stores might mean for the winter.

With the rabbits I determined to do as Fritz proposed: Whale Island would take them without letting them take us. The thought fitted another I had been turning over like a seed in the hand—that Whale Island might serve as a refuge, a kind of fortress if ever we were forced to quit our home in haste, and that in such a retreat we should be grateful for a steady supply of animal food.

I tried to interest the boys in the block of talc I had discovered, thinking of stoves and heat and future contrivances, but at that moment my wife’s voice called us to supper, and the smell from the pot silenced every other ambition.

The bears’ paws lay there, steaming, the skin and sinew softened to a gelatinous tenderness, and the broth around them glossy with fat. The odour was deep and hearty—woodsmoke, meat, salt, and that wild, almost sweet note that clings to game. They were so like human hands in shape that Jack could not resist a roguish “Fee-fo-fum,” but no one’s appetite suffered for the jest. We ate with the steady pleasure of bodies that have earned their food, and even as we chewed we listened—because here, sound was never mere background. A quiet night, a clean chorus of crickets, the surf’s regular breathing: these were as comforting as a full dish. Noise without meaning—sudden, metallic, furious—was what we feared, for it belonged to danger and to the old world we had escaped.

We fed the fire for the watch, crept into the tent, and slept soundly.

We worked hard in the days that followed: the bear’s meat was smoked, the fat melted and stored, bamboos gathered in quantity. Yet I wanted one more excursion before we settled into the nearer routines, and at early dawn I roused the boys.

Fritz mounted the mule; I rode Lightfoot; Jack and Franz took their usual steeds. The dogs bounded ahead, and we went first to the euphorbia to collect its gum, then to see whether the ostrich had deserted her eggs in the sand.

Ernest watched us go without the slightest look of regret—book and quiet suited him as much as chase suited Jack. He turned back to the tent to help his mother and to read, as if ink and paper were also provisions to be stored.

We rode down the Green Valley quickly, following our former track. We reached Turtle Marsh, filled our flasks, and came to the rising ground where Fritz had once seen mounted strangers. Today there was only wind, and the wind’s voice lay clean over the land.

Jack and Franz begged for a gallop. I let them press ahead, while Fritz and I turned to the euphorbia trees. From the incisions I had made, red gum had exuded and then hardened in the sun into resinous tears. I rolled the coagulated drops into little balls and stored them in a bamboo jar.

Fritz watched me closely. “Did you not tell me that the juice of that tree was poisonous, father? Why have you collected such a quantity?”

“I did indeed say so,” I answered. “It is a most deadly poison. The inhabitants of the Cape use it to poison springs where wild animals assemble to drink, and so slaughter them for their hides. I intend, however, to use it to destroy the apes should they again commit depredations, and also in preparing skins to protect them from insects.”

We rode on after the boys. They were still some distance away when suddenly four magnificent ostriches rose from the sand as if the earth itself had thrown them up. The sound of their rising was like heavy cloth snapped in the air—the rush of wings, the thud of feet, the startled snort of horses.

Jack and Franz saw them and, with shouts, drove them toward us. The male ran in front—shining black feathers, great tail plume waving; three females followed, ash-grey and swift. Their speed was astonishing. They were within gunshot before they noticed us.

Fritz had had the forethought to bind his eagle’s beak, so that if it struck the bird down it could not tear it. Now he unhooded and cast the falcon upward. The eagle climbed, circled, then dropped like a thrown knife onto the head of the foremost ostrich. The great bird, confused and terrified, faltered—just enough.

Jack was on him in an instant. He flung his lasso; the coils caught wing and leg; the ostrich crashed down in a storm of sand and feather.

The other three ostriches vanished into distance, and we leaped down to approach our prize. Even bound, it fought with such strength—kicking right and left with those terrible legs—that for a moment I nearly despaired of getting it home alive.

Then I remembered what darkness had done to it before. I seized my coat and hunting bag and flung them over its head, shutting out the light.

Its fury fell away as if someone had turned a key. The struggling stopped, and we could come close. We buckled a broad strip of sealskin around its body and fixed stout cords on either side for leading. Another cord looped its legs to prevent it from breaking into a full gallop. Then we freed it from the lasso.

“Do you know,” I asked the boys, “how the natives of India secure a newly captured elephant?”

“Oh, yes!” Fritz said at once. “They fasten him between two tame elephants.”

“We’ll do that to this fine fellow,” Jack added, delighted with the image, “and tame him double quick.”

“The only difficulty,” Jack went on, “is that we have no tame ostriches. However, I daresay Storm and Grumble will have no objection to perform their part.”

So we led Storm and Grumble up on either side of the recumbent bird and secured the cords to their girths. Jack and Franz mounted, each with a stout whip. We pulled away the covering from the ostrich’s eyes and stood ready.

For a few moments, as sight returned, it lay perfectly still. Then it sprang up and tried to dash forward. The thongs held. It was brought to its knees. It struggled, found the resistance unyielding, and at last seemed to accept the new law of its body. It set off at a sharp trot. The boys’ merry shouts made the air ring and echoed off the sand, an exuberant racket that would have been unbearable had it been the harsh, purposeless clangour of machines. This was living noise—breath, hooves, laughter, the honest uproar of effort—and it carried the island’s wild heart in it.

Still, even the ostrich could not run forever with two horses as anchors. Storm and Grumble refused at last to continue such a race across loose sand, and the bird came to a stand.

The boys had enjoyed their long run. I told them to walk the prisoner home slowly, while Fritz and I went back to the nest. The eggs were warm; the mother had left them only recently. We took about half, leaving the rest, and I packed the chosen eggs carefully in a large bag and slung it before me.

We soon overtook our advance guard and reached the tent without further incident.

My wife’s face, when she saw us, held astonishment and dismay together like two jars on one shelf.

“My dear husband,” she exclaimed, “do you think our provisions so abundant that you must scour the deserts to find some great beast to assist us to devour them? You must discover an iron mine next, for iron is what ostriches chiefly live on, is it not? Oh, I do wish you would be content with the menagerie you have already collected, instead of bringing in a specimen of every beast you come across. And this is such a useless monster!”

“Useless! Mother,” Jack cried, affronted on the ostrich’s behalf. “You would not say so had you seen him run. Why, he will be the fleetest courser in our stables. I am going to make a saddle and bridle for him, and in future he shall be my only steed. Then as for his appetite, father declares it is most delicate—he only wants a little fruit and grass, and a few stones and tenpenny nails to help his digestion.”

Jack’s instant claim of ownership stirred his brothers.

“Very well,” he said, quick as a knife. “Let us each take possession of the part of the ostrich we captured. Your bird, Fritz, seized the head; keep that. Father shall have the body. I’ll have the legs, and Franz a couple of feathers from the tail.”

“Come, come,” I said, amused despite myself. “Jack has a very good right to the ostrich, seeing that he brought it to the ground. If he succeeds in taming it and converting it into a saddle-horse it shall be his. From this time, therefore, he is responsible for its training.”

The day was too far advanced to think of setting out for Rockburg. We fastened the ostrich between two trees and spent the remainder of the evening preparing for our departure.

At early dawn our picturesque caravan moved homewards. The ostrich was still so refractory that we again marched him between Storm and Grumble. Since our best steeds were thus employed, the cow was harnessed to the cart laden with our treasures. Room was left for my wife in the cart. Jack and Franz rode Storm and Grumble. I rode Lightfoot, and Fritz brought up the rear on Swift.

At the mouth of the Gap we halted. The boys’ line of feathers and rags had served its purpose, but I replaced it with a stout palisade of bamboos. I also collected pipe-clay there, thinking ahead to winter work and the slow, satisfying labour of shaping vessels—pots that would one day hold grain, broth, and whatever sauces my wife could coax from our herbs.

When we reached the sugar-cane grove, we stopped to collect the peccary hams we had left to smoke. The meat had taken on that firm, deep colour that comes when smoke and time have done their quiet work, and its smell—sweet, resinous, meaty—clung to our fingers. My wife asked me to gather seeds of an aromatic plant in the neighbourhood that smelled of vanilla. I collected a good supply, already imagining those seeds dried and guarded like a small library of flavour, each one a word in a language the tongue remembers even when the world forgets.

We pushed on toward Woodlands, intending to rest there for the night. The tent went up; we lay down on beds of cotton and slept like people who have learned to be grateful for stillness.

In the morning we examined our farmyard. Everything looked prosperous: our domestic creatures multiplied, busy and content. The sight made us long more than ever for Rockburg, and we decided to hasten there with all possible speed. The number of pigs, goats, and poultry had increased greatly since our last visit; my wife wished especially to take two fine broods of chickens.

We found, too, that the herd of antelopes Fritz and Jack had driven through the Gap had settled nearby. Several times we saw the graceful animals browsing among the trees, their presence like a promise that our island could be both pantry and pasture if we treated it wisely.

While at the farm, we repaired stalls and our dwelling-room—making the former safer against wild beasts and the latter fit for our use when we returned. When all was arranged, we rested, and early the next morning completed our journey to Rockburg.

By midday we were home. We threw open doors and windows to let in fresh air; the animals were settled into their places; the cart’s mixed cargo unloaded and put away. The familiar sounds returned—our own threshold’s creak, the cluck and scratch of hens, the steady, comforting tap of tools against bamboo—each noise a mark of possession and peace.

As much time as I could spare, I gave to the ostrich. For the present we fastened him between two bamboo posts in front of the dwelling.

Then I turned to the eggs. I determined, if possible, to hatch them by artificial heat. I arranged a stove and kept it at a uniform temperature, placing the eggs upon it carefully wrapped in cotton wool, like treasures in a padded chest. Each day I listened—almost foolishly—for some change: a shift, a faint tapping, anything that might translate the silent future inside those shells into a sound my heart could understand.

Next morning Fritz and I took the boat first to Whale Island to establish the angora rabbits, and then to Shark Island, where we placed the dainty little antelopes. We left them in liberty and abundance, and returned quickly to cure the bears’ skins and add our provisions to the stores in the cellar.

On the way back we met Jack, making toward Rockburg in great glee with a basket that seemed too heavy for his pride alone. Inside lay an immense eel, sleek and muscular.

Ernest had set two lines the previous night; one had been dragged away, but the other held this splendid fellow. We ate half at dinner—firm white flesh, rich and sweet when properly cooked—and salted the remainder for storage. Even here, on a shore that had never heard a market bell, we kept the old human rhythm: eat, preserve, sow, and remember.

For a little while we returned to duties about the house. Thinking our verandah would be improved by creepers, I sowed around the foot of each bamboo pillar vanilla and pepper-seed, with others that would climb and spread. They would shade the house in summer and make it pleasanter to look upon—usefulness and beauty braided together, as they ought to be.

I made hen-coops for the hens and chicks we had brought from Woodlands, for I knew Knips and Fangs—those inquisitive rascals—might be tempted to make anatomical experiments detrimental to the youngsters’ welfare.

Ernest’s rat-skins were declared a nuisance indoors and hung outside. The odour was so powerful that even then Jack pretended to faint every time he passed them, making Franz shriek with laughter and my wife shake her head, half-scolding, half-amused.

The museum received additions: the condor and vulture were placed there to be stuffed when the rainy season gave us leisure. The mica and asbestos were brought in as well—not to lie idle, but to wait until I could use them for china-making and lamp-wicks.

After two days thus spent, we faced the larger tasks ahead: cultivating fields of wheat, barley, and maize; managing the ostrich eggs; taming the captives.

Agriculture, though least to our taste, was most important. We began there. The animals drew the plough, but digging and hoeing taxed us to the utmost. We worked two hours in the morning and two in the evening, and the words of Scripture proved themselves on our backs and in our hands: “In the sweat of thy face shalt thou eat bread.” Yet even that sweat had its own honest salt; it tasted of continuance.

In the intervals we devoted ourselves to the ostrich. But our efforts seemed vain. He remained untameable, wild-eyed and stubborn. I decided to try the plan that had subdued the refractory eagle: tobacco fumes.

The effect almost alarmed me. The ostrich sank to the ground and lay motionless. Slowly, at length, he rose and paced between the bamboo posts—subdued, but to my dismay he refused all food. Three days he pined, weaker each day, and the sight of so large a creature fading before us was like watching a fire go out when there is no wood left to add.

“Food he must have!” I cried. “Food he must have!”

My wife, practical where I was anxious, attempted an experiment. She mixed maize flour with butter and rolled it into balls. One she placed within the bird’s beak. He swallowed, stretched out his long neck, and looked for more as if the world had suddenly become edible again. A second, third, and fourth ball followed. His appetite returned; his strength came back like colour returning to a cheek.

The wildness did not return with it. His fierce nature seemed to have drained away, leaving a creature that could learn. He ate rice, guavas, maize, and corn readily, “washing it down,” as Jack said, with small pebbles—an action that astonished Franz until I explained that birds require such stones to grind their food within them, as smaller birds take gravel.

After a month of careful training, the ostrich would trot and gallop, obey the sound of our voice, and feed from our hand. He grew docile—so docile that our minds, always forced into invention, were taxed again: how does one saddle and bridle a bird?

A bit for his beak was impossible. I tried idea after idea and rejected them all. Then I remembered the power of light and its absence over him: how his fury had ceased when his eyes were covered, and how, with sight restored, strength returned.

I made a leathern hood reaching from neck to beak, with holes for eyes and ears. Over the eye-holes I contrived square flaps—blinkers—arranged with whalebone springs so they closed tightly of themselves. The reins connected to these blinkers, so the rider could raise them or let them fall.

When both blinkers were open, the ostrich galloped straight ahead. Close the right eye, and he turned left. Close the left, and he turned right. Shut both, and he stood stock still—as if darkness itself were a command.

Proud as I was of the contrivance, I could not test it fully until I had made a saddle. After several failures I succeeded in manufacturing one to my liking, something like an old-fashioned trooper’s saddle, peaked before and behind—for my greatest fear was always that the boys would fall. I set it on the shoulders near the neck and secured it with strong girths round the wings and across the breast, so it could not slip down the bird’s sloping back.

Thus, through smoke, sweat, seeds, and steady practice, we drew the wild into the circle of our domestic life, and found again—as if in an old story told in a new land—that survival is not merely taking, but learning how to keep, how to name, how to remember, and how to feed those we love.

My last pot had scarcely cooled on its bed of ashes when the sky gathered itself into a bruised, black frown. Great clouds shouldered up from the sea; wind worried the palms; rain came down in sheets that turned our paths to porridge and shut us indoors as firmly as if a door had been barred. The wet season had returned.

Even in our roomy dwelling, with tools to mend and stores to order, the hours began to clink and drag like an ill-made chain. The boys’ laughter dulled. The horses in the stable shifted and stamped, and the muffled drum of rain on leaf and thatch made a constant roof-song—at first comforting, then wearing, because it never varied, because it gave no quiet.
