When the downpour slackened, a brief ride could brighten faces; but the moment we were back under cover, the same heavy, watery hush returned, and with it the sinking of spirits.

Fritz, who had been watching us all with the keen eye he brought to every piece of work, said to me one morning as the storm muttered far off like distant cannon, “Father, why should we not build a canoe—something swifter, more manageable than what we have? I long for a light skiff that will skim where our boats labor.”

The notion ran through the house like a gust of clean air. Even the younger ones sat up. Only my wife—whose heart never grew friendly to the sea, however often it fed us—drew her hands closer about her apron as though to keep us all from sliding into the water.

“Our chances of drowning,” she declared, “are already great enough with the pinnace and the canoe. Must you add another temptation to that perfidious element?”

I could not blame her. The sea around us was as beautiful as polished steel and as treacherous as steel, too; and its voice—now a whisper, now a roar—could soothe the mind one hour and unsettle it the next. But I promised her, and I meant it, that we would build no frail toy.

“It shall be no cockle-shell,” I said. “I have in mind the Greenlander’s cajack—light, yes, but stout and safe. It will occupy the boys, and if we do it well, it will be a masterpiece of our little colony.”

So the boat-building began, and with it our house found a new rhythm. We set the skeleton first: whalebone for the ribs, split bamboo canes to stiffen the sides and curve the deck. The deck we carried nearly the whole length, leaving only a square opening where the paddler would sit, snug and low, as if the boat were part of his own body. All day the tools made their bright, honest sounds—saw rasping, knife clicking, mallet tapping—music that pushed the rain’s monotone into the background and reminded us that we were not merely waiting out weather, but shaping our future.

By the time the frame stood complete, the season itself seemed to relent. The clouds tore open, the sun returned with a brilliance that made every wet leaf flash, and we rolled our half-finished craft toward the door. The front opening was just wide enough to let her pass, as if the house had been built with this moment in mind. Outdoors, under full light, we drew the skin over her.

Seal-skin served us again—thick, tough, and willing. We stretched it tight and stitched it down; every seam we made watertight with caoutchouc, working it carefully into the needle holes until no bead of water could find a path. When at last she lay finished on the sand, she looked strange—like some sleek sea-creature turned to our service—yet she was so light that one hand could lift her. We launched her, and she bounded on the surface like an india-rubber ball, eager to be gone.

By unanimous vote Fritz was declared her owner. But my wife stood with her hands on her hips, gaze fixed on that small deck and the narrow opening.

“I will not hear of his entering it,” she said, “until I have contrived a swimming-dress. If a sharp rock punctures it, or the fin of a fish tears it, and it collapses—then what?”

I did not consider our cajack the bubble she imagined. Still, I knew what fear can do to a household: it can sour every meal, make even the gentlest night-sounds feel like threats. So I offered my help.

We made the garment like a double waistcoat: linen coated with a solution of india-rubber, every seam painted again with caoutchouc until the whole was airtight. We left a small opening so air could be forced between outer cloth and lining; inflated, it would buoy the wearer like a bladder. It was comical to look at, and I confess I doubted its perfection. Yet the care my wife gave it—fingers pressing each seam, ear turned as if to listen for any faint sigh of leaking air—was a kind of tenderness in itself.

Meanwhile the island around our home was singing its own busy song. Seeds we had scattered were up and thriving; the verandah had begun to change from bare structure to green room, as creepers climbed the pillars and breathed out sweet scents in the heat. Birds filled the air with layered calls; even the insects seemed to keep time. After weeks of rain’s unbroken beat, the many-voiced day felt like health returning.

At last the swimming-dress was ready. One fine afternoon Fritz offered, solemn as if he were volunteering for a trial of arms, to prove it before us all. We gathered on the beach. He donned the odd waistcoat, we forced air in until it stood away from him, puffed and stiff, and his brothers burst into such roars of laughter that the gulls answered with harsh cries overhead.

Fritz marched into the water with exaggerated dignity, then slipped into the cajack, took his paddle, and moved off—swift, balanced, low to the surface. The little craft cut the bay cleanly; the water made its quick, whispering hiss along the skin; and the sounds of shore—the boys’ shouts, the dogs’ barking, my wife’s anxious call—fell behind him in a widening ring. We followed in one of our boats. He reached Shark Island, turned neatly, and came back as if he had been born in that narrow deck.

The experiment succeeded so perfectly that Ernest, Jack, and little Franz—though they had laughed loudest—begged their mother for swimming-dresses of their own.

Since we were already at Shark Island, we went to see the colonists we had settled there the autumn before—our animals, our small beginnings of order. All were well. We could tell, by the neat, sharp prints, that the antelopes had found and used the shelter we had built them. Having nothing more urgent to do, we scattered maize and salt in places they frequented, then strolled along the other shore.

The beach there was a cabinet of curiosities: shells of delicate shape and shining interior, bits of coral like frozen lace. The boys gathered these for their museum, their voices rising and falling over the surf’s steady breath. Along the waterline lay seaweed in many colors—olive, red-brown, deep green—and my wife, with the practical eye that always found food in what looked like mere wreckage, had the boys collect armfuls and stow them in the boat.

As we rowed back, I noticed she had selected certain long leaves with edges notched like a saw. She held them aside as carefully as if they were herbs. At home she washed them, then dried them in the oven, and did it with such quiet secrecy that my curiosity became a constant itch.

“Are those leaves to be a substitute for tobacco?” I teased her at last. “Do you long for its refreshing smell?”

She smiled—everyone knew her dislike of that weed—and she answered lightly, “Do you not think a mattress stuffed with these would be wonderfully cool in summer?”

But the little twinkle in her eyes told me I would not learn the truth so easily.

Not long after, the boys and I returned from a long, tiring expedition. We flung ourselves down in the verandah, grateful for the shade and the soft stir of air through vine and leaf. The island’s soundscape was gentler then: the low cooing of birds, the distant wash of waves, the soft cluck of our fowls settling—no harsh metal clamor, no grinding engines, only living things and the wind’s own instruments. It soothed the mind in a way no amount of rest alone could manage.

From within, my wife called, “Could any of you enjoy a little jelly?”

She appeared carrying a porcelain dish that looked almost too fine for our rough life, and in it a clear, trembling jelly—so transparent it caught the light and held it. Cut with a spoon and set before us, it quivered like captured water.

“Ambrosia!” cried Fritz after the first taste.

He was right. It was delicious: cool on the tongue, with a clean sea-freshness tamed by citrus, sweetened but not cloying, leaving behind a faint perfume that made the mouth want more. We ate every portion she had brought and still stared at her as if she had performed magic.

“Aha!” she laughed, watching our astonishment. “Is not this an excellent substitute for tobacco—far more refreshing than that nasty weed? Behold the produce of my mysterious seaweed.”

“My dear wife,” I said, “it is a masterpiece of culinary art. But where did you meet with such a dish? What put it into your head?”

“While staying with my Dutch friends at the Cape,” she answered, “I often saw it. I recognized the leaves at once on Shark Island. Once you know, it is simple: soak the leaves in water—fresh water, changed every day—for a week. Then boil them for hours with orange juice, citron, and sugar. That is all.”

All! As if patience and exactness were nothing. Yet I understood: like preserving a dialect in a family, such recipes survive only when someone repeats them faithfully, not in vague translation but in the old cadence of practice. The taste in my mouth seemed to carry her past with it—Cape kitchens, borrowed words, remembered measures—brought alive here among palms and sea.

The boys thanked her heartily and declared they must go at once to Shark Island for more leaves. I agreed to accompany them; I wished to examine our plantations there.

Everything flourished. Palms and mangroves had shot up with a speed that still astonished me, and seeds I had tossed idly into rock clefts had germinated, promising to cloak bare boulders with green. We even found, higher among the rocks, a bright spring of delicious water. Around it were many footprints: antelopes had been drinking there, as we had hoped.

Content with that, we grew anxious about Whale Island. When we landed, it was clear at a glance that the rabbits had multiplied. Young shoots bore the ragged signatures of many little teeth. Only the cocoanut palms had been spared.

Such depredations could not be allowed. With the boys’ help I built a hedge of prickly thorn around each stem, a simple barrier but enough to discourage most nibblers. Before we embarked, I noticed some of the seaweed there had been gnawed too. Wondering what sweetness or salt had tempted the rabbits, I gathered a little to examine at home.

The whale’s skeleton still lay where it had been stranded, but now the birds and weather had picked it clean and bleached the bones to a perfect whiteness. As I stood among those pale arches, listening to the wind blow through them like a low flute, a new plan formed. The vertebrae joints looked as if they could serve in a machine.

I separated ten or twelve and rolled them down to the boat. We towed them after us like a strange necklace trailing in the water. All the way home my mind worked on a crushing-machine—something to save our hands and arms, to grind and press what needed pressing. Stone was beyond me, but bone and wood might answer.

Next morning I went out to choose a tree for the wooden blocks I would need. I expected company, but when I reached the stables I found the boys had slipped away with guns and traps, leaving me to choose between our bull and buffalo. I took Storm—reliable, if not graceful—and set off with Floss and Bruno trotting beside.

Crossing the bridge, I passed the cassava field and stopped short. It had been overrun and laid waste. The plants were torn and trampled, the ground rooted as if by ploughshares driven mad. I bent to study the tracks; they resembled those of pigs.

Annoyed, I followed the trail. It led toward our old potato field, then grew faint. I hunted back and forth, listening for grunts, for the crackle of brush. At last Floss and Bruno began to bark in a sharp, excited way that meant discovery. Ahead, in a clearing, stood our old sow with her whole family—bristling, teeth showing, grunting so savagely that even our dogs hesitated to close.

I raised my gun and fired twice into the herd. Two fell; the rest bolted, and the dogs rushed after them. I called the dogs back, lifted the fallen pigs, and went on through the forest. Soon I found a tree suited to my purpose, marked it, and returned home.

Ernest, who had remained behind, helped me flay the young porkers. We handed them to my wife, who immediately began planning supper—salt rubbed into flesh, fire laid just so, herbs chosen with that instinct that turned our island’s rough abundance into comfort. I hoped the other boys would return by then.

They did—late, with the sound of hoofs like sudden thunder. Jack came first, wild with triumph, astride his two-legged steed, the ostrich. Behind him, Fritz and Franz followed more steadily, game-bags bulging on their cruppers. The contents were displayed at once: four birds, a kangaroo, twenty musk-rats, a monkey, two hares, and half a dozen beaver rats lay in a heap before me like the day’s accounting.

Then Fritz, without explanation, threw down a bundle of thistles.

The boys were almost feverish with excitement. Jack could hardly contain himself.

“Oh, father, you cannot think what grand fun hunting on an ostrich is! We flew like the wind. Sometimes I could scarcely breathe we went so fast, and I had to shut my eyes because of the rush of air. You must make me a mask with glass eyes to ride with, or I shall be blinded one day.”

“Indeed,” I replied. “I must do no such thing.”

“Why not?” he demanded, honestly astonished.

“For two reasons. First, because I am not obliged to do whatever you demand; and second, because you are quite capable of doing it yourself.” Then, seeing their bright faces, I added what was due. “I congratulate you on your supply of game. You have worked hard. But I wish you would let us know when you plan such long expeditions. You forget that, though you know you are safe, we at home pass the hours in anxiety.”

I sent them to care for their animals and wash, and promised supper.

Supper was worth the wait. My wife had turned the young pork into a roast that filled the house with a rich, crackling fragrance, fat hissing where it met heat, skin browning into a lacquered crust. She served it with what we had—roots and greens from our fields—and we washed it down with fragrant mead whose honeyed scent seemed to catch sunlight and hold it. The very act of eating—chewing, savoring, listening to the contented silence between remarks—restored us more than any lecture could have done.

While we ate, Fritz gave the day’s account. They had set traps near Woodlands and caught musk-rats by tempting them with small carrots. Other traps, baited with fish and earthworms, had taken several beaver rats and even a duck-billed platypus. Hunting and fishing had filled the rest of the day; Jack boasted, with reason, of the kangaroo he had run down with his swift courser. They had not forgotten the garden either: Fritz handed his mother cuttings of cinnamon and sweet-apple trees, treasures that would root our future meals more deeply into this soil.

At last Fritz asked me to examine the thistles. He believed, he said, they were used in making wool.

He was right. I recognized them at once as fullers’ teazle, with sharp little hooks on stem and leaf—the very plant used to raise the nap of cloth.

We resolved to rise early and attend to preparing the booty. The boys’ eyelids were already heavy, their voices thick with tired satisfaction, so I ended the day with evening devotions, grateful for the quiet that followed: not the dead quiet of the rainy season, but the earned hush after work well done, when even the sea seems to speak more softly.

The number of creatures we had killed made skinning a long, unpleasant business. Standing before that array, I decided I would not spend our strength wastefully. Among the ship’s stores—in the surgeon’s chest—I found a large syringe. With a few alterations it could serve a new purpose. I fitted two valves inside the tube, perforated the stopper, and in my hands I had a powerful air-pump.

The boys stared when I took up the kangaroo.

“Skin a kangaroo with a squirt?” one of them cried, and laughter broke out.

I made no answer. I hung the kangaroo by its hind legs from a tree branch, made a small incision, inserted the mouth of the syringe, and began to force air between skin and flesh. Slowly the hide distended, changing the animal into a grotesque, swelling shape. I kept pumping until the skin was nearly separated. Then a bold cut down the belly and a few quick touches where the fibres still held, and the hide came away.

“What a splendid plan!” the boys shouted.

“But why does it do it?” they asked, wonder turning quickly into the hunger to understand.

“For a simple reason,” I said. “The skin is fastened to the flesh only by slender fibres. Between them are countless little chambers. When we force air in, those fibres stretch, and at last—elastic as they are—they crack. The skin is then free and may be drawn off easily. The Greenlanders have long known this; they even distend a seal’s skin to tow the animal more easily ashore.”

We treated the remaining animals the same way, and in a couple of days all the skins were off and prepared for use.

Then I called the boys to help procure wood for the crushing-machine. We set out with saws, ropes, axes, and other tools. Reaching the tree I had marked, I sent Fritz and Jack up to lop off the highest heavy branches, so that when the trunk fell it would not damage its neighbors. When they came down, Fritz and I attacked the stem with a long saw like those used in a saw-pit, one on each end. The teeth bit steadily. When the cut was deep enough, we fastened ropes to guide the fall and sawed again.

It was laborious. The forest around us carried every sound—saw-groan, rope-creak, our own breathing—so that even our effort seemed amplified by the living acoustics of the place. At last we took the ropes and pulled together. The trunk cracked, swayed, tottered, then fell with a crash that sent birds up in a startled storm.

We lopped the boughs, then sawed the trunk into blocks four feet long. It took us two days to cut down and divide the tree, and on the third we carted home four large and two small blocks. With these and the whale-vertebrae joints, I soon completed my machine.

While I labored over this, I paid too little attention to our fields of grain. One evening, when the fowls returned with obvious indifference to their usual feed and their crops full, suspicion struck me. They had come from the direction of our cornfield.

I hurried there, half-expecting ruin. Instead, joy met me: the grain stood ripe. The heads bent heavy; the color had changed to that satisfying gold that promises bread, porridge, and quiet days ahead.

My wife, seeing at once how much work had sprung up overnight, was startled into real distress. Reaping and threshing would now be added to fishing, salting, pickling—already enough to keep her hands moving from dawn to dark.

“Only think,” she cried, “of my beloved potatoes and manioc roots! It is time to take them up. How are we to manage, with all this press of work? I cannot see.”

“Do not be downhearted,” I told her. “There is no immediate hurry about the manioc. Digging potatoes in this light soil is easier than in Switzerland. And as for planting more, we need not if we leave the younger plants in the ground. For the grain we shall harvest after the Italian fashion—less economical perhaps, but quick. Since we have two crops a year here, we can afford not to be too particular.”

Without delay I leveled a broad space of firm, clayey ground for a threshing-floor. We sprinkled it with water, rolled, beat, and stamped it. As the sun dried it, we watered again and repeated the treatment until it lay flat, hard, and smooth.

Then we slung our largest wicker basket between Storm and Grumble, armed ourselves with reaping hooks, and went into the field. I told the boys not to fuss about the straw’s length. They were to grasp the corn where convenient without stooping, wind one stalk around their handful, and toss it into the basket. Speed mattered now.

The plan delighted the boys; the basket filled again and again. Soon the field was left with tall, headless stubble. My wife was horrified.

“This is dreadful!” she cried. “You have left ears on short stalks, and that splendid straw is wasted! I do not approve of your Italian fashion at all.”

“It is not waste,” I said. “The Italians use high stubble for cattle, letting them feed among it and eat what grain remains. Afterwards, when grass grows up through it, they mow it all together for winter fodder. And now we shall thresh in Italian fashion too—it will spare arms and backs.”

We laid the little sheaves in a great circle on the threshing-floor. The boys mounted Storm, Grumble, Lightfoot, and Hurry, and set off at a brisk trot, laughing and calling as they went. Round and round they rode, trampling and stamping grain free, while dust and chaff rose in clouds. My wife and I worked with hayforks, lifting and turning the sheaves so the animals would pass over them again and again. Now and then the beasts snatched mouthfuls of what they were beating out; we let them, remembering the old command: Thou shalt not muzzle the ox that treadeth out the corn.

For winnowing we waited for a strong land- or sea-breeze, then threw the threshed corn high with shovels. The wind carried chaff away in a pale stream and the heavier grain fell clean. Our poultry visited constantly, clucking and pecking with avid interest, until my wife had to keep them back.

“Let them enjoy themselves,” I told her. “What we lose in grain we gain in flesh. I anticipate delicious chicken-pie, roast goose, and boiled turkey.”

When the harvest was housed, we found we had reaped sixty-, eighty-, even a hundred-fold what we had sown. Our garner was filled with good store. Expecting a second harvest, we at once prepared the field for sowing again and began mowing down the stubble. As we did, flocks of quails and partridges came to glean among the scattered ears. We caught only a few, but resolved that next season we would spread nets and take them in quantity.

My wife’s satisfaction returned when she saw the straw carried home and stacked neatly. Our maize crop, which we did not thresh like the other grain, gave us soft leaves for stuffing mattresses; the stalks, when burnt, left ashes rich in alkali—useful in their own way. I changed what we sowed next to rye, barley, and oats, hoping they would ripen before the rainy season returned.

Just as we finished these agricultural labors, shoals of herring appeared. This year we pickled only two barrels. But we showed far less mercy to the seals that arrived directly afterwards. We hunted them vigorously, needing their skins for many purposes—most especially to complete the cajack.

On the tiny deck of that little vessel I had made a sort of magazine to hold pistols, ammunition, water, and provisions, and I intended to cover it with seal-skin so it would be quite watertight. A couple of harpoons furnished with seal bladders were to hang alongside—small preparations, perhaps, but in our life every careful detail was the difference between a bold excursion and a reckless one.

The morning Fritz chose for his first true venture in the cajack arrived with a hush that seemed made on purpose for boldness—a sea smoothed to glass, the bay breathing in slow, green sighs, and every gull-cry hung for an instant above the water before falling away. Yet the stillness did not soften the comic sight of my eldest as he squeezed himself into the tight little craft in the strange swimming dress his mother had contrived for him—trousers, jacket, cap, all close and clinging—and then began to inflate himself by puffing and blowing into the skin covering, as if he meant to become part boat, part boy.

He crouched there, cheeks ballooning, shoulders rising, his whole figure swelling until—like the fabled frog—he seemed in danger of bursting from his own ambition. The lads shouted with laughter. Even my wife, who had stitched and fastened every seam with anxious ingenuity, could not keep her mouth from curving; though she tried to hide the smile behind the very hands that had made his ridiculous bravery possible.

I had our larger boat brought out and kept ready, because fatherly pride never quite smothers fatherly caution, and I wished my wife to see that if the sea decided to prove itself less tender than the morning looked, we were prepared to go at once after him.

From a shelving strip of shore we slid the cajack into the bay. It floated as lightly as a leaf on a bowl of jade. Fritz set his paddles and began his trials: first a straight dart that cut the water with arrowy neatness, then a wheel to the right, then to the left; and, as if the sea were a domestic floor and he meant to dance upon it, he leaned and turned until the little craft lay half on its side.

My wife cried out, the sound sharp as a snapped string in that wide quiet; but the cajack only kissed the water and rose again. It neither capsized nor drank in the sea. Fritz recovered, grinning, and skimmed away, encouraged by our cheers.

Then, warmed by triumph and the dangerous ease of it, he pushed too boldly into the strong pull of Jackal River’s mouth. The current caught him with indifferent strength and carried him outward as if the bay had only been a cradle, and the open sea its true bed.

That was more than I had bargained for. I sprang into the boat with Ernest and Jack, and we bent to the oars. Behind us my wife urged us on with a voice that tried to command the elements by sheer will and, between commands, could not keep from naming the cajack what she felt it was—“that horrid soap-bubble”—as though words might pop it and end her fear.

Outside the bay we reached the rocks where once the wreck had lain, and we scanned the water until the breadth of it felt like mockery. There was no sign—only the vast, breathing surface and the changeable music of wind and tide. At last, far off, a faint puff of smoke lifted, and then came the crack of a pistol: sound, that faithful messenger over water, running to us quicker than sight.

We answered with our own shot. Another replied. We steered by those brief, harsh syllables in the air, and soon, above the rush of our own oars, we heard Fritz’s cheerful halloo. The cajack shot out from behind a point of land as if it had been hiding there for sport, and we drew alongside.

“Come to this rocky beach!” he cried, eyes bright with the kind of pride that makes a boy forget his own peril. “I have something to show you.”

We grounded the boat and climbed out—and there, among the rocks, lay a young walrus, well grown, thick-limbed, and quite dead, the skin still damp and shining, the very heft of it proclaiming how hard the sea had worked to build such flesh.

“Did you kill this creature, my dear Fritz?” I exclaimed; and though I spoke in wonder, my glance went instinctively to the thickets and the sea beyond, half expecting some unknown hunter—some naked islander, perhaps—to step forward and claim the prize.

“To be sure, father! Don’t you see my harpoon?” He held it up, indignant at doubt. “Why do you doubt it?”

“I scarcely know,” I replied, and could not help laughing, for success so swift and so strangely apt for an amateur Greenlander startled me. “I congratulate you, my boy. But you have alarmed us. You should not have gone out of the bay. I left your mother in grievous trouble.”

He sobered for an instant, and his honesty came through the triumph. “Indeed, father, I had no idea of passing out of sight. Once in the current I was carried along and could not help myself. Then I came upon a herd of walruses, and I so longed to make a prize of one that I forgot everything else. I chased them when I was beyond the pull of the current, and when I got near enough I harpooned this one—twice. He swam slower than the rest. He tried to take refuge among these rocks and expired. I landed and scrambled to him—but I gave him the contents of my pistol first, before going close up. I remembered the big serpent’s parting fling at you, Jack.”

“You ran a very great risk,” I told him. “The walrus is inoffensive, but wounded it can turn furious and, with those tusks, can destroy a stout boat. Thank God for your safety. I value that above a thousand such creatures. Now—what shall we do with him? He must be fourteen feet long, though not full grown.”

Fritz ran his hand along the massive head with an artist’s longing. “We cannot move such weight from among these rocks,” he admitted, “but do let me carry away the head with those grand snow-white tusks. I should like to fasten it on the prow of the cajack and name it the Sea-horse.”

“We must certainly carry away the ivory,” I said. The air had thickened, close and sultry as breath held too long. “But make haste. A storm is brewing.”

“The head! The head!” Jack cried, already seeing a trophy more splendid than sense. “Think how it will look on the cajack!”

“And think how it will smell,” Ernest added dryly, pinching his nose as if imagination alone offended him. “What a treat for the steersman when it begins to putrefy.”

“Oh, we will prepare for that,” Fritz insisted, his mind already arranging processes like recipes. “It shall be soaked and cleaned and dried until it is as hard as a wooden model. It shall not offend your delicate nose in the least, Ernest.”

Ernest, ever ready with a fact, observed, “I supposed the walrus to be peculiar to the Arctic regions.”

“And so it is,” I answered, even as we set to work. “Though they may occasionally be seen elsewhere; these may have wandered far. On the eastern coast of Africa there is a smaller kind called the dugong—long incisor teeth but not tusks, and more like a seal than a walrus.”

With no proper tools for such butchery, our labour became slow and stubborn. The knife dragged; the sinews resisted; the thick skin yielded only in long, reluctant strips. Yet necessity teaches hands to become clever, and we managed to sever the great head and cut away lengths of hide. Fritz, flushed with accomplishment, remarked that henceforth the cajack must be provisioned like a true hunting craft—with a stout knife, a hatchet, and, he added with practical foresight, a small compass in a glass-topped box fixed before the opening where the steersman sits. I promised him it should be so.

When the work was finished, I proposed to take Fritz and his frail boat aboard ours, so we might return together. But he begged earnestly to go alone as he had come; he longed to be our avant-courier, to announce our approach and his own victory to his mother. At last I yielded. The cajack slipped away ahead, a small dark speck on the bright water, while we followed more slowly, burdened with the walrus head and hides.

Then the weather, which had been a friendly stage for heroics, changed its temper. Black clouds gathered thick and fast. The air dropped as if the sky had leaned closer. A tremendous storm broke upon us with startling suddenness. Fritz vanished from sight; he was beyond our reach.

We buckled on our swimming belts and lashed ourselves to the boat. Waves rose and broke over us like moving walls. Wind tore the surface into white fury. Rain fell in torrents that stung the skin and drowned the softer sounds, leaving only the harsh, relentless roar. Lightning flared livid across the darkness, making the sea for one instant a sheet of hammered metal and, the next, a blind gulph again.

Ernest and Jack faced it nobly, their young bodies braced and their voices steady when they spoke. I watched our boat—our frail, faithful bark—lift and settle with a buoyancy that gave me courage, even while dread sat cold in my chest. The storm swept on and passed as abruptly as it had come. The sky began to clear; yet the sea continued its long after-anger, and for a time every wave still threatened to throw us down and finish what the wind had begun.

For ourselves I did not utterly despair. But for Fritz—alone, ahead, in that narrow shell—I could not keep hope from breaking into prayer. In my heart I felt him lost, and I begged only for strength, if the worst should be, to say, “Thy will be done.”

At last we rounded the point and entered Safety Bay again, drawing near our little harbour. And then—what a shock of joy, what a mercy so sudden it felt like the world turning back upon itself—we saw my wife kneeling on the shore with Fritz beside her, and little Franz too, their heads bowed, their hands clasped, praying so earnestly for our deliverance that they did not perceive us until we cried out.

They looked up. My wife’s face, wet with rain and tears alike, changed in a breath from fear to gratitude. We sprang ashore to one another, and the joy of that meeting—after the storm’s brutal noise—was like stepping into a warm room where the kettle sings. Together we gave thanks for the lives spared.

Back at Rockburg, we stripped off our drenched clothes and put on warm, dry garments that smelled of smoke and stored wool. My wife, as if to anchor our shaken spirits in familiar labour, set about a meal with brisk hands. The ordinary miracle of food—its steam, its salt, its comfort—rose between us and the memory of the lightning. We ate, and in the secure ritual of sitting together, we began to speak calmly of the danger we had just survived, as if naming it could keep it from returning.

Afterwards the walrus head was carried to our workshop, and there my wife’s domestic ingenuity met Fritz’s hunter’s vanity and Ernest’s fastidious nose in a kind of household treaty. It underwent a thorough cleaning—scraped and washed, soaked, and tended as carefully as any curing meat—then embalmed and dried until it hardened and lost the offence of decay. Ere long it was fixed upon the prow of the cajack, the snow-white tusks gleaming, and the little boat, crowned with that grim splendour, did indeed look imposing. The strips of hide, tanned and prepared, became valuable leather—another proof, in our island life, that nothing useful should be wasted and every creature taken must be honoured by complete employment.

The storm had not limited its mischief to our nerves. Heavy rain flooded the streams and battered the crops that ought to have been housed before the rainy season. The bridge over Jackal River was partly broken down. Water tanks and pipes needed repair. Days filled with work—mending, shoring up, restoring—until the island’s damage felt stitched and bound again into the routines that kept us alive.

One day near the cascade, while we laboured, we found a number of dark-red berries scattered on the ground, about the size of hazel-nuts, each with a small leafy coronet at the tip. The boys, lured by colour and the careless appetite of youth, tasted them at once. A chorus of angry exclamations followed—spitting and spluttering. Even Knips rejected them with offended dignity. They would have been thrown aside in contempt, had not the smell—warm, sharp, strangely familiar—drawn me to examine them more closely.

The scent, carried on the damp air, seemed to cut through the roar of the cascade like a thin, bright note. I decided they were cloves.

At once some plants were set in the nursery garden. My wife’s eyes lit with the quiet triumph of a cook who has been granted a new language for the tongue. That excellent spice, she said, would flavour boiled rice and other dishes in place of pepper—no small variety for a family whose meals, however wholesome, could too easily become monotonous. Soon, when the pot steamed on the hearth, the clove-scent rose through Rockburg like a memory one could not quite translate into words: not merely “spice,” but the suggestion of far ports, of kitchens left behind, of names for tastes that in our island life might otherwise die out.

We had brought a good supply of clay from the bed near Falconhurst, and I proposed to use it for making aqueducts. The recent rains had shown how quickly our young corn responded to water, and I resolved to irrigate the fields with the drainage from our crushing-mill. Thus disaster instructed us: the island’s violence became the argument for better channels, better storage, better habit.

The fishing season returned successfully. Salmon, sturgeon, herring—silver bodies flashing, scales bright as coins—rewarded our annual efforts, and the store-room once more looked like security itself. Much as I wished for a constant supply of fresh fish, I could not accept Jack’s naive proposal that we tether a shoal of salmon by the gills to the bottom of the bay, as we had secured the turtles. Even in this life of contrivances, there are limits that nature insists upon, and the idea made us laugh in spite of ourselves.

Quiet days followed, and I noticed a restlessness in the boys—an impatience with the steady, repetitive labour of farm and repairs. Routine, which keeps a family alive, can also dull young minds; their bodies longed for movement, their spirits for novelty. They were hungry, not merely for food, but for change.

At length Fritz spoke what all three felt. “Father, we want a quantity of hurdles, and we have scarcely any more bamboos. Had we not better get a supply from Woodlands? And you said you wished you had more of that fine clay—we might visit the Gap at the same time.”

I had no objection. It was soon settled that Fritz, Jack, and little Franz should go together. Ernest, who felt no great desire to accompany his brothers, would remain with his mother and me and assist in the construction of a sugar-mill—an undertaking I had long contemplated, and one that promised to turn our cane into something more lasting than mere stalks in a field.

Before they started, Fritz begged bear’s meat from his mother “to make pemmican.”

“And what may pemmican be?” she asked, half amused, half mistrustful, already imagining the kitchen’s limits being tested by one of her sons’ schemes.

He answered with the air of a young man reciting from the chronicles of explorers. “It is food carried by the fur-traders of North America on their long journeys—bear or deer flesh first cooked, then pounded or ground to powder. It is portable and nourishing.”

She consented, as she said, “to humour him,” though her face showed little faith in the outcome. Yet, because she was wise enough to know that men who travel need more than bread—need belief, need the comfort of a provision prepared by familiar hands—she set to the task. Over two days the bear’s meat was cooked, dried, and reduced as best we could manage, the work filling Rockburg with a dense savour—smoke, fat, and the deep, lingering scent of wilderness brought into a domestic place. Fritz watched with reverence, as if the very pounding were a rite.

They were ready at last. As they loaded the cart, I observed Jack quietly slip a basket containing several pigeons under the packages.

“Oh, oh,” I thought, “the little fellow has doubts about that pemmican, and thinks a tough old pigeon would be preferable.”

The weather was exquisite when they set off—clear, bright, and so pleasant it felt almost unearned after the storm. We admonished them, as parents must, to prudence and caution; and they departed in the highest spirits. Storm and Grumble drew the cart and were ridden by Fritz and Franz. Hurry carried Jack swiftly across the bridge in advance, followed by Floss and Bruno, barking at his heels.

The sugar-mill occupied us for several days. We built it much like our other mills, and I need not describe it here. Yet I will say that the rhythm of its planned labour—the turning, the crushing, the promise of sweetness—comforted my wife more than she admitted. The sound of honest work is its own kind of music, and far kinder to the spirit than the shriek of storm or the sudden crack of gunfire.

On the evening of the first day, as we rested in the porch at Rockburg, our talk naturally returned to the absentees. We wondered what they were doing and how they fared. Ernest looked rather mysterious and hinted that he might have news by the next morning.

As he spoke, a bird alighted on the dove-cot and entered. In the failing light I could not tell whether it was ours or a stray. Ernest sprang up at once, eager, and went to see. He returned in a few minutes with a scrap of paper in his hand.

“News, father! The very latest news by pigeon-post, mother!”

“Well done, boys!” I cried, taking the note. “What a capital idea!” And I read:

“Dearest parents and Ernest,

“A brute of a hyaena has killed a ram and two lambs. The dogs seized it. Franz shot it. It is dead and skinned. The pemmican isn’t worth much, but we are all right. Love to all.

“Fritz

“Woodlands, 15th instant”

“A true hunter’s letter!” I laughed, though my wife’s face tightened at the word hyaena. “What exciting news. When does the next post come in, Ernest?”

“Tonight, I hope,” he said, delighted with the system.

My wife sighed and declared she would much rather wait to hear everything when her sons were safe home again than receive these sharp glimpses into danger carried on wings. Yet even she could not deny that the small, soft flutter of a pigeon’s arrival was a sound far gentler than the silence of not knowing.

Thus the winged letter-carriers kept us informed, day by day, of the outline of adventures which were afterwards told in fuller detail.

As the boys approached the farm at Woodlands, they were startled by a sound like human laughter repeated again and again—an ugly imitation, too wild and too hollow to be truly mirth. The oxen grew uneasy, the dogs drew close and growled, and the ostrich bolted outright, carrying Jack into the rice swamp as if panic were a whip.

The laughter continued. The animals became unmanageable.

“Something is very far wrong!” cried Fritz. “I cannot leave the animals; but while I unharness them, do you, Franz, take the dogs and advance cautiously to see what is the matter.”

Without hesitation Franz slipped into the bushes with his gun, Floss and Bruno tight behind him. Through an opening in the thicket, at a distance of about forty paces, he saw the cause: an enormous hyaena in a state of mad excitement, dancing around a newly killed lamb and uttering that ghastly, hysterical laughter that had pealed through the forest.

The creature ran back and forth, rose on its hind legs, whirled round and round, nodded its head, and performed frantic, ludicrous antics—horror masquerading as play. Franz kept his presence of mind. He waited until the hyaena calmed enough to begin tearing at its prey with growls that turned the air sour. Then, firing steadily both barrels, he broke its foreleg and wounded it in the breast.

Meanwhile Fritz had unyoked the oxen and secured them to trees and now hurried to Franz’s aid. The dogs and the dying beast were locked in mortal struggle; but though it severely wounded both Floss and Bruno, it soon succumbed. It was dead when the boys reached the spot.

Their shout of triumph guided Jack back from the swamp. Before any inspection or boasting, their first care was for the dogs. They dressed the wounds as best they could, hands gentled by affection even in the midst of blood and danger. Only then did they examine the hyaena: as large as a wild boar, a mane of long stiff bristles on its neck, grey fur marked with black, jaws of extraordinary strength, muscular thighs, claws sharp and powerful. But for Franz’s timely shots, it would certainly have been more than a match for the dogs.

After unloading the cart at the farm, they returned for the carcass of the tiger-wolf—as it is sometimes called—and spent the remainder of the day skinning it. When the carrier pigeon had been dispatched to Rockburg, they lay down on bearskin rugs, tired enough to sleep, yet full enough of life to dream of adventures past and future.

The following day they devised a scheme to survey the shores of Wood Lake and mark the spots where the surrounding marsh could be crossed. Fritz took the cajack; the other boys kept to shore. Whenever firm footing reached the water’s edge, they planted a tall bamboo bearing a high bundle of reeds and branches—a visible sign for future use, as if they were writing a map in the island’s own alphabet.

They captured three young black swans after spirited resistance from the old birds, and later brought them to Rockburg to ornament Safety Bay. Presently a beautiful heron thrust his long neck from among the reeds to see what disturbance had come to his domain.

Before he could satisfy his curiosity, Fritz unhooded his eagle. The proud bird flapped and struggled, but its legs and wings were bound gently and firmly, and it had to submit to inspection by delighted captors.

Alarm came again swiftly. A large powerful animal puffed through the dense reed-thicket close by, making a curious whistling sound. It vanished almost at once. From their description it must have been a tapir—dark brown, shaped like a young rhinoceros but without a horn, with an upper lip prolonged into a short trunk like an elephant’s in miniature. Gentle enough unless attacked, capable of fierce defence, dangerous to dogs with its teeth, and perfectly at ease swimming and diving in swampy rivers. Fritz followed in the cajack for a time but saw no more of it.

Meanwhile Jack and Franz returned by the rice-fields and encountered a flock of cranes. They managed to catch five or six alive, among them two demoiselle or Numidian cranes, using arrows fitted with loops of cord dipped in birdlime so that the bird aimed at was often entangled and brought down uninjured—an original contrivance, half hunting and half craft.

They lived comfortably on peccary ham, cassava bread, fruit, baked potatoes, and milk—provisions that, in their telling later, sounded less like mere fuel and more like a small travelling kitchen, each taste a reassurance that home could be carried in the mouth. As for the pemmican, one trial was enough. It went to the dogs. Fritz, however, did not abandon the idea; he determined to attempt it again, knowing its value when properly prepared.

After collecting rice and cotton, they went on to Prospect Hill. There, Fritz said afterwards, they found the pine wood in possession of troops of monkeys, who howled and chattered like demons and pelted them with pine cones until the air seemed full of hard, spinning missiles. At last the boys fired a few shots; several monkeys fell; the rest fled, and the party continued in peace—only to discover that Prospect Hill itself had been overrun and ruined, as Woodlands had been the previous summer. Dirt and disorder met them everywhere. They cleaned and set things right as best they could, yet preferred the tent for sleeping.

Fritz felt at a loss how to guard the place. Then he found a bottle of poisonous euphorbia gum in the tool chest and devised a plan that succeeded beyond his expectations. He mixed poison with milk, bruised millet, and anything he thought the monkeys would eat, and put the mixture in cocoanut shells hung high enough to be out of reach of our own animals.

That evening, Fritz said, was calm and lovely—the sea murmuring in the distance, the moon rising to lay a pale beauty over land and water. For a time, in that solemn stillness, their feelings were touched; the world seemed to hold its breath.

Then the spell broke.

From every side came an outburst of hideous, discordant noises, as though the forest itself had been wounded. Snorting, snarling, shrieking filled the woods beneath them. Jackals howled mournfully from the hills; Fangs answered in the yard, and Floss and Bruno backed him with barking and yelping. Far away beyond the rocky fastnesses of the Gap came hollow snortings and neighings that reminded one of the hippopotamus’s strange cry. Above these were occasional deep, majestic roarings that made their hearts quail with the conviction that lions and elephants spoke in the dark.

Overawed and silent, they tried to sleep, but soon the most fearful cries in the adjoining woods announced that the apes were suffering from the poisoned food. The dogs could not keep quiet amid such uproar, and the boys did not sleep until morning. When they rose late, they saw an awful spectacle: dead monkeys and baboons thickly strewn beneath the trees around the farm.

Fritz would not tell us how many. He only said he wished he had not found the poison. They cleared away the bodies and the dangerous food, burying some deep and carrying the rest to the shore to pitch over the rocks into the sea. Then they travelled on to the Gap.

That same evening a messenger pigeon arrived at Rockburg with a note whose concluding words struck my heart like a sudden cold wind:

“The barricade at the Gap is broken down. Everything laid waste as far as the sugar-brake, where the hut is knocked to pieces, and the fields trampled over by huge footmarks. Come to us, father—we are safe, but feel we are no match for this unknown danger.”

I lost not an instant. Late as it was, I saddled Swift and set off at once to their assistance, telling Ernest to prepare the small cart and follow with his mother at daybreak, bringing everything we might need for camping out several days.

Bright moonlight favoured my ride, and I reached the Gap sooner than they expected. My arrival surprised and delighted the boys. Early next morning I examined the footprints and ravages of the great unknown.

There was no doubt: an elephant had visited our cane-brake. Thick posts in the barricade were snapped like reeds. Trees near the spot where we had planned a cool summer-house were stripped of leaves and branches to a great height, as if some enormous hand had combed them bare. Worst of all, the young sugar-cane plants were either devoured or trampled down and destroyed.

It seemed not one elephant but a troop had invaded. Tracks were numerous; footprints of various sizes. Yet to my satisfaction I saw that the marks could be traced not only from the Gap but back to it in equal numbers. We concluded, therefore, that the mighty animals had withdrawn into their own wilds after this freebooting incursion, and did not remain hidden within our territory.

Still, the lesson was clear: our ramparts must be strengthened. That night, sitting beside an enormous watch-fire whose crackling and steady roar made a brave counter-music to the remembered uproar of the forest, I laid plans with my sons. They told me, in turn, the details of all they had seen and done—tales so vivid and various that, though we should have slept, each of us was more inclined to keep watch, to listen to the island’s breathing in the dark, and to guard what we had built with hands that, by now, had learned both labour and defence.

“We spend our years as a tale that is told.” The line came back to me with the steady insistence of surf against stone as I turned page after page of my journal and found, almost with surprise, that ten full years had slipped past us on this island—years not marched out like soldiers, but poured, day by day, like milk into a pail: warm, ordinary, nourishing, and gone before one thinks to measure them.

There were moments, in that looking back, when a shadow lay across my mind—less grief than the quiet awe of time’s passing—yet gratitude quickly drove it away. God had kept our household in health and peace; the boys, trained early to labour and self-command, had grown into men. If any young reader should ever open these pages, let him understand that there is an adventure more enduring than storms or shipwrecks: the patient, cheerful industry of a united family, where duty is learned at the table as much as in the field, and where faith is not shouted but practiced, like daily bread.

It would weary even the kindest listener were I to recount, year by year, every hunt, every exploring party, every contrivance hammered together and improved upon—enough, indeed, to fill an encyclopaedia. Yet before I reach the events that ended this long interval, I must set down what our “New Switzerland” had become; for the place itself, shaped by our hands, shaped us in return.

Rockburg and Falconhurst remained our winter and summer homes. Rockburg, once so bare and scorching that my wife could scarcely endure it, had been softened into a dwelling that seemed to breathe. We coaxed fountains from the rock; we trained verandahs under trellises until they were hung with flowering creepers that dripped scent in the heat—honeysuckle sweetness, lemon blossom sharpness, and the resinous breath of young European saplings brought to strange soil and persuaded, by care, to remember home. Shrubs and trees, both native and from our old world, stood in planted groves; the air moved through them like a slow hymn, and with it moved sound—the clean chatter of leaves, the steady trickle of water, the gentler music that heals the mind more surely than any medicine. I often thought, hearing that living choir, how cruel to the spirit must be the ceaseless clang of cities, where iron screams over stone and even art is sold in noise. Here, our peace was measured in the island’s own language: wind, wing, and running water.

Out beyond, Shark Island—once a harsh sentinel of rock—was now feathered with palms. It guarded the entrance to Safety Bay; the battery and flagstaff were plain on its crest, as if the island itself wore our signal as a crown. The drained swamp had become a lake, leaving only enough reeds and marsh to shelter the waterfowl who had accepted us as masters without fear. On the blue surface moved black swans with solemn grace, snow-white geese that scolded and conversed like village wives, and richly coloured ducks that flashed as they turned. Among the rushes, at intervals—like bright words in a half-forgotten dialect—appeared the brilliant Sultan, marsh-fowl, crimson flamingos, soft blue-grey demoiselle cranes, and crested herons. Their calls wove together into a soundscape that never repeated itself yet never jarred: a living order that no conductor could commercialize.

Jack’s giant frogs, Grace and Beauty, had grown until they were, in truth, almost the size of small rabbits. They knew their names—names given with the authority boys take in such things—and at his call they waddled from the marsh, solemn as old servants, to swallow grasshoppers or the daintiest fly. Under the spreading trees, old Hurry the ostrich paced with grave dignity, as if he carried in his long legs the memory of kingdoms. Pigeons of every beautiful kind nested in the rocks and dove-cots, and their soft cooing was often the undertone to our evenings, a domestic music as necessary as firelight.

Rockburg could now be approached only by the bridge. Above the river, near the cascade where once it might be forded, we had planted a living barrier: orange and lemon trees, Indian figs, prickly pears, and thorny shrubs interlocked so densely that it was a wall of leaf and spine—fragrant, formidable, and, when in bloom, perfuming the air so richly that even the bees seemed drowsy with it.

Shark Island’s rabbit-warren supplied us with food and fur. The antelopes, failing on Whale Isle, were moved there among the shaded groves, and Whale Isle itself we gave over to necessary work that offended the senses—candle-making, tanning, wool-cleaning—so that the rest of our dominion might keep its sweetness. At Woodlands the farm flourished; our flocks and herds gave mutton, beef, and veal, and my wife’s dairy, with its crocks and pans and clean, sour-sweet smell, was so abundant that it often seemed to command her more than she commanded it.

The boys had never outgrown their delight in naming animals, as if language itself were another form of husbandry. A creamy-white cow was Blanche; a bull with a voice like thunder was Stentor. Two young onagers, fleet as arrows, were named Arrow and Dart. Jack had a descendant of his old favourite Fangs the jackal, and insisted on calling him Coco, maintaining—loudly, and with relentless demonstrations—that no word could be heard at a distance unless it contained an “o.” He would shout his examples until our ears rang, and then laugh as if he had discovered a new law of nature. I sometimes wondered, watching the boys coin names so freely, how many words, in the wider world, were dying unlamented—how dialects, like neglected gardens, go to weeds until the old meanings cannot be recovered. Here, we preserved memory in syllables, even when the syllables were foolish.

We enjoyed excellent health. My wife suffered now and then slight fever; the boys had their small accidents, inevitable in such a life. Fritz, at twenty-four, was moderately tall, uncommonly strong and active, high-spirited and ready. Ernest, two years younger, was tall and slight, mild, calm, studious; the indolence and selfishness of his earlier youth had been largely overcome by necessity and affection. Jack, twenty, resembled Fritz but more lightly built—swift, agile, made for climbing and leaping rather than sheer strength. Franz, seventeen, had something of each brother: wit and shrewdness, though not Jack’s arch drollery. All were honourable, God-fearing, dutiful to their mother and me, warmly attached to each other.

Though so many years had passed in seclusion, my conviction remained that we should not always be alone. Yet time that carried our sons toward manhood carried their parents toward age; and sometimes, when the island’s evening sounds softened into silence, anxious thoughts pressed on me—what if we were never restored? what if the boys, one day, stood alone here? I did not speak of these shadows. I took them to God in prayer, and always found strength renewed, like a fire coaxed back to life by careful breath.

My elder sons would often make expeditions and return only after many hours. Any reproof I had prepared would vanish at the sight of their joyous faces.

One day Fritz was absent from Rockburg the whole day. Only toward evening did we notice his cajack was gone and that he must have ventured out to sea. Uneasiness drove us to Shark Island. Ernest, Jack, and I climbed to the watch-tower, hoisted the signal flag, and loaded the gun.

We gazed long across the ocean, glittering under the level beams of the setting sun, until at last we discerned a black speck. Through the telescope it resolved into the returning cajack. Even at that distance I saw it moved more slowly than usual. We fired the cannon so he might know we watched, then hurried back to the harbour to meet him.

As he came in, the reason for his delay was plain: he towed a large sack, and the skiff itself was heavily laden.

“Welcome, Fritz!” I called. “Welcome back—wherever you come from, and whatever you bring. You have quite a cargo.”

“Yes—and my trip has led to discoveries as well as booty,” he answered, pushing in. “Discoveries that will tempt us again in the same direction. Come, boys—help carry these things up. When I have rested, I will tell you all.”

We gathered around him as soon as the goods were secured. He began with the frankness that always belonged to him.

“My absence without leave deserves reproach, father, more than this reception. I must apologize. But ever since I possessed the cajack, it has been my ambition to make a voyage along the coast beyond the point where I killed the walrus. I prepared beforehand—provisions, compass fixed in front of my seat, rifle, harpoon, axe, boat-hook, fishing-net—so that when a good morning came, I could go at once. And I resolved to take Pounce, my eagle. I mean to do so always.”

He described the dawn—calm sea, gentle breeze—and how the current bore him out. He rounded the point and passed above the spot where our ship’s wreck lay under water. Through glass-clear depths he had seen the scattered ironwork, cannon balls, and other indestructible remnants lying on the flat rocky bottom like bones of a great animal picked clean.

Then he spoke of the coast: rugged cliffs, rocks thrusting from shore or rising in jagged masses from the sea. Sea-fowl crowded the inaccessible ledges. On lower ridges seals, sea-bears, and walruses basked, plunged, or hauled their heavy bodies up by tusks and awkward strength. He admitted he had not felt comfortable among such monsters, and had worked hard to pass quickly and unnoticed.

After more than an hour and a half he reached a high, precipitous cape that ran far out to sea. In the rock face he saw a magnificent archway—at first glance like the entrance to an immense vaulted cavern. He passed beneath this noble portal and examined the interior.

“It was tenanted by numbers of a small swallow, scarcely larger than a wren,” he said. “The walls were covered with thousands of their nests. They were rudely built, each resting on a kind of platform like a spoon without a handle. I detached a number. They looked fibrous and gelatinous—more like sponges or fungi than nests. I brought them home in my fishing-net.”

“If we had trade with the Chinese,” I told him, “your discovery would be valuable. These are edible birds’ nests, made by the esculent swallow. New, nearly white nests are valued even at their weight in silver. In Java and elsewhere they are gathered at great risk. When soaked they soften and swell, and are made into a strengthening soup. I think you might try them, wife—only for curiosity.”

My wife regarded the queer trophies with a doubtful expression. “I do not fancy the look of them,” she said, “but I can try, if they truly turn to jelly. Boiling birds’ nests is cookery quite out of my line.”

“Oh do, mother!” Jack cried. “Let us taste birds’ nests. Though the thought makes me imagine my mouth full of feathers.”

Fritz, curious as ever, asked from where the swallows obtained such substance.

“It is not exactly known,” I said, “whether they find it or produce it. But it is clear much of it is furnished by certain glands which pour out a viscid secretion.”

Fritz continued. The cavern, he said, was not an end but a corridor; it turned and opened into a lonely bay so calm and lake-like that he judged it nearly land-locked. Its shores, beyond the rocky boundary, spread into a fertile plain toward what seemed the mouth of a river. Beyond that lay rough, likely marshy ground, and a dense cedar forest that shut off the view.

The water beneath his skiff had been clear as crystal, and in the shallows he saw beds of shellfish—like large oysters—attached to rocks and to one another by tufts of hairy filaments.

“If these are oysters,” he thought, “they must be worth eating.” He hooked up clusters with the boat-hook, landed, flung them on the sand, and went back for more, intending to tow them in the net. But the hot sun made the shells gape. He began to “examine” them with his knife—and found resistance in places. Closer examination produced pearly balls, like peas, various sizes. He had brought a number home in a box.

The boys clamoured to see them. In their palms the pearls caught the light with a soft gleam, not the harsh glitter of glass but something deeper, as if the sea’s own muted colours had condensed into spheres.

“You have discovered treasure indeed,” I said. “Beautiful pearls. Valueless here, perhaps, but wealth if ever we return to the civilized world. We must visit your oyster beds as soon as we can.”

He told how he named the place Pearl Bay. The bay, he explained, was sheltered by reefs and sandbanks with only a single channel to the open sea. The tide had set strongly inshore, so he could not pass out then. He examined the headland crags, hoping for a second archway, but found nothing remarkable—only innumerable sea-fowl, from gull and sea-swallow to mighty albatross.

“They took my approach as trespass,” he said, with a half-laugh. “They beset me, screaming and wheeling, until I lost patience and struck about me with the boat-hook. One blow stunned an albatross; it fell into the water.”

He then attempted the narrow channel, succeeded, and returned home, seeing our flag and hearing our salute.

His narrative ended there, and for the household it was enough: edible nests, pearl oysters, a new bay—new work, new supplies, new names to give. The boys were already dreaming of huts and fisheries; my wife was considering, with a cook’s wary courage, what could be made of swallows’ nests and how they might taste when coaxed into broth.

But the next morning Fritz drew me aside and, in a voice lowered not by fear but by the gravity of wonder, told me what he had not spoken before.

“There was something extraordinary about that albatross, father. I allowed you to think I left it. I did not. I lifted it onto the cajack and saw a rag wound round one leg. I removed it and—God knows how—English words were written on it. I read them plainly: ‘Save an unfortunate Englishwoman from the smoking rock!’”

For a moment I could not answer him. The words, so simple and so impossible in our isolation, struck me like a sudden cry heard in a forest that one believed empty. My mind spun between hope and caution.

“Is this reality?” Fritz went on. “Or delusion? Can it be true—a fellow creature breathes in this lonely region?”

He told me how he had sat stupefied, then, as the bird stirred, compelled himself to act. He tore a strip from his handkerchief and wrote, “Do not despair! Help is near!” He bound his reply carefully round one leg, replaced the original rag on the other, gave the albatross water, and worked until it revived. Then it rose—unsteady at first—then steadied and flew swiftly away westward.

“Now one thought occupies me,” Fritz said. “Will my note reach her? Shall I find her—shall I save her?”

I listened with astonishment and deep interest. Yet I forced myself to speak soberly.

“My dear son, you have done wisely to confide this to me alone. Until we know more, we must not unsettle the others. It is possible those words were written long ago, perhaps on some distant shore, and the unhappy stranger may by this time have perished. By ‘the smoking rock’ one would think of a volcano. There are none here.”

Fritz could not accept such gloom. He argued the rag did not seem very old; that smoke could rise from rock not volcanic—perhaps from a fire, perhaps from some strange natural cause. Above all, he clung to the hope that an appeal so direct had been meant to be answered.

In truth I was as eager as he; but I judged it prudent to lessen rather than kindle hopes that might end in bitterness.

We consulted earnestly and resolved that Fritz should go in search of the writer of the message—but not until his canoe was altered to carry two persons and provisions enough for a long absence. Impatient as he was, he saw the wisdom of delay.

When we returned to the house, we found the boys already at the oysters Fritz had brought. They prised shells open with the eagerness of children cracking nuts at a winter hearth. Each time a pearl rolled out, there was a shout, a chorus of astonished exclamations; the small hard globes gleamed among wet flesh and brine.

“May we not establish a pearl-fishery at once, father?” they cried. “We could build a hut by the bay and work there regularly!”

An excursion to Pearl Bay became the subject of every conversation and every plan. Preparations were made on a grand scale, and the outing would serve, as Fritz intended, as the foundation for his more secret voyage—secret only because he would not yet burden the household with the uncertain hope of a stranger’s rescue.

One day, when all were present, I spoke with deliberation.

“I have been considering, dear wife, that our eldest son is now of an age to be dependent on himself. Henceforth I shall leave him at liberty to act according to his own judgement. Especially in voyages or excursions, he must not be hampered by fear of alarming us should he remain absent longer than we expect. I have entire confidence in his prudence—and in his affection. He will never needlessly cause us anxiety.”

Fritz looked at me with gratitude. His mother embraced him, her voice trembling as she said, “God bless and preserve thee, my boy!”

It took time to make several scraping machines—rakes of my own invention—for detaching and lifting oysters from their rocks. Meanwhile Fritz altered the fittings of his canoe so it had a spare seat. His brothers, of course, assumed he meant to take one of them as shipmate; he allowed their mistake to stand.

They busied themselves making what they thought useful—ropes, bags, small tools—bearing the delay with tolerable patience. At last the day came.

We took leave of my wife and Franz and went aboard our yacht with some of the dogs. Jack, proudly installed in the new seat beside Fritz in the canoe, shared with him the honour of leading the way as pilot.

We passed safely through the rocks and shoals near Walrus Island into calm water sheltered by jutting cliffs. The sea lay like polished metal, and for the first time we saw paper-nautiluses—the fairy shells—sailing lightly over the dazzling surface, each like a living boat with a fragile white sail.

We could not look upon them without longing to keep them. The canoe gave chase and soon secured half a dozen. They were handed aboard to be carefully preserved for our little museum, and we named the place Nautilus Creek. Even as I spoke the name aloud, I felt that naming is a kind of claiming—yet also a kind of remembering—and I wondered what words, in what tongues, had once named these waters before we came, and whether those tongues had vanished as completely as a tide-mark washed from stone.

Further on we rounded a short, flat promontory ending in an abrupt rock, which we called Cape Pug-Nose; then, at some distance, appeared the grand cliffs of a headland running far out to sea. I supposed we should have to weather it, but our pilots held their course, and soon we saw the majestic archway that offered a short passage to Pearl Bay.

The architectural splendour of pillars, arches, and pinnacles around that entrance struck me with admiration. It resembled a gothic cathedral carved by waves rather than masons, and I proposed the name Cape Minster.

As we approached, a cloud of little swallows darted out, divided into flocks, soared and wheeled, then returned as swiftly as they had come, vanishing into the long dark tunnel whose sides were festooned with nests. The air there was cool and damp, tasting of salt and stone; every sound was altered—oar-dips became hollow taps, voices fell into reverent echoes, wings rustled like pages turning in a vast, dim book.

We detached nests as we passed, careful to leave those with eggs or young. The best were high, but broken shelving rocks gave footing for climbers like Fritz and Jack. They moved with the fearless grace of youth, returning with as many as we could reasonably carry—odd pale trophies, light as dried seafoam.

The tide assisted us, bearing us onward along the nave of this natural cathedral. Aisles and side-chapels seemed to open between columns and arches. In that dim religious light the world looked older than any human story; yet our oars, our breathing, our ordinary labour belonged there too, as if domestic life could inhabit even the grandest mystery without profaning it.

When we emerged into dazzling sunshine, we floated upon the calm expanse of Pearl Bay. For some minutes we could scarcely look around, the light was so sudden and the scene so bright.

Fritz had not overstated its beauty. Romantic islets studded the waters, giving a smile to features already perfect. Meadows and groves lay along the shore; hills rose gently; brooks murmured as if the land itself spoke in a soft local tongue. Birds called from reed and tree; the bay answered with its own hush and lapping, and I felt again that our well-being depended not only on food and shelter but on this acoustic mercy—the island’s refusal to rage in needless noise.

We cruised, surveying for a landing place near the shallows where the oyster beds lay. At last we found a convenient spot close to a sparkling streamlet. As the day declined, we made quick arrangements for a watch-fire. Then we ate a hasty supper—plain fare, yet rendered precious by salt air and labour—and left the dogs, with Coco the jackal, to sleep on shore as sentinels.

We returned to the yacht for the night and anchored within gunshot of land, the bay quiet around us. Above, the swallows’ distant murmuring and the soft pulse of water against the hull braided together into a lullaby, and in that sound I felt both the comfort of our settled routine and the faint, unsettling pull of Fritz’s secret: a human voice, perhaps, calling from some “smoking rock” beyond our known shore.

My wife and Franz, startled at first by Fritz’s empty place, were still glad enough to see our little party come safely home. They listened close, as if every word were a footstep brought back to the hearth. When I spoke of the lion and his mate, my wife’s breath caught in her throat; she turned pale, and her fingers tightened on the bowl she had been holding as though it might steady her. And Jack—though he would have laughed at any one else’s fear—looked so wan and shaken that she searched his face again and again, unable to believe he carried no wound.

When the tale came to Juno, Franz’s eyes brimmed over. He asked, softly and with the dignity of one who has a historian’s tenderness for the faithful dead, whether we had brought her back to be laid near the home she had guarded so long. The next day he watched her burial as carefully as if the very soil must be set in order; and Ernest, at his request, composed a little epitaph, which we cut upon a slab and set above her:

JUNO

A servant true lies here:
A faithful friend,
A Dog,
To all most dear;
Who met her end
Fighting right bravely in her master's cause.

Then came the more ordinary consolations—the kind that can be tasted. The wild boar’s flesh, still sweet with the clean, foresty smell of the hunt, and the truffles, dark as damp earth and pungent as thunder-rain, were given over to my wife. She received them with the bright, practical joy of a woman who could turn peril into supper. Already she was speaking of stews thickened slowly, of slices smoked and dried for journeys, of sauces coaxed into richness with patience and heat. She would gladly have had the boar’s head as well; but my promise stood. Ernest’s museum was to claim it, not the oven—though my own mouth watered at the thought of it baked whole, in the fashion we had once learned, sealed with clay until the scent of fat and spice would have crept under every door like a warm hand.

The boar’s head and the lions’ skins we carried to the tannery on Whale Island, where salt, scraping, and long, rhythmic labor would turn raw spoil into something that could last—an island craftwork accompanied always by its own music: the rasp of tools, the slap of wet hide, the hush and suck of water along the shore.

Five days went by, each day with its familiar sounds—the morning birds, the insects’ thin fiddling in the heat, the evening fire’s crackle—yet none of it soothed the wrongness left by Fritz’s absence. The island, when one listened for it, seemed to hold its breath where his step should have been. At last I could no longer hide my uneasiness. I resolved to follow him.

The decision lifted everyone’s spirits as if a cloud had moved off the sun. Even my wife, once she knew we would sail in the pinnace and not attempt any reckless crossing in a smaller craft, agreed to come with us. It was settled, provisions stowed, water secured, guns examined, and the dogs called aboard, their nails clicking sharply on the planks.

On a bright morning, with a fair breeze, the five of us pushed off for Cape Minster.

The little yacht bounded over the water as if delighted with its own swiftness. The sea-breeze, salt and cool, made our cheeks glow; the sun painted the wave-crests into quick, silver sparks. I set our course for the archway’s entrance ahead, that familiar landmark like a gate into another chamber of the island’s world.

Then, straight before us, I saw a dark, greenish shadow just under the surface—a mass that looked like a sunken rock. It startled me, not for its presence alone but for the way it seemed not to belong to any map in my mind. I threw down the helm at once, but we were already upon it.

A slight shock ran through the hull—wood and water speaking together in a sudden, blunt syllable—and then, as quickly, the resistance vanished. We had surged over it. The danger, for that instant, was past.

I looked astern to mark the spot—and the “rock” was gone. Where the great shadow had been, the water lay smooth and innocent, as though it had never held anything at all.

Before our amazement had time to settle, Jack shouted, pointing hard to starboard.

“There is another!”

Sure enough, another greenish bulk seemed to lie just beneath the surface.

“The rock is moving!” cried Franz, and his words had barely left him when the sea itself heaved and a great black body rose, sleek and terrible, from the depths. From its upper extremity burst a column of water with a roar like a cannon’s breath, flung high into the air, then falling in cold spray around us—rain made violent, and loud enough to drown even our thoughts.

The mystery was plain now: a cachalot—an immense sperm whale, its length and massive head leaving no doubt.

The monster, angered by the scrape of our keel, slid back as if to measure us, then turned with dreadful intent. I remembered grim seamen’s tales—boats smashed like toys, ships stove in by a furious rush—and for a moment the bright day narrowed to the sound of our own breathing and the slap of water against the hull.

In desperation I sprang to one of the guns; Jack, quick as a spark, leaped to the other. We fired almost together. The shots struck home; the whale lashed the sea into foam, the sound of its tail like a great wet whip cracking again and again, and then it plunged down and vanished.

We did not relax. I would not lose such a prize, nor risk a second assault unready. We reloaded and stood toward shore, where the whale seemed to be making. Soon we sighted it again in shallower water, thrashing fearfully, the sea darkening around it with blood.

We approached as close as I dared, and fired again.

For a few moments its struggles grew even more frantic—then the vast body shuddered from head to tail, and the sea, which had been roaring with violence, fell into an uncanny quiet around it. The cachalot lay motionless: dead.

The boys’ shout of triumph rose—then died on their lips. Behind a rock a canoe shot into view, paddled by a tall, muscular savage. He stood up in his narrow skiff to study us, watchful as a heron. When he saw we were turning toward him, he snatched up his paddle and darted behind the rock again.

An awful thought struck me with a force colder than the whale’s spray: there might be a tribe of blacks hidden on these shores—and Fritz might have fallen into their hands.

We were not to be taken easily. Our guns were loaded and run out. The bay’s sounds sharpened—water ticking against stone, the wind’s steady hiss in the rigging, and, somewhere far in the forest, a deep, indistinct calling that might have been bird or beast or human voice. In such a place one learned that sound could mislead; and yet it was also the first warning, the first comfort, the first sign that the world still lived beyond one’s own fear.

A dusky face appeared, peering from a lofty rock, then vanished. Another peeped from lower down. Then the skiff put out again as if to reconnoitre. Even Jack looked uneasy and waited for my word.

“Hoist a white flag,” said I, “and give me the speaking-trumpet.”

I raised the trumpet and called out as peaceably as I could in Malay, searching my memory for words learned long ago. But neither the white cloth nor my careful phrases seemed to calm him; he began to turn back.

Jack, impatient as ever, snatched the trumpet.

“Come here, you black son of a gun,” he shouted. “Come on board and make friends, or we’ll blow you and your—”
